StitchCraft

Image to embroidery

Automatic digitizing makes a design that sews well from a picture of flat colours, such as a logo, clip art or a drawing, once it is traced into shapes. Settings on the shapes are optional. StitchCraft chooses the stitches of each shape by its width at the size it sews. A colour drawn under others sews what shows of it, and neighbouring shapes overlap where they meet. Specks and the background are left out, and the sewing is ordered for few colour changes and short jumps. Milestone M13 of the roadmap builds it, and its steps M13.8 and M13.20 are still to come. Photographs sewn in blended colours need another technique, outside the roadmap.

The flow

A picture becomes a machine file in 2 steps:

  1. In VectorCraft, place the picture, scale it to the size it will sew, and trace it with Object › Image Trace › Make.
  2. Run stitch export picture.vectorcraft --auto -o picture.pes --preview picture.png.

With VectorCraft installed, stitch export picture.png -o picture.pes takes both steps itself (below).

An Image Trace object is a group of the traced paths, with the source image hidden beside them (imageTrace.make in VectorCraft’s crates/engine, main at 0842941). stitch export already reads those paths, and Expand is optional. Without --auto it sews each of them as Ink/Stitch sews an SVG shape: every area as a tatami fill and every line as a running stitch, in paint order. In a test document of a traced square and a strip 2.1 mm wide beside it, both sewed as tatami fills. With --auto, each shape sews as this page describes. M13.8 makes automatic digitizing the default for VectorCraft documents, and --auto is then needed for SVG files only (below).

VectorCraft’s exporter plug-ins (P1, 0.14) move the second step into VectorCraft, as File › Export › Export As › PES, which StitchCraft’s formats are exporters for since M6.9. Their auto option is --auto. Since M13.7, StitchCraft’s Auto effect on the traced group says it in the document itself, with the rules’ settings in its dialog, and previews each path’s stitches: the document then sews digitized automatically from either step, without the switch.

An SVG file traced elsewhere, such as by Inkscape’s Trace Bitmap, sews the same way with stitch plan picture.svg --auto. Without the switch, an SVG file’s shapes sew as Ink/Stitch sews them, since the file may be one of Ink/Stitch’s.

What VectorCraft does and what StitchCraft does

The 2 programs split the work. VectorCraft’s Image Trace (its crates/trace) already does what a picture needs before it is embroidered:

  • Its colour modes reduce the picture to a few flat colours. Its Document Library palette traces with a swatch library’s colours exactly.
  • Its Noise setting leaves out areas smaller than a number of pixels, and Ignore White leaves out white areas.
  • Its Abutting method traces each colour’s areas as paths that share their edges with their neighbours.
  • Create Strokes traces lines up to its Stroke width as stroked centre lines.
  • The paths are smooth curves over the steps of the pixels.

StitchCraft links none of VectorCraft’s crates (integration), and a tracer of its own would repeat VectorCraft’s. StitchCraft does the embroidery. It chooses the stitches of each shape, the overlaps between shapes and the order they sew in.

stitch palette (M13.7) writes a machine profile’s thread palette as a GIMP palette file (.gpl). VectorCraft loads that file as a swatch library (Window › Swatch Libraries › Other Library…), and Image Trace’s Document Library palette then traces the picture in the threads’ colours.

Automatic elements

An automatic element is a shape whose stitches StitchCraft chooses. The readers make one of:

  • each object of a VectorCraft document that has no StitchCraft effect, with --auto;
  • each path with the StitchCraft Auto effect, on the path itself or on a group or a layer around it, with or without --auto (M13.7, REQ-VC-016). A path nearer to a StitchCraft stitch effect, its own or a group’s, sews as that says. The effect’s parameters are the shape’s settings of the rules (below). A stitch effect says how an object sews, so an Auto effect beside one isn’t used, and SC-W0807 says so;
  • each SVG element without inkstitch: settings, with --auto.

An element with settings is not digitized automatically, whether or not the switch is given: it sews as its settings say, as far as its reader reads them. So a picture can be digitized automatically and one of its shapes then set by hand. A shape’s fill gives its area and its stroke gives its line, each with its thread, width, join and cap, as for any element. A trim or a stop that an Ink/Stitch command asks for is not a setting of the stitches, and an automatic element keeps it. An element sewn as several elements keeps it for the last of them. Start and end positions, from Ink/Stitch’s commands or VectorCraft’s markers, and a target point, are not kept: automatic digitizing places where its parts start and end, and the readers say the commands and markers aren’t used (REQ-GEN-001). The elements it makes have none. An automatic element’s clip paths are applied before it is digitized, where an ordinary element’s are applied as it is generated: the shape digitized is what its clips leave of it, and its columns follow what shows (REQ-GEN-005, auto/clips.rs). A line’s cut end takes the line’s cap. A shape wholly outside its clips is left out (SC-W0013).

The readers mark the element (Element::automatic), and they read the switch from Reading, which every host takes. Before anything is generated, the engine turns each automatic element into ordinary elements whose settings say what to sew (stitchcraft_engine::auto). The generators, the assembly and every host then treat them as shapes set by hand. SC-I0901 counts the shapes digitized, by the stitches they sew as. An automatic line sewn across its width follows the drawing, and the reader names what the drawing draws across the line that its stitches don’t follow (REQ-AUTO-005). Of a VectorCraft document, that is the line’s alignment inside or outside a closed path and its width profile (SC-W0807). Of an SVG file, it is transforms that stretch the line more one way than the other (SC-W0802). Under such transforms a viewer draws it wider one way, and its stitches are as wide as the transforms stretch it on average. Its caps are followed (below), and so are its corners, joined as the drawing joins them. An SVG join that is not set is SVG’s miter, limited by stroke-miterlimit, where Ink/Stitch reads a miter limited at 5.

Digitizing doesn’t add an error of its own (REQ-AUTO-006). A part that its width’s stitches cannot sew is sewn otherwise, or left out with a warning, and a shape that paints nothing sews as it would without the switch. One odd shape among hundreds never keeps a design from being written.

Automatic digitizing by default

M13.8 makes a VectorCraft document digitized automatically without a switch. The step comes before MC-8’s sew-out, as the owner chose on 2026-11-21, and the sew-out then confirms or corrects the default rather than waiting for it. A VectorCraft document is drawn to be embroidered. An SVG file may be one of Ink/Stitch’s, so it keeps its reading, and --auto asks for automatic digitizing there.

One reading. Without a switch, stitch plan reads a document as --auto reads it, and writes the same machine file byte for byte, with the same diagnostics (REQ-AUTO-163). So the cap at the end of planning and the warning of how thick a design sews hold under the default as they do with --auto. One constant of the VectorCraft adapter, stitchcraft_vectorcraft::AUTO_BY_DEFAULT, is the default. Reading::default() stays plain, and each host passes the reading it wants. The command line resolves its switches in one place, ReadingArgs::reading, which stitch plan, stitch bug-report and stitch expand call with the kind of design. The exporters’ option takes the constant as its default, in the manifest and for a host that passes no auto. Level B of the compatibility gate reads a document as ReadingArgs reads it without a switch, and the docs pictures keep the reading each shot names.

What doesn’t change (REQ-AUTO-164). The default digitizes the shapes --auto digitizes: an object with no StitchCraft effect of its own and none on a group or a layer around it. The reader already leaves an object a Fill, Satin or Stroke effect reaches to that effect (stitchcraft_vectorcraft::document), so a shape set by hand sews as it was set. Every part of an expanded design has its effect and digitized_as, so an expanded design is never digitized again. The Auto effect makes a shape automatic with or without the default. Markers grouped with a shape the default digitizes are said unused (SC-W0807), as with --auto. A text object without effects becomes lettering, as with --auto.

The notice (REQ-AUTO-165). One line, SC-I0924, says how many shapes without StitchCraft effects the default digitized and how many are set by hand, with effects or with markers, and that --no-auto sews them as drawn. It isn’t said when --auto is given, or when no shape is digitized by default. The VectorCraft reader counts the elements that are automatic only because the reading asked (VectorCraft::by_default): an element an Auto effect reaches is automatic by the effect, and isn’t counted. stitch plan says the notice first. A picture is always digitized automatically, so its run has no notice. A bug-report bundle has none either. The exporters’ option shows in their dialog, and the exporters don’t repeat it. Of the traced badge with a Fill effect on one of its 4 shapes, the case of REQ-AUTO-165, stitch plan says:

info SC-I0924: 3 shapes without StitchCraft effects are digitized automatically, as a VectorCraft document is by default, and 1 shape is set by hand: plan it with --no-auto to sew them as drawn.

Markers (REQ-AUTO-166). Under the default, a stitch:start or stitch:end marker grouped with a shape without StitchCraft effects sets that shape by hand, as the owner chose on 2026-11-21. The shape isn’t digitized automatically. It sews as drawn from where its markers say, as with --no-auto. No SC-W0807 is said of its markers. With --auto the person asks for automatic digitizing of every shape without settings. Then the shape is automatic and its marker is unused (SC-W0807). The Auto effect makes it automatic too. The field Reading::marked_as_drawn holds the rule. The command line sets it with the default, the exporters with their option on, and a bug-report bundle records it. One function decides it, by_paints in stitchcraft_vectorcraft::document.

The opt-out. --no-auto sews the shapes without effects as drawn: every area a tatami fill and every line a running stitch, in paint order (REQ-CLI-026). It is refused beside --auto, with a picture, which is traced to be digitized automatically, and with stitch expand. The exporters’ Digitize automatically option is on by default, and off it is --no-auto (REQ-VC-036). A bug-report bundle records auto when its run digitized automatically, so a bundle from before the default, which has no auto, replays plain (REQ-CLI-027). A document has no setting of its own for it: a shape a person wants sewn as drawn takes a StitchCraft Fill or Stroke effect.

What changes, measured on main at ae75d386, and the same on 211b2784 and on M13.8’s own code, with the reference machine, --no-auto against the default. The reader fixtures, larger than any hoop, were measured on a hoop of 1 m.

Every traced document’s thickest spot falls from over the warning’s 4 fills, 4.15 to 5.68, to under it, 2.89 to 3.98. The badge and the pear sew a third of the stitches, since the default leaves their background out (SC-W0906) where the plain reading fills it. The dogs sew a few hundred fewer stitches in 3 or 4 more thread changes, and the running dog in 9 threads has more area at 3 fills, 94 mm² against 79, as the pear has, 10.5 against 4.8. On the drawn shapes of drawing-format1 the default sews thicker. A stroke round a fill becomes a satin column on the fill’s edge, and the cap thins no column’s top stitches (REQ-AUTO-112), so 10 mm² stay above the cap and under the warning. M13.8 doesn’t change a rule for it. The cost is known, and MC-8’s sew-out weighs it (machine testing). Planning takes about 10 times as long, 2.3 s against 0.2 s for the dogs in a debug build at optimization level 1. ribbons sews too many stitches for a PES file either way (SC-E0602), as a reader fixture may.

Which stitch a shape gets

Automatic digitizing chooses the stitches of each part of a shape from the part’s geometry alone, by the first rule of one table that the part matches (M13.21, REQ-AUTO-030). The picture’s subject is no input of a rule, and a flower and a dog go by the same table. A part is a connected piece of what shows of an area, once the layers are flattened and the specks are merged (Neighbours).

Width alone chose the stitch before. A blob as wide as a satin column then sewed as a column bent into its corners, and a ragged patch of a traced photo as several columns that fan where they meet. On the traced running dog, 62 of 107 shapes sewed so.

The measures

  • Widest. The diameter of the largest circle that fits inside the part. The rules use one fact about it, whether a circle as wide as a bound fits.
  • Width. The diameter of the circles that fit inside the part centred on its centre lines, averaged along them (width along the line).
  • Slenderness. The part’s area divided by its width squared, which says how long it is for its width. A rectangle 3 by 9 mm measures 3, a disc 0.8, and a letter drawn with an even stroke 6 or more.
  • Unevenness. How much the width varies along the lines: its standard deviation divided by the width. The letters of a drawn patch measure 0.11 to 0.23, and the ragged patches of 4 traced photos 0.39 to 0.52 at the median.
  • Lines. How many centre lines the part has. A part of one line has no branch.
  • Reach. How far the part lies from one of its centre lines on both sides of the line, in radii of the widest circle that fits inside it centred on its lines. A column has 2 rails, the part’s outline on either side of its line. Each rail is measured by its farthest corner beside the line, and a column by the nearer of its rails. A part measures what its farthest column does. However its width tapers, a column measures 1, and a star measures 1.6 to 2.6.

The rules

The rules’ table is generated from the code’s own (stitchcraft_engine::auto::rules::RULES), with each bound’s default, so that the table, the notice a shape gives and the tests at each bound can’t drift apart. The rules are tried in this order. First a shape whose setting names a stitch sews so. Then a speck merges into a neighbour and a line runs. A ragged thin line has merged into its neighbour before the rules are tried (Neighbours). A wide part sews as a split satin column once a person raises the widest split satin column (Wide columns). Areas are filled next, with compact parts and uneven parts after them, and then a part with arms. Any other part sews as satin columns, unless its columns are refused. One function compares every bound (auto::rules::choose), a length to the micrometre and a ratio to its third decimal. A width written in another unit that converts to a bound give or take a rounding error, such as 7 mm in CSS pixels, is that bound.

A compact part is filled (REQ-AUTO-031) because a blob has no way to run. Its centre line is what its corners leave of its middle, so a column along that line sews stitches of unlike lengths round a point. A fill sews rows across the blob at the design’s angle. On a part under 7 mm wide a row is a stitch or 2. The fill then looks like a satin column without the fan. A dot is the smallest compact part.

An uneven part is filled (REQ-AUTO-032) for what its columns would do: a part of several lines sews a column along each, and the columns meet at its junctions. Where the strokes are even, as a letter’s are, the columns meet cleanly. Where the width swells and shrinks, each column fans at its ends and the outline sews scalloped. A part of one line is held to its evenness once it is 4 mm wide somewhere, and is then filled whole, with no piece leaving the fill: a leaf sews as one fill with its outline. A narrower one, a ray or a tapering stroke, narrows smoothly, and one satin column sews it well (shapes whose width swells and tapers). A piece of an uneven part that is narrower than the narrowest satin stroke leaves the fill, cut as a narrow part of a broad one is (Shapes in parts), and sews as a line. The measure is of the whole part, so a letter of a thick and a thin stroke is uneven too, though each stroke is even. A design drawn in such strokes turns the rule off, which the logo style does.

A part with arms is filled (REQ-AUTO-050, M13.27). A star of 4 points 5 mm across has 2 axes, and its centre lines keep one of them. The points of the other axis are short for the star’s middle, so they are left out of the lines as bumps, as the corner of a sharp turn is (centre lines). Such a star counted one line, 0.97 mm wide and 7.5 times as slender, which is neither compact nor uneven. It sewed as one satin column from point to point, with its 2 other points as bumps on the column’s sides. This measure tells such a part from a column. The points left out lie on both rails, far from the line. A column’s rails lie no farther from its line than the radius of its widest circle. A part that measures more than 1.4 is filled. The corner of a sharp turn lies on one rail only. A letter V measures under 1, and keeps its columns. The rule comes after the uneven one, so a part that was filled before is filled by the same rule as before, and only columns moved.

2 other measures were tried first. Counting the points left out as centre lines makes a star uneven, but a letter V 4 mm tall varies by 0.29 of its width, 0.01 under that rule’s bound. Measured on one side alone, or as the longest stitch for the line’s length, a sharp corner counts as a star’s point does. The rule has 2 limits. A star of 3 points measures 0.87, since its third point lies on one rail as a corner does, and stays a column. A star nowhere wider than the narrowest satin stroke is a line before its columns are looked for.

Holes (REQ-AUTO-033). A part that is filled is filled whole under the areas that fill its holes, and they sew on it (Layers). A dog’s nose sews as one fill with the nostrils on it, where it sewed as 4 columns round them. A hole that nothing fills is left open, and a part sewn as columns keeps its holes, as a ring or a letter’s counter does.

What leaves a fill (REQ-AUTO-034). A piece of an area leaves the fill only where it is narrower than the narrowest piece of a fill, and only when the rules make it a line or a column. A balloon’s string leaves the balloon, and a wing 6 mm wide stays in the fill.

Refused columns (REQ-AUTO-035). A satin stitch longer than the widest satin column lies loose, and a column no wider than that on average still sews one where its rails part, at a corner or round a bend. A column’s longest stitch lies between the 2 points as far along its rails, as shares of their lengths from one rung to the next, at a corner of either rail. A part with a column that would sew a longer one is filled, unless it is wide (Wide columns), and SC-I0902 gives the stitch’s length. The widest satin column is the longest satin stitch a machine sews well, so a shape may set it lower and never higher. It is the same for every machine profile, for the reason wide columns gives.

Seeing and changing the choice

SC-I0912 says, for each part of a shape, which rule it sewed by and the measures that decided it (REQ-AUTO-036). The settings come in 3 tiers (Settings):

  • A shape names its stitch with auto_stitch. A part that can’t sew so goes by the table.
  • A design leans the rules towards satin columns or fills with auto_prefer, which moves 3 bounds together. auto_fill_uneven turns the filling of uneven parts off, and auto_fill_angle gives every fill one angle.
  • The bounds themselves are advanced settings. No dialog shows them, and stitch plan --set sets one on every shape digitized automatically, unless the shape sets it.

A style is a named set of values of these settings, and no rule of its own: stitch plan --style logo for flat art drawn in strokes, and --style photo for a traced photo, which is the defaults.

Choosing a style

The style stays the person’s choice (REQ-AUTO-150, M13.42). An auto style would choose logo or photo from the picture’s own measures, and no measure tried chooses well on a varied set. This section keeps the evidence, measured on 2026-11-20 on main at 6c565e4c.

What the choice changes. The 2 styles differ in 2 settings. auto_prefer of satin leans 3 bounds towards columns. auto_fill_uneven off keeps the columns of an uneven part and sews each ragged thin line as a line where photo merges it into its neighbour. So the wrong style costs:

PictureIn its own styleIn the other
A traced photoits thin lines merge, and it sews thinlogo: 2 to 11 times the area at 3 fills, up to 7.1 fills, the cap out of budget (SC-W0913). With the cap’s work fixed (The cap at the end of planning), up to 5.5 fills
Flat art, lettering or a drawingits lines and columns sew as drawnphoto: up to 20 % of its detail lost, as lines merge into fills (SC-I0915)

Some designs sew alike in both: a badge of broad flat shapes, a drawing of even lines or a sky of flat bands doesn’t change any part’s rule. The 4 traced dogs keep 1 to 5 % less of their detail in photo, so neither style is better on every count.

The set. 25 pictures of known style and 3 undecided ones. Drawn in code for this step: flat art in an even pen, flat art with hard bands of shade, photo-like pictures with soft edges and grain, 2 drawings in an uneven pen, lettering on a field, and 2 flat pictures photographed, with light that falls off and grain. A person picks logo for the flat art, the drawings, the lettering and the photographed art, and photo for the soft pictures. A person can’t place 3: a badge with a soft gradient, and 2 pictures that are half photo and half flat art. The repository adds the 4 traced dogs, the pear, the traced badge and the lettered patch, and 4 traced photos of flowers, a bird and a building were measured locally. Each was traced by VectorCraft 0.24.1 in 6 colours, 100 mm across, from 1,000 and 500 pixels.

The measures. None separates the 2 groups:

  • Flat colour in the picture, the share of pixels whose neighbours match within 3 levels: 0.80 to 0.96 for drawn flat art, as a JPEG too, and 0.29 at most for the photo-like pictures. A photographed badge reads 0.001, and a dog cut out of its photo on a white ground reads 0.64, so both get the wrong style.
  • Flat colour in the subject, the ground’s colour left out: the dog reads 0.03, but a drawing in an uneven pen reads 0.43 from 1,000 pixels and 0.10 from 500, below a furry picture’s 0.25.
  • Thin lines drawn evenly, the roadmap’s example: a trace draws every thin line ragged, a pen line too. Every picture had 0 to 0.006 of its thin-line area even, and the even shield’s 4 lines were the only exception.
  • Small parts, the share of parts under 2 mm²: 0 to 0.88 for flat art and 0.04 to 0.97 for photos. The running dog reads 0.07 at 100 mm, 0.46 at 60 mm and 0 at 150 mm.
  • Uneven parts, the share of area in parts of several centre lines whose width varies by more than 0.3: 0.38 for a hand-drawn flower, 0.77 from 500 pixels, and 0 to 0.26 for 5 of the photos.

Even thin lines tell nothing apart. A bound on flat colour, of the picture or of its subject, misstyles 4 of the 19 pictures with a source to measure, and a bound on small or uneven parts 6 or 7 of the 25. Each bound moves with the picture’s pixels and size. Which style sews a picture better can flip too: a lettered disc sewed 572 mm² at 3 fills in logo and 87 in photo from 1,000 pixels, and 73 and 351 from 500. A picture of both kinds, such as a label on a photo, wants logo on the label and photo on the rest, which one style can’t give. Shape settings, marks (--keep) and Expand serve such a picture.

What is said instead. One notice, SC-I0923 (REQ-AUTO-151, auto::style), tells the person that the choice matters for this design and which way the other style goes. The other style is the preset whose auto_fill_uneven differs from the shape’s, with its values set over the shape’s settings as --style sets them. The notice counts 2 things:

  • Thin lines. In the photo style, the lines that merged into their neighbours. In the logo style, the ragged thin lines that merging would take, found as merging finds them, which stay as they are. A merge keeps some of them for the picture’s likeness, so the notice says that they may merge.
  • Parts. Each part is decided again from the measures that decided it, under the other style’s bounds. A part filled here that the other style sews as satin columns counts with its rule, and a part sewn as columns that the other style fills counts too.

The notice names the other style and its settings, and says nothing else: no opinion of which style is right, and nothing of what the picture shows. A design whose counts are all 0 gets none. It changes no stitch. The count of the parts plans nothing again, so it leaves out how a fill splits off its narrow pieces, which auto_prefer leans too, and the lines that a merge keeps for the picture’s likeness.

What it costs. The photo style adds a unit of work for each part and no geometry. The logo style measures the thin parts of each area once more, as merging would. On the 28 pictures, each planned 3 times in each style before and after the notice, the fastest plans took 3.5 % longer in all in the logo style and 1.6 % less in the photo style. The time goes to the 4 large traced photos, which took 1 to 8 % longer in the logo style, where the runs’ noise is about 7 %. Every machine file was the same byte for byte. A label on fur and a building’s photo planned with SC-E0101 in the logo style, before the notice too.

What it says on the set. The notice is silent on the 6 pictures that sew alike in both styles. Among them are 3 drawn in an even pen and the traced badge. On the other 22 it speaks in both styles, and its direction is right. Flat art planned as a photo hears that 1 to 5 of its parts, filled by the rule uneven or compact, would keep their columns, and a traced dog planned as a logo hears that 39 to 52 of its parts would be filled and 14 to 30 of its lines may merge. Its counts mislead:

  • A count is no weight. The lettered disc hears of 1 part in each style, though that part, its ring, was 572 mm² at 3 fills in logo and 87 in photo. The lettered patch hears of 5 parts, and the lettered sign of 2, where under 2 % of their area sews by another rule.
  • Lines that may merge outnumber those that merge. The soft fruit in logo hears of 14 lines, and 6 merge in photo. The furry picture hears of 234, and 108 merge.

So the notice says that the choice matters and which way the other style goes, not how much it costs. The previews and SC-W0913 tell that.

The grounds of the bounds

The narrowest and the widest satin column are established: digitizing manuals put the narrowest satin at 1 to 1.5 mm, and split a satin stitch at 7 mm to keep its look. The least slenderness, the most a width varies and the narrowest piece of a fill are StitchCraft’s own. Their values come from measurements of 9 pictures, which are 2 traced dogs, 2 traced photos, a pear, 3 emblems and a lettered patch, whose letters all kept their columns. Between a slenderness of 2 and 4 the running dog’s result moves by 7 shapes of 107. The bound of a part with arms, 1.4, lies between what was measured on 2026-11-15. Drawn bands, leaves, crosses and letters measure at most 1.0, the lettered patch’s letters at most 1.19, and the dogs’ parts that sew as columns at most 1.23. Drawn stars of 4, 5 and 6 points measure 1.61 to 2.62, and 5 blobs of the dogs’ fur 1.51 to 2.11. MC-8 confirms the defaults or changes them.

What it costs

Measured on the 4 traced dogs on 2026-11-14, against the step before (tests/auto_photos.rs records the numbers):

Traced dogShapes sewn as satin columnsAs fillsJumpsThread changesSliversBare
On turf, 6 threads64 to 274 to 45124 to 8210 to 124 to 00.204 to 0.020 %
On turf, 9 threads73 to 313 to 49143 to 9412 to 2210 to 10.346 to 0.097 %
Running, 6 threads59 to 216 to 47113 to 8112 to 163 to 10.107 to 0.026 %
Running, 9 threads79 to 297 to 60135 to 11218 to 282 to 00.073 to 0.001 %

The satin stitches that lean along their column (SC-W0208), which a fan sews, fell from 26 to 34 a dog to 4 to 8. The thread changes rose with M13.21, and each is a rethread by hand on a machine with one needle. A design of many small fills has many lines, each of which sews after the fills it borders. M13.22 searches for the order that changes thread least, and the dogs changed thread 8, 12, 8 and 12 times (Sewing order). Measured then, on 2026-11-14, with auto_fill_uneven off they changed thread 6, 9, 7 and 10 times and jumped 113, 135, 105 and 132 times, where they jumped 84, 91, 79 and 108 times as planned. The switch trades thread changes for jump threads. Each later step’s thread changes are recorded in tests/auto_photos.rs.

Shapes whose width swells and tapers

Built in M13.59 (REQ-AUTO-174, REQ-AUTO-175). A shape whose width swells and tapers sews as one fill with its outline, and a piece of a broad part whose width falls to a point stays in the part’s fill. A satin column that narrows to a point still ends blunt: holding its pull compensation was measured and left out (below). The owner reviewed 18 drawings sewn by automatic digitizing on 2026-11-21, and 3 of her notes have one cause, the choice between a satin column and a fill for a shape whose width changes along it:

  • 2 leaves of one kind, mirrored, sewed differently: one as a fill with an outline, the other as a satin column without one. The demo badge’s lake, a flat top over a curved bottom, 5.6 mm at its widest, sewed as a column without an outline beside fills that have one.
  • A crescent sewed as a fill in its middle with a satin column at each tip. The tips zigzagged unevenly, and a line showed where their stitches met the fill’s rows.
  • The rays of a sunrise, narrow wedges, ended blunt.

  • Better. The crescent is one fill whose outline runs to both points, its rows even to the tips. The leaves sew alike. The lake is filled and outlined to its points as the shapes round it are. The rays narrow to a point.

c, deciding like parts together, isn’t needed on this set. With the rules above only the crowding bound still parts twins, on specks. e, an outline round a column among outlined fills, isn’t needed either: with n no column 4 mm or wider is left of a pointed oval. The rules

The owner chose n + b + d on 2026-11-22. n and b are built by the settings there are. d was built, measured with M13.60 on main, and left out of the step on 2026-11-22: it made a design sew above 4 fills.

  • A part of one line at least auto_min_fill_width_mm (4 mm) wide somewhere, whose width varies by more than auto_max_unevenness (0.3) of itself, is filled whole when auto_fill_uneven is on: a leaf, a lens, a petal. Its outline draws its points (REQ-AUTO-174). A narrower one keeps its column.
  • A piece of a broad part whose width varies by more than auto_max_unevenness stays in the part’s fill. An even piece, a lollipop’s stick or a balloon’s string, leaves it as before (REQ-AUTO-175).

auto_prefer moves both bounds, so satin keeps a leaf 5.4 mm wide and varying by 0.39 a column. The logo style, with auto_fill_uneven off, keeps every part of one line a column as today. A tapering piece stays in its fill in every style. The piece rule is part of one shape’s treatment, and a drawing in strokes has no broad part to cut. A shape that should sew otherwise names its stitch: auto_stitch of satin sews a narrow part as columns, and fill fills the shape whole. Both exist today. One part of a shape can’t be set apart from the others, and Expand serves that.

What changes besides: REQ-AUTO-032 no longer exempts a part of one line, and the star points of star.svg and TS-13C sew in their fills. The leaf of the rules’ fixture rules.svg, 4 mm wide at most, keeps its column.

How it is built. The rules measure a part of one line against a fill’s narrowest piece only when its width varies by more than the bound and uneven parts are filled (Measures::fill_wide, in auto::columns::measured): that shrinks the part once, and other parts cost nothing more. A piece that tapers is kept in the fill as the narrow parts are sorted (auto::narrow), and its shape’s auto_stitch of satin keeps it a column.

  • Points that stay blunt. A satin column that tapers to a point, as a sun’s ray does, still ends about 0.9 mm wide 0.5 mm from its point, since pull compensation moves each needle point 0.4 mm out however narrow the stitch. Holding the compensation to 40 % of the stitch’s width pointed the rays, 0.00 to 0.32 mm wide there, and lowered the area above the cap of the traced documents from 363.7 to 344.4 mm². It also raised 5 designs’ thickest spots by up to 0.09 fills, and a traced dog sewn with its details last went to 4.008 fills. It was left out, and a column’s point is a step of its own.
  • A crescent’s points. The outline runs through each point once, in along one edge and out along the other (REQ-AUTO-175’s case). The fill’s last rows reach to 0.2 mm from the point, and their turns run 0.1 to 0.2 mm beside the outline for the last 0.5 mm. In a preview they read as a fork. They lie under the outline’s thread, which is 0.4 mm wide. Holding a fill back from an outline at its points would move every outlined fill, and is left for a step of its own.

M13.58 keeps a column’s spacing on the inside of a bend and pulls stitch ends in only where a spot would sew above 4 fills. A pointed oval sewn as a column fans its stitches round its ends, and M13.58 would open the outside of that fan. Filled, the oval has no fan, so the 2 steps meet only on the columns n keeps.

Outlines drawn 2 ways

The rules measure a part’s geometry. Until M13.53 the result also depended on the outline’s drawing: how many points it has, where they fall and where a ring starts. The same outline drawn 2 ways that sew alike within a thread’s width could sew by 2 rules. Every step that touches outlines then reshuffled which shapes were columns, and its own effect couldn’t be seen through that noise. Measured on main 6f5703b7 (2026-11-20).

Causes in the code

Each finding below has its smallest pair of outlines drawn by hand, at most 0.02 mm apart, which sew by 2 rules today. They are the cases of REQ-AUTO-135 to REQ-AUTO-138 (tests/auto_drawing.rs).

  • A cap or a tip. An end of a centre line is a cap when its corner lies inside the circle on its chord, edge included (centre lines). A square corner lies exactly on that circle. A bar 2 mm wide and 12 mm long whose corner moves 0.02 mm towards its middle leaves the 2 corners beside it at 89.6°, outside the circle. That end becomes a tip, the line runs on into a corner, and the end’s other corner lies 1.40 times as far from the line as the bar’s widest circle reaches. The bar sews by the rule arms, as a fill, where drawn square it sews as a column.
  • Every point goes into the triangulation. The triangulation cuts long edges but keeps every point of the outline. Points added along an edge change its junctions and where the rungs fall, and rungs that cross are held in place into a fan, which the crowding test sees. A bar whose end leans 0.02 mm sews as a column drawn with 4 corners and is refused for crowding drawn with a point every 0.1 mm, 2.98 mm of one edge to 1 mm of the other. A wedge does the same at 3.4 mm, and a quarter ring at 2.66 mm.
  • A column read back. A part’s columns are drawn with rails and rungs and read back as any satin column is read, to within a millionth of a millimetre (generators::satin::drawn). When one rung reads back otherwise the whole part is refused and filled (SC-I0902). An L or a T with one end corner moved 0.02 mm is refused so. A T whose stem joins its bar 0.02 mm off square is refused when its ring starts at another corner.
  • Direction and place. A ring drawn the other way round doesn’t change a rule on any picture, and a whole picture moved 0.05 mm changed 2 shapes of more than 10,000.

The measure of stability

The probe draws every automatic shape of a picture again in 6 ways, within 0.02 mm of the drawing. A curve split in 2 at its middle is the same curve with twice the points. A dense drawing has a straight piece every 0.1 mm. A jittered one has every point moved 0.02 mm in a direction its position chooses, so that a point that 2 shapes share moves alike in both. The others start each ring halfway round, draw it the other way, or move the picture 0.05 mm. A shape changes when the rules of its parts change, specks aside. The set is the repository’s traced pictures, the traced lettered patch and the hand-made pairs, 392 shapes, and 558 letters of 3 fonts at 3 heights, as drawn and as traced from pictures of them. A local set of 4 traced photos, 8,833 shapes, is never committed.

Each of the 4 traced dogs has 27 to 38 of its 69 to 92 shapes change. A shape whose measure lies within 10 % of a bound of the rules may fall either way however the outline is drawn, and no rule can keep it steady: 70 of the pictures’ 163 and 126 of the letters’ 324 are so. In 346 of the photos’ 489 a column can’t be drawn under one of the drawings.

A fix, prototyped

The prototype measures and draws each part on its outline drawn again in one standard way:

  • Thinned. A point goes when the ring without it stays within 0.05 mm of every point it had between its neighbours. The point whose leaving moves the ring least goes first, a tie going to the lower point. A point whose triangle with its neighbours holds another point of the part stays, so that no ring comes to cross another. The result doesn’t depend on where a ring starts or which way it runs.
  • Evenly spaced. Between its corners, where it turns by more than 30°, each ring is cut again into even pieces no longer than its area over its perimeter, from its lowest point when it has no corner. A run between 2 corners is cut into twice as many even pieces until it lies within 0.05 mm of the thinned ring. Without that, a large part’s pieces cut across its curves, and its fill laid 9 stitches over its neighbour on a dog of 6 threads.
  • A cap within 0.05 mm. An end is a cap when its corners lie within 0.05 mm outside the circle on its chord.
  • A rung left out. A column that reads back otherwise is drawn again without the first rung whose leaving makes it read back, and refused only when none does.

With all 4, the shapes that change fall from 163 to 98 on the pictures and from 324 to 38 on the letters. Drawing more points changes 6 letters where it changed 174. Of the 98 left, 39 have a measure within 10 % of a bound and 21 a column that can’t be drawn. On the photos the count falls only from 489 to 389, and 203 of those have a column that can’t be drawn under one of the drawings. The letters as drawn and as traced sew by other rules 20 times, against 32 today. Without the even spacing the counts are 114 and 109, and without the rung left out a few more.

A column judged as it sews

The knife edges above refused many poor columns by accident. A test in their place has to judge the column on the stitches it will sew, so that the outline’s drawing can’t change the verdict. The prototype samples each column’s stitches as the rules pair its rails, one every 0.4 mm, and measures them:

MeasureWhat a sewer sees
CrossingA stitch crosses one of the next 5 away from their ends
LeanThe angle between a stitch and square to the way the column runs
FanThe most of one rail paired with 1 mm of the other
SwingHow much the stitches’ lengths spread: 90th percentile less 10th, over the median
SlopeThe most a stitch’s length changes per millimetre the column runs
AspectHow many times as long as wide the column is
OutsideThe share of the stitches’ length more than 0.1 mm outside the part
BareThe share of the part’s inside, 0.15 mm in from its edge, that no stitch covers

Lean, fan, swing and slope are also taken on the column’s body, a median width or more from each end, because a column’s ends fan by design where they run under another column.

The trade sets satin’s width and leaves its shape to the digitizer’s eye. Wilcom gives about 10 mm as the widest satin and 12 mm at most, and uses fill for large areas (stitch techniques). Another guide gives 0.8 to 8.9 mm, satin for most lettering and fill from about 2.5 mm (stitch types). Wilcom’s turning satin turns its stitches evenly along the shape, tighter at pointed ends (turning stitches). Ink/Stitch, read at d59c9ab, checks a satin column’s rails and rungs as drawn and warns about a column narrower than 0.3 mm. It doesn’t judge how the stitches lie, because a person draws each column.

The measures were taken on 4 groups. The columns that main makes and the redrawn outline keeps were taken as mostly good, and the columns that only the redrawn outline makes as often poor. The 832 columns of the 2 built-in fonts are digitized by hand. Letters as drawn and as traced are twins. The groups overlap on every measure:

Filled at 12 mm the letters look clean, but the trade sews most lettering in satin for its sheen.

The bounds are knife edges too. Under the 6 drawings, 165 of the pictures’ 392 shapes and 324 of the 1,116 letters change rule on main, and 100 and 38 with the first prototype. The redrawn outline whose runs lie within 0.05 mm of the thinned ring leaves 102 and 55. On the 4 traced photos the hand-digitized bounds lower the magnolia’s area at 3 fills from 217.5 to 198.8 mm². They raise the coleus’s from 306.3 to 336.0 mm², the goose’s from 156.8 to 179.3 mm² and the coleus’s thickest spot from 4.16 to 5.31 fills, as the redrawn outline alone does.

So no set of bounds meets the step’s bar on the pictures and the letters together. The area at 3 fills comes with every column the redrawn outline adds, poor or not: a well-formed narrow column adds 2.8 to 11 mm², because it lies on its neighbours’ 1 mm overlaps. The hand-digitized fonts’ columns keep a body fan within 3 and a body swing within 0.55 on the same kinds of letters, so the columns that look poor fan where the rules place their rungs.

Rungs placed and less overlap

A third prototype kept the outline drawn again, with its even spacing, and changed 2 things in place of a test. It was measured on the same set as the first 2.

Rungs placed from the rails. The rungs come from the 2 rails alone, not from the triangulation. Both rails are sampled at even steps of at most 0.1 mm. The rungs follow the pairing of the rails that runs forward on both and makes the stitches shortest: the sum of each rung’s length times how far the centre moves on. Every such pairing moves the centre the same distance in all. The shortest one lies square across a straight column and turns about the inside of a bend. Its rungs run forward on both rails, which keeps them from crossing there. Taken as it is, it piles a bend’s stitches on the inner corner: one column of the running dog fanned 20 to 1. So the pairing is eased. How far one rail runs ahead of the other is averaged over the column’s median width on each side, which keeps both rails running forward and spreads a bend’s turn over 2 widths. A rung is kept every millimetre of the centre line.

On the columns that the outline drawn again makes, the eased rungs lower the body fan’s 90th percentile from 3.6 to 1.9 on the pictures and from 4.5 to 2.4 on the letters. The share of columns within the hand-digitized bounds rises from 0.74 to 0.81 on the pictures and to 0.87 on the letters. The stitches lean more: the body lean’s 90th percentile rises from 19° to 26° on the pictures and from 15° to 26° on the letters, and 5 picture columns and 2 letter columns cross where 2 and none did. Easing over 2 or 3 widths leans and crosses more. Placed rungs also crowd less, so fewer parts are refused: judged on them, the pictures make 154 columns where they made 120, and the running dog’s area at 3 fills rises from 94 to 130 mm². So the prototype chooses each part’s rule on the rungs as found and sews its columns on the placed rungs.

Less overlap under a part’s column. An area sewn before a part’s satin column reaches its whole overlap of 1 mm under it, and is cut away under it only 1 mm inside the column’s edge. A column narrower than 2 mm then lies whole on the area, and with its own underlay and top stitches the spot sews 3 fills. A column’s stitches reach its pull compensation of 0.4 mm past both rails because the thread pulls it in that far, and an area’s rows pull in as far across them. So an area under the column has to lie only its pull compensation inside the column’s edge, and the prototype keeps 0.4 mm of it there. On main this alone lowers every traced picture’s area at 3 fills, the patch’s from 420 to 278.5 mm². The fabric measure doesn’t find a gap with 0.4 mm kept, or with none kept, because it doesn’t pull stitches in: a sew-out has to show how much a column needs. Keeping 0.2 mm would hold the dogs’ area within the noise of main’s, but no measure gives 0.2 mm, so it would be tuned to them.

PictureArea at 3 fills, mm²: main, third prototypeThickest spot, fillsStitches astrayJump threads

The badge sews as on main, and planning takes 5 % longer. The third prototype misses the bar in each of its parts. The running dog’s area at 3 fills rises 18 mm² and the other dog’s 10 mm², where the noise is 8.5 mm², and 2 photos rise 32 and 50 mm². The coleus’s thickest spot rises 1.15 fills and the castle’s 0.71, as with the outline drawn again alone. The running dog gains 3 stitches astray and the patch 1, which the even spacing brings. Under the 6 drawings 101 of the pictures’ shapes and 54 letters change rule. The outline drawn again alone gives 102 and 55, and the bar is fewer than 100 and 38. The letters as drawn and as traced sew by other rules 22 times, against 32 on main. By M13.47’s measures of the joins, the share of joins more than 1.5 times as thick as their stroke rises at apexes from 0.62 to 0.70 and at crossings from 0.68 to 0.71, and falls a little at most other kinds. By eye, a placed column lies straighter than main’s (the black column on the dog on turf), but the patch’s A and M of the upper word and its T and LEM of the lower word sew as rougher satin where main fills them cleanly. Serif letters sewn as satin lose their serifs.

So placing rungs well helps the columns it is given, and keeping less overlap under a part’s column takes thread away on main as much as with the outline drawn again. The same measures fail as before. The area at 3 fills comes with the columns that the outline drawn again adds, wide ones too, and with the fills it reshapes. The 2 photos’ thickest spots and the stitches astray come with the outline drawn again and its even spacing, not with any column.

The step stopped

The owner chose on 2026-11-20 to stop the step after these 3 rounds, and main’s rule stays. No prototype meets the step’s bar. Measured against main on each round’s base, the outline drawn again alone raises the area at 3 fills by up to 29 % and sews the patch’s letters with fanned columns. A refusing test with fixed bounds adds knife edges of its own and fills 391 letters. Rungs placed from the rails, with less overlap under a part’s column, still raise the dogs’ and 2 photos’ area and the stitches astray.

2 findings may become steps of their own:

  • Less overlap under a part’s satin column. Keeping only the pull compensation, 0.4 mm, lowers main’s own area at 3 fills: the patch’s falls from 420 to 278.5 mm² on base efb23abe. A sew-out has to show how much overlap a column needs before a profile may change.
  • Rungs placed from the rails on the columns main already makes. Placed rungs fan less (the 90th percentile of the fan falls from 3.62 to 1.87 on the pictures) but lean more and cross more. They were measured only with the outline drawn again, never alone.

The step opens again only with a rule that keeps the drawing out of the choice and doesn’t raise the area at 3 fills, measured on every picture and letter of the set. The prototype’s choices would then stand as they were measured: a tolerance of 0.05 mm, a quarter of a running stitch’s own tolerance of 0.2 mm and 2.5 times the jitter measured; a column that reads back otherwise fixed where it is built, since leaving out its rung hides the cause of most of the photos’ changes; and every automatic part measured on its outline drawn again, with no setting to turn it off.

What isn’t built

Nothing: REQ-AUTO-135 to REQ-AUTO-139 are planned, and their cases are marked planned. The cases of REQ-AUTO-135 to REQ-AUTO-138 fail on main. The case of REQ-AUTO-139 passes on main: it measures each column’s stitches in the plan against the bounds that a rule which reopens the step must keep. The 3 prototypes’ code and the measures are kept with the step’s notes, not in the repository.

Wide columns

A part between 7 and 10 mm wide that is otherwise a good column, a border or a bar, sews as one satin column whose stitches split at 7 mm (M13.54, REQ-AUTO-152 to REQ-AUTO-156), once a person raises auto_max_split_satin_width_mm. The owner chose this in the stitch-rules work, so that a wide border keeps satin’s sheen where a fill reads flat. It is off by default (auto::wide).

What the trade does. Digitizing software splits a satin stitch longer than about 7 mm, which would lie loose and snag, into shorter ones. Wilcom’s Auto Split is “used primarily to prevent long stitches in wide columns”, with the tip “Use a length of 7.00 mm to preserve the satin effect” (split satin stitches). It “distributes needle penetrations in a random pattern so that they do not form a line down the middle of the shape” (long satin stitches). BERNINA’s Satin Special spreads its splits at random for the same reason (split long stitches). Wilcom’s blog puts the widest satin at about 10 mm (a column judged as it sews). mySewnet keeps patterned satin for columns “wider than about 8-10 mm”, and asks for zigzag and edge-walk underlay together under very wide satin (satin column). Wilcom supports wide columns with zigzag and double zigzag underlay (zigzag underlay). Ink/Stitch’s satin column splits a stitch longer than max_stitch_length_mm by its split_method. Its documentation names the default, simple and staggered methods, random_split_jitter_percent, random_split_phase, min_random_split_length_mm and split_staggers (satin column). StitchCraft’s satin generator has them since M4.5 (REQ-SAT-003, top stitches).

The machine’s longest stitch is another limit. The reference profile’s longest stitch is 12 mm, a common value for Brother home machines that MC-1 and MC-2 confirm. Wilcom’s machine formats give 12.1 mm for ternary coding and 12.7 mm for binary (stitch and jump length). The widest satin column, 7 mm, is how long a satin stitch lies well, which the thread and the fabric decide, not the machine. So it stays one value for every profile, and a wide column’s stitches split at it.

The rule. A row of the table, tried just before the area rule. Its parts are wider than auto_max_satin_width_mm somewhere and no wider than auto_max_split_satin_width_mm anywhere, to the micrometre. Such a part is wide when the other rules would make it a column and it has one centre line whose width varies by at most auto_max_unevenness of itself. It sews as a satin column whose stitches split at 7 mm. Any other such part is an area, as before. A stroke as wide sews as a wide column along its centre line (REQ-AUTO-156). Only a whole part is wide. A piece cut from a broader part keeps today’s rules.

  • One line and even (REQ-AUTO-153). Where 2 wide columns meet, each fans its split stitches into the other. A column whose width swells has stitches of unlike lengths, whose splits fall unevenly.
  • Refused (REQ-AUTO-154). A wide part’s columns are refused when they can’t be drawn or crowd their stitches on one rail, as a satin column’s are. A stitch longer than 7 mm between the rails is no reason, since it is split. A ring drawn 10 mm wide measures its longest stitch at 10.003 mm between its points, and held to the bound as REQ-AUTO-035 holds a column it was refused at the width it was drawn to.
  • How it is drawn. M13.53 found that the rules react to how an outline is drawn (outlines drawn 2 ways). The bound is a new edge of that kind, so it holds within a tolerance, WIDE_TOLERANCE, 0.05 mm: an eighth of the thread’s width, which no stitch shows. A ring drawn 10 mm wide with 180 points a side measures 10.003 mm where its points fall, and a person who sets the bound to the width they drew expects it to be wide. Every case at the bound uses the constant. No part of the set lies between 9 and 11 mm wide and would sew a wide column. If M13.53’s judge of a column as it sews is built (REQ-AUTO-139), a wide column meets it too.
  • Its holes. The layers close the holes of a filled part under what fills them (REQ-AUTO-033). A wide part sews along its middle, so it keeps its holes, as a column does.
  • The split (REQ-AUTO-155). max_stitch_length_mm 7, the default method, random_split_phase on and random_split_jitter_percent 10. The underlay is a contour and a zigzag, as for a column wider than 4 mm.

What the split looks like. Measured with a prototype on bars, rings, arches and stroked circles drawn in code at 7.5, 8.5 and 10 mm, with each split method. A split point is a needle point inside the column between 2 points far across from it. The table gives the bar 8.5 mm wide.

SplitRows of split points, 0.25 mm apartNext split point within 0.2 mmStitchesUnder 0.5 mmThickest
Default, equal parts6, 252 of 257 in one98 %5105313.96 fills
Simple7, 251 of 257 in one98 %4932313.84 fills
Staggered, 49, 3 rows of 62 or 630.5 %5321313.90 fills
Random phase, 10 % jitter21, none over 158 %53841463.73 fills

The stitches and the thickest spot are the whole drawn sheet’s. Equal parts and simple splits put nearly every split point on one line along the column, the line the trade avoids. Staggered splits never meet their neighbour’s but repeat every 4 stitches, a ladder of short diagonals. Random phase scatters them, and only 6 to 9 % meet the next at any width. The prototype counted only 6 split points of a simple split at 7.5 mm. That was the measure, not the generator: simple splits each stitch 7 mm from its start, and at 7.5 mm, with the pull compensation, that point lies 1.3 mm from the far rail, where the count, which takes a point between 2 neighbours more than 2 mm across from it, leaves it out. The build’s satin generator splits all 251.

Which parts of the set change: none. The build confirmed it with the setting at 10 mm: every design and photo sews the same stitches, thread, jumps and thickness, and plans in the same time. A traced dog’s patch that crowds 6.2 to 1 and Alpine Night’s far ridge, whose column can’t be drawn, each add an SC-I0902 for a part refused as a wide column. On the 13 repository designs (the 4 traced dogs, the pear, the 2 traced badges, the lettered patch, the round patch, the patch, the badge, the emblem and Alpine Night) and the 4 local photos, 13 parts are wider than 7 mm and no wider than 10 mm.

7 of the traced dogs, 7.4 to 9.8 mm6 have 2 centre lines or more and vary by more than 0.3, and one crowds its stitches 6.2 to 1
The traced badge’s gold disc and Alpine Night’s mooncompact
Alpine Night’s far ridge and 3 parts of the photostheir columns can’t be drawn

No part of the set lies within 10 % of the bound, 9 to 11 mm wide, and would flip there. The lettered patch’s border, the badges’ rings and the emblem’s frame are 7 mm wide or less, or wider than 11 mm. The rule is for drawn artwork, and its cases are drawn in code.

What it costs. The build’s numbers (2026-11-20), the drawn sheet of a bar, a ring, an arch and a stroked circle, with the setting at 7 mm, as on main, and at 10 mm:

At 7.5 mm, 8.4 % of the bar’s split points lie within 0.2 mm of the next. Planning took as long either way, 0.03 to 0.04 s for the sheet. At 10 mm the ring is wide now that its long stitch no longer refuses it, and the arch is refused for crowding its inner rail 2.1 to 1 and filled, with SC-I0902.

What gets worse is thickness and thread: the columns sew about 50 % more thread than the fills. The thick spots have 2 causes. At 7.5 and 8.5 mm they lie where a ring or a stroked circle closes. There its last stitches lie over its underlay and its first, as on a 6 mm column (3.35 fills over 3.75 mm²). At 10 mm the ring also passes 3 fills along the inner third of its band all round: its inner rail is 15 mm from the centre against 25 mm for the outer. Its stitches lie 1.67 times as close there, over the zigzag underlay. The bare fabric lies in a thin band along the inner edge of the curved columns, the arch and the stroked circle, where the crowded needle points moved in along their stitches (REQ-AUTO-071, retired in M13.58). It is a satin column’s own, 5.92 mm² on the sheet 6 mm wide. The bars and the rings leave none. The short stitches and the jumps of a fill’s row ends mostly go.

Decisions (main, 2026-11-20, the owner’s first concern being how thick a design sews):

  1. Off by default everywhere, the logo style included: auto_max_split_satin_width_mm is 7 mm unless a person raises it. A wide column sews thicker and with half as much thread again, so no style turns it on before the owner has sewn it at MC-8. With the default, every design sews as on main.
  2. The upper bound is the person’s, up to 12 mm (WIDEST_SPLIT_SATIN). The docs give 10 mm as the trade’s usual widest, and what a wider column costs.
  3. The split, at a random phase with 10 % jitter. It is the only method of the 4 whose split points don’t fall in lines. Random means the design’s seeded generator: the same design sews the same stitches every time.
  4. 7 mm stays one value for every profile, as above.
  5. The bound holds within a tolerance, as above.

What isn’t built. A wide piece of a broader part, a wide column of several lines, and patterned satin (mySewnet’s patterns, Wilcom’s program splits) are left out.

Stitch types by width

An automatic element is measured at the size it sews, in millimetres (REQ-AUTO-002). Its width at a place is the diameter of the largest circle that fits inside it there. This section says how each stitch is made once the rules have chosen it.

A stroke is a line as wide as it is drawn (M13.1):

  • No wider than min_satin_stroke_width_mm it sews as a running stitch along its path (REQ-AUTO-002), tripled from 0.5 mm wide (below). A satin column that narrow is too narrow to stitch across (REQ-SAT-015), so the bound is the same.

  • Up to 7 mm it sews as a satin column as wide as its stroke, with its path as the column’s centre line, as a satin column drawn as one path does (REQ-SAT-016, REQ-AUTO-003). A centre line is one subpath, so a stroke of several sews a column for each, in order. The second and later columns are named after the stroke with #2, #3 and so on. A traced line’s ends are round or square, and its caps are part of the line drawn. An open end with a round or square cap reaches half the stroke’s width past the path’s end, straight on, as far as the cap does. A round cap’s end is then square, which covers its corners too. A column cannot follow a subpath that turns more tightly than the stroke is wide, such as a small ring traced from a dot. Nor can it follow a subpath shorter than a CSS pixel. Its rails would fold over themselves, or vanish. The rails the satin column would make tell whether it can follow a subpath. A subpath it cannot follow sews as the area it paints, filled as a wider line is (SC-I0902). So does a dot that a round or square cap draws. A dot with a butt cap draws nothing, and is left out (SC-W0401).

  • Wider than that, the area it paints is filled (REQ-AUTO-004), unless it is no wider than the widest split satin column: then it sews as a wide column along its path (REQ-AUTO-156). That area is each segment widened by half the stroke’s width to each side, with its corners joined as the stroke’s join joins them and its open ends capped with its cap. It is the stroke’s area within the shapes’ tolerance. A miter longer than its limit is cut square. Those pieces, joined into one area, are the fill’s outline. A part of it smaller than 1 mm² is a speck, merged into a neighbour or left out as specks are (Neighbours). An area that would reach more than 10 m from the origin is left out (SC-W0903). No design lies there.

    The pieces are not handed to the area code one by one, which would cost many times the work of their outline. Each open subpath is one ring instead. It follows the right side forward, the end cap, the left side back and the start cap. The outer side goes round each join, and the inner side goes in to the path’s point and out again. A closed subpath is 2 rings, one for each side. Such rings wind round each point as many times as the pieces cover it. The nonzero rule then fills exactly the pieces’ union. A line of more than 32 chords is cut into lengths of 32 chords. Each length is a ring made into its area alone, and the areas are then joined. A 10 mm line a metre long fills within the default budget that way.

Triple runs (M13.4, REQ-AUTO-015). A running stitch that automatic digitizing makes is a triple run from 0.5 mm wide: each stitch is sewn 3 times, bean stitch 1. A running stitch sewn once is as fine as the thread, and a line drawn half a millimetre wide or wider sews bolder tripled. A stroke is as wide as it is drawn, and a narrow part (below) as wide as the circles that fit inside it along the middle. Widths are compared to the micrometre, and MC-8 confirms or changes the bound.

Every automatic satin column, a stroke’s as well as a shape’s, has the settings of its width (M13.5, below). A shape’s area is a tatami fill apart from its narrow parts (M13.4), its parts as wide as a satin column (M13.5) and the narrow parts of its broad ones (M13.6).

Narrow parts (M13.4, REQ-AUTO-013). A part of a shape that is narrower than min_satin_stroke_width_mm everywhere sews as a running stitch along its centre line, as a stroke that narrow does. No circle that wide fits inside such a part: shrunk by half the width with the polygon buffer of M5.7, nothing of it is left. The width is taken half a micrometre wider, so that a part as wide as the bound, give or take a rounding error, is narrow, as a stroke is. Before that, a part smaller than 1 mm² is a speck (Neighbours), and stays in the area. So does a part whose centre line has no length, a dot.

  • What sews. The rest of the shape is a tatami fill, and each narrow part’s line sews after it, in the order of the parts. It is named after the shape with #2, #3 and so on, or with the shape’s own name when nothing of it is filled. A trim or a stop after the shape follows its last part.
  • Its width. A part’s width at a point of its centre line is the diameter of the largest circle centred there that fits inside it. Averaged along the line, leaving out the piece that runs on into a tip’s corner, it says whether the part is a triple run (centre lines). A strip as wide all along as a stroke is as wide as the stroke.
  • To its neighbours a narrow part’s line paints the part. It sews after a fill it borders. An area sewn before it reaches under it, and is cut away under the part where the part covers it, as under an area (Neighbours).
  • Once the layers are flattened. A shape’s parts are found in what it sews once the layers are flattened (Neighbours): what shows of it, with the holes its details fill closed. A stacked trace’s dark outline under the colours inside it shows as a ring, and sews as lines and columns, as an abutting trace’s outline does. A broad area’s holes under its details are closed, so the strips of it between the details are part of its fill. The parts are found before specks merge and before each area reaches under its neighbours.
  • SC-I0901 counts a shape whose every part runs as a running stitch, and any other as a tatami fill. Finding a part’s width and centre line is part of the work of digitizing its shape. A part whose width or centre line can’t be found, a bug, stays in the fill, and SC-E0009 names its shape.

Parts as wide as a satin column (M13.5, REQ-AUTO-016 to REQ-AUTO-018). Filled, a part as wide as a satin column sews rows no longer than it is wide wherever they cross it. A digitizer sews it as satin columns along its strips and round its rings. A part that isn’t narrow, and is nowhere wider than 7 mm, is as wide as a satin column. Shrunk by 3.5 mm, taken half a micrometre wider, nothing of it is left. Such a part sews a satin column along each of its centre lines, between its own outlines (centre lines):

  • Its columns. Each line sews one column over the triangles round it. A line is cut where it turns by more than 60°, so that the column before a corner covers it and the one after ends under it, as an arm of a T ends under its bar. A branch of the line is a column of its own however short, as each arm of a t’s crossbar is. A branch to the second sharp corner of an end is a bump instead, sewn with the column beside it. So is a tooth, a step of a traced outline as narrow as the narrowest satin stroke somewhere and no longer than its junction is wide. A column of its own, it would sew a short line beside the column and cut that column in 2. A column’s rails are the part’s outline on either side, and its rungs lie square to it.
  • Their ends. A column that ends where another runs on ends under it: it sews first, and is 0.4 mm longer at that end, its pull compensation, by its push compensation, so that no fabric shows where they meet. Otherwise a column ends at its farthest point along its line, flat along the end’s edge when that lies within 20° of square to the column, and at a point otherwise.
  • Their order. A column that ends under another sews before it. Then the longest sews first, and each next column is the one with an end nearest where the last one ended, sewn from that end. A ring starts at its rung nearest. Where the columns that end under each other go round, as a pinwheel’s bars do, the first in that order sews first, and the last ends on top of it.
  • Their width. A column is as wide as the circles that fit inside the part along its line, as a narrow part is, without the piece of its line into a tip’s corner. One no wider than the design’s narrowest satin stroke runs along its line as a triple run, unless the column is wider than that stroke somewhere along its line, but at its 2 ends, or reaches farther than that from its line beside it. Both are measured inside the column’s own outline, the triangles round its line. A running stitch covers a thread’s width along its line, so where the column is wider, the fabric beside the line shows unless an area sewn before it reaches under it. Measured on average alone, the strands of fur in the owner’s traced dog that widened at an end ran, and so did its small blobs, each beside other lines, and they left fabric bare. So did a column whose line ran along one side of a bump 1.4 mm across: the bump’s triangles were the column’s, far from its line. Where 2 strokes meet, the part is wider than either, by where the junction’s triangle has its corners. Measured inside the part, the circle at the junction of a T in strokes 0.9 mm wide was up to 1.13 mm, and the T’s bar sewed as a satin column one way round and as a triple run mirrored. A column’s own outline crosses to the other column there. A line’s end lies where it meets another line or runs into a corner, and measures that. On TS-13B, the outer legs of the M 4 mm tall, 0.81 mm wide on average, are 1.05 mm wide where the M’s middle strokes meet them, inside their own outlines, and sew as satin columns.
  • A dot. A part whose every line is shorter than half its width along it is a dot, and stays in the fill, as a part whose line has no length does. A disc or a square has no way to run, and its lines are only what its triangles leave of its middle. Its corners lie on one circle, where the triangulation ties, and how each tie breaks depends on where the part lies and how it is drawn. Without the rule, of 2 dots alike one could sew a column across and the other a fill. A disc’s or a square’s lines are 0.4 times as long as it is wide, and discs and squares no wider than a satin column are fills wherever they lie. A rectangle 3 mm wide is a column from 3.5 mm long.
  • What sews. The columns sew after the rest of the shape’s area, named as a narrow part’s lines are, and paint the part to its neighbours. SC-I0901 counts a shape as satin columns when none of it is filled and one of its parts sews a satin column.
  • Drawn. Each column is drawn with its rails and rungs, as the Stroke to Satin filter draws one, and read back it must give them. A part whose triangles don’t make a column round one of its lines, or one of whose columns is read otherwise, stays in the fill, and SC-I0902 says so. A rung added to tell a column’s rails from its rungs goes across an end that doesn’t reach under another column where one can take it (satin columns).

Shapes in parts (M13.6, REQ-AUTO-019). A shape wider than 7 mm in places and narrower in others sews in parts. Its broad parts are a tatami fill, and its narrow parts sew over the fill as lines and columns, as a digitizer sews a balloon’s string over the balloon:

  • Its broad parts are what an opening with a disc 7 mm across leaves: the shape shrunk by 3.5 mm and grown back by 3.5 mm, with the polygon buffer of M5.7, each taken half a micrometre wider, as the bound is. An opening finds them without the shape’s centre lines.
  • Its narrow parts are the rest of the shape, cut where they meet the broad parts. The opening rounds each convex corner of a broad part, and leaves the corner as a narrow part. A narrow part that reaches less than 1 mm² beyond 3.5 mm from the broad parts is such a corner, or a stub too short for a column, and stays in the fill, as a speck does. Length along its centre line tells no corner. The piece a square corner leaves has a horn along each side of the corner, and its centre line runs 6 mm along them, less than half a millimetre wide.
  • The cut. Where a narrow part meets a broad one, the opening leaves an arc, and a column would end there in a point at one of its corners. Each narrow part is cut square there instead, at a chord across it square to the way its sides run on, from the end of the arc that lies farther along that way. What lies past the chord goes to the fill. A chord that would cross the outline of the piece or of the part is left out, and the arc stays. That holds a hair from the chord’s end too. Where an arc ended 2.5e-10 mm before a corner of a traced cat’s ear, the chord crossed the edge after the corner, and the piece had no triangulation (M13.23). A column ends flat along the cut, however long it is, so that a star’s points end along the star’s middle. The triangles that touch the cut are the column’s end, and its line stops where they start (centre lines).
  • What sews. Each narrow part sews as a part of its own. One narrower than min_satin_stroke_width_mm everywhere runs along its centre line, and one no wider than 7 mm sews as satin columns. Any other stays in the fill. The fill is the shape without its narrow parts. It sews first, and reaches the overlap, 1 mm, under the lines and columns sewn over it (Neighbours).

Centre lines. A narrow part’s centre line, its width along it and the rungs across it come from a constrained Delaunay triangulation of its outline, with points added along its long edges so that no triangle is much longer than the part is wide:

  • Each edge of a triangle that lies inside the part is a chord across it, as long as the part is wide there.
  • A triangle with 2 inside edges is part of a strip, and its inside edges are the strip’s rungs.
  • A triangle with 3 inside edges is a junction, where 3 strips meet, and one with 1 is an end.

The centre line joins the middles of the chords (M13.4, REQ-AUTO-014). A round end stops it about half its width from the end, on its middle, and a sharp point draws it on to the point. The small bumps of a traced outline, and the corner past a sharp turn, would branch it, and are left out. A branch longer than its junction is wide is an arm, and has a line, as each arm of a small T’s bar has. At each junction left, the 2 branches that run on most nearly straight are one line and the third ends there, and a ring has a closed line. No piece of the line is sewn twice. A part’s lines sew one after another, the longest first, each from its end nearest where the last one ended. The centre lines page has the rules in full.

A part as wide as a satin column sews a column along each of its lines (M13.5, above). Its rails are the part’s outline on either side, and its rungs come from the triangles across it. A junction joins the 2 lines that run on most nearly straight, and the column of the third ends under theirs. A ring, such as a letter O, is a closed column between its outer and inner outlines. The satin generator sews these columns as it sews columns drawn with rails and rungs.

Settings by width

Every automatic satin column, a stroke’s or a part’s, has the underlay of its width (REQ-AUTO-018), compared to the micrometre. These are starting values, and MC-8 confirms or changes them:

Column widthUnderlay
Below 2 mmCentre walk
2 to 4 mmContour
Above 4 mmContour and zigzag

A stroke’s column is as wide as the stroke, and a part’s as its width along its line. Each has a pull compensation of 0.4 mm, a setting of its own (auto_pull_compensation_mm): the thread pulls a column in from its rails, and stitches that reach that far past them sew the column as wide as drawn. Past a rail that a shape sewn after the column runs along, the column sews by the overlap instead (Neighbours that overlap). A column ends at its rails’ ends rather than at the point nearest the next shape, which would join its ends to the next shape with a straight run across it. Fills keep the tatami fill’s defaults, underlay included.

Holes and edges of columns

A letter traced as an area is cut into columns, and the reader has to see its counters and straight edges. On MC-8’s traced lettered patch (2026-11-17) the owner found the A black all over, and notches along the letters’ edges. 2 rules keep them (M13.36):

  • A hole shows what lies under it (REQ-AUTO-084). A column’s pull compensation reaches 0.4 mm past each rail, and the 3 columns round the A’s counter, a triangle 1.4 mm across inside, sewed over all but 7 % of it. It was no better at M13.30 or at M14.5’s merge, 3 %. A hole that a disc 0.8 mm across fits in, and whose box no shape drawn after the area meets, keeps its columns’ stitches on its edge: a rail along it has no pull compensation on that side, the other rail keeps its own. Each edge’s thread then spreads half a thread into the hole, and the hole keeps (1 - 0.4 / 1.4)² of itself, half of the 10 mm A’s counter. A smaller hole closes under its edges’ threads anyway, and a later shape in the hole’s box may be a detail that the area reaches under (REQ-AUTO-029). A filled area keeps a hole as its rows stop at its edge.
  • An even column keeps its edge (REQ-AUTO-085). Beside a junction a straight stem’s other rail runs round the junction’s corner, and the stem’s outer points lie 0.3 mm apart, closer than the 0.35 mm at which a point moves in (The thickest spot). Every other point then moved in by a quarter of the column, 0.25 mm past its edge, and left a notch: 6 on the M’s stems, 0.65 mm apart. A column whose stitches pair no more than 1.5 mm of one rail with a millimetre of the other moves its first crowded point no farther than its pull compensation and 0.1 mm, a quarter of a thread, past its edge, and the point’s thread still covers the edge. So does a column along a hole kept open, held to the hole’s edge. A column that fans, at a bend or where it ends on the slant under another, keeps the quarter: held there too, TS-13A rose from 4.28 to 4.57 fills and the badges from 4.40 to 4.58.

Where 2 columns of one shape meet, the one that ends under the other reaches its pull compensation past the chord between them. No join of the patch’s letters or TS-13B’s words opened a gap a thread wide, on main or since: the gaps the owner saw were the notches.

Measured with the cases’ plans (tests/auto_letters.rs, fabric::shown), before (main at M13.35) and after. The thickest spot and the jump threads include the start rule of a narrow column:

DesignA’s counter showingNotches in lettersThickest, fillsAt 3 fills or more, mm²Jump threads
Lettered patch7 % → 62 %6 → 04.33 → 4.2052.0 → 43.38 → 8
TS-13B’s word, 15 mm25 % → 73 %14 → 53.88 → 3.634.5 → 1.07 → 7
TS-13B’s word, 10 mm7 % → 62 %6 → 03.63 → 3.280 → 08 → 8
