The 15 mm word’s M and C keep 5 notches where their points fan. The 4 mm word runs along its letters’ middles, and its A’s counter, 0.56 mm across inside, isn’t kept open.

Smooth satin edges

M13.58 built this section’s rules (REQ-AUTO-170 to REQ-AUTO-173, REQ-SAT-030). Before it, a column moved its crowded points in as it was made (REQ-AUTO-071, retired), which is now the crowded value of auto_short_stitches.

The owner reviewed 18 simple drawings sewn by automatic digitizing on 2026-11-21. On most of them the biggest fault was the small stitches. On the inside of a curve every other stitch of a satin column ends short of the edge, and the short ends sew a second row inside it. Her words: “it just feels like the stitch needs to be longer so instead of having kind of two rows, you just have one”. How thick a design sews stays her first concern, with the warning’s limit of 4 fills.

Crowding on the inside of a bend

A column places its pairs of needle points the zigzag spacing apart at the outside of a bend, as Ink/Stitch does (Top stitches). On the inside they lie closer, by the ratio of the 2 radii. A straight column’s points lie 0.4 mm apart, so a distance of 0.35 mm moves in the points of almost every curve. A ring 3 mm wide whose inside is 24 mm from its centre has inside points 0.353 mm apart, and 12 scattered stitches of it end short. On the 18 drawings 1,044 needle points move in. At 0.25 mm 208 do, and the owner found that better.

A looser distance alone breaks the limit on one test design. TS-13C’s digitized half then sews 4.11 fills at one spot, where 3.92 is its thickest today. The spot is on the inside edge of the face’s outline, a column 4.8 mm wide that curves gently: its inside points lie 0.316 mm apart and its outside points 0.41 mm. Hair lines cross it there, over the face’s fill and the base layer. The layers at that spot:

LayerFills
Base layer0.25
Face, fill1.00
Outline, satin column1.57
Hair, first line0.79
Hair, second line0.50

At 0.35 mm the outline’s inside points there move in, and the spot stays under 4. At 0.25 mm they don’t. The cap’s last resort (REQ-AUTO-112) can take a column’s point out only by joining its 2 stitches into one along the far rail, and here no point passes its test of cover. With no point moved in at all, 4 of the 16 designs of REQ-AUTO-073 sew above 4 fills. TS-13A’s blue strip fans 25 stitches into a corner and lays 4.32 fills by itself, 6.05 with the gold fills and the base layer under it. The last resort took 575 mm of its top stitches there and left the corner at 6.05. The running dog in 6 threads reaches 4.1 fills at 0.20 mm where a column of 1.5 fills crosses a fill of 1.2 fills and 3 more layers.

So the stitches that crowd matter only where the layers under and over them reach the limit. TS-13C’s column crowds in a gentle curve, and TS-13A’s in a fan.

Rules for smooth edges

  • Spacing on the inside (REQ-AUTO-170). An automatic column measures the spacing between its pairs on the inside of a bend, and lets the outside open to 1.25 times the spacing, 0.5 mm, before the inside crowds. At 0.5 mm the outside’s stitches lie 1.25 thread widths apart, the cover of a column sewn at a spacing of 0.5 mm along that edge. A bend whose inside radius is 0.8 of its outside’s or more sews with no crowding at all, and a tighter one crowds by the ratio of its radii over 1.25.
  • Every stitch runs full length as it is made (REQ-AUTO-170). An automatic column moves no needle point in: digitizing sets its short_stitch_distance_mm to 0, and its short_stitch_inset to 25 50, the first held as REQ-AUTO-085 holds it, which is what the cap pulls a point in by.
  • Thickness decides where an end moves in (REQ-AUTO-171). Where a spot still sews above the warning’s limit once the cap’s 3 kinds are done, the cap’s last resort first pulls in crowded points of the columns there that move no point in as they are made. A point is crowded when it lies closer than its column’s spacing and 5 % to its rail’s point before or after it: 0.42 mm at the spacing of 0.4 mm. The inside of a bend keeps its points the spacing apart within 5 %, so this takes in every point there. At 0.35 mm, 2 traced dogs with their details sewn last sewed 4.12 and 4.04 fills, and at the spacing itself one still sewed 4.04, where both sewed under 4 before: their points lay 0.41 mm apart, and none moved. A point moves in along its 2 stitches, towards the middle of their other ends, from where it first was, by its column’s first inset and then by its second. Of those that lower the thickest spot, the one that lowers it most moves first, one at a time. A point whose 2 neighbours on its rail have both moved stays. The edge keeps every other stitch. A lettering column’s points stay, and a column that pairs evenly moves a point no farther past its edge than REQ-AUTO-085 holds it, since its first inset is held. It stops once no spot is above the limit or no point lowers it, and then the last resort takes points as REQ-AUTO-112 says.
  • SC-I1119 counts what moved (REQ-AUTO-172). The note says how many column points the cap pulled in, and where.
  • Today’s look is one setting away (REQ-AUTO-173). auto_short_stitches is above_limit by default. crowded gives today’s columns: the spacing kept on the outside, and a point closer than 0.35 mm moved in by 25 % and then 50 %, as REQ-AUTO-071 says. A shape that sets short_stitch_distance_mm or short_stitch_inset keeps its own, as before.

The bend’s spacing is a satin column’s own setting, bend_open_percent (REQ-SAT-030), 0 by default, which keeps Ink/Stitch’s spacing on the outside. Automatic digitizing sets 25 on its columns. A satin column a person draws, and lettering, keep 0. The rules are in these files:

RuleWhere
Spacing on the insidegenerators/satin/pairs.rs, the gap between 2 pairs
No point moved in as madeauto/mod.rs, satin_params
Crowded points pulled inauto/cap/pull.rs, before the cap’s last resort takes points
The columns it may pull inpipeline.rs, the automatic columns with short_stitch_distance_mm 0
The settingauto/params.rs, a rule of automatic digitizing

The cap moves needle points for the first time. Until now it only took them out. A moved point keeps its place in the run. The plan keeps its jumps, trims and thread changes (REQ-AUTO-102).

Smooth edges measured

Prototyped on 2026-11-21 on main 1952f48c (a patch kept outside the repository), on 4 sets:

  • the 18 review drawings,
  • the repository’s 29 SVG drawings in docs/fixtures,
  • the 4 traced dogs and the traced pear,
  • 6 traced photos outside the repository, of cats, geese, a building and a castle.

A needle point counts as shortened when the plan of the same rule with no point moved in has none there. The area above the cap counts pieces of 1 mm² or more at 3.25 fills or more:

A rail gap is the distance between a column’s needle point and the one before it on the same rail. The bare-fabric measure (fabric::unsewn) found the same bare share with every rule on every design. The cases of REQ-AUTO-085 pass with each rule: no notch in the patch’s letters or TS-13B’s words.

The costs of each rule:

  • (a) alone moves in 85 to 99 % fewer points, and every crowded column sews up to 4 fills where today’s 0.35 mm held it lower. The area above the cap doubles on the 16 designs, from 92 to 209 mm².
  • (b), 0.25 mm with a second plan, changes one column of TS-13C back to today’s look all along. It moves in more points than 0.25 mm alone, and it plans a design twice when a spot is above the limit.
  • (c) alone opens every bend’s outside. At 1.25 times it removes the crowding of gentle curves at its source, lays 1.2 % fewer stitches and less thread in bends. It needs (a), or a distance, where a fan crowds: TS-13A’s strip still sews 5.38 fills with no point moved in.
  • (c) and (a) move in 36 points on the 58 drawings, traced dogs and photos where today moves in 2,153. The area above the cap falls on the drawings and stays within 6 % on the dogs and photos. At 1.5 times the outside opens 2.8 times as often past 0.55 mm for 56 fewer points. At 1.1 times 66 more points move in.

Built (2026-11-22), the rules measure close to the prototype. On the 58 drawings, traced dogs and photos the cap pulled in 5, 22 and 5 points, 32 in all. The area above the cap is 66, 82 and 29 mm², where the prototype left 69, 79 and 29. The drawings sew 58,424 stitches, 1.2 % fewer than the 59,087 of before. The goose still sews 4.4 fills, as before, and no other design sews above 4 fills. Ten sew a tenth or 2 more at their thickest than before, under the limit: the shield, the photo cat and the 2 traced dogs in 6 threads 4.0, and the zigzag fixture 3.7 at its sharp inside corner. On the 16 designs of REQ-AUTO-073 no design sews above 4 fills. The patch’s lettering sews 3.95 fills at its thickest, where the prototype, which opened lettering’s bends too, sewed 3.97. TS-13D’s thickest spot, 3.97 fills as before, is on the inside edge of the ring round its star: the ring’s own top stitches lay 2.68 fills there, over the star’s fill and the base layer, and the cap pulled its points in. A disc with a dot on it is filled whole under the dot (REQ-AUTO-182) and sews 3.16 fills either way. Asked to sew as satin, its ring is a wheel round the dot and sews 3.98 fills, where it sews 3.70 with crowded. Planning TS-13A takes 9.5 % more instructions and TS-13D 4.7 % more, the work of pulling points in (conformance/perf.toml).

One test design shows a cost of (c). TS-13B’s thickest spot becomes a place where 2 fills meet, 3.46 fills with no column there, which the cap can’t lower and today’s 3.85 column spot hides. REQ-AUTO-073’s case lists it.

Decisions on smooth edges

  1. The rules above, by default, at 1.25 times: the fewest ends moved in, no spot above the limit, and less thread above the cap on the drawings than today.
  2. A setting for today’s look: auto_short_stitches crowded gives today’s look on a whole design, and a shape’s own short-stitch settings give it on one shape.
  3. A sew-out decides the 1.25: the test piece of MC-8’s kit (docs/src/plan/machine-testing.md) sews a mug’s handle, a ring and a sharp corner both ways.
  4. REQ-AUTO-071 retires once the rules are built. Its distance is kept as the crowded setting.

Letters sewn as strokes

Block letters and a logo’s bars are drawn in strokes: straight bands of nearly even width that meet at joins. An embroidery font sews each stroke as a satin column of its own, its stitches across it. The columns along a part’s centre lines run on round the corners where strokes meet. An M’s middle strokes took in the corners of its stems there, their rails ran on unevenly, and their stitches leaned up to 56°. TS-13B said so 6 times (SC-W0208). A part that reads as strokes now sews a column along each (REQ-AUTO-087), and where 2 strokes meet one runs through and the other stops (REQ-AUTO-088). The code is normalize/strips/strokes.rs and its joins strokes/joins.rs, called from auto/columns.rs.

Reading the strokes. Each ring of the part is cut into straight runs, from its sharpest corner: a run’s points lie within 0.05 mm of the line between its ends. 2 runs are a stroke’s sides when all of these hold. They run opposite ways within 5°, and lie as far apart at both ends within a tenth. They are at least the narrowest satin stroke and at most the widest satin column apart, and overlap along each other by half that width. The longer is as long as the width and the shorter half as long. The part fills the band between them but for 2 % of it. Sides on the same 2 lines, within 2° and a tenth of the width, are one stroke, as an E’s stem is between its arms.

Joins. 2 strokes meet where their bands, a width longer at each end, share more of the part than 0.02 of their widths’ product (0.01 mm² at least). A stroke runs through when the part goes on past the join by half its width both ways along it. Of 2 that both do, as in a cross, the longer runs through and the other loses its band, narrowed on each side by its stitches’ reach across, and sews in 2 columns. Of 2 that end there, the one whose end lies more nearly square to it runs through, by more than 0.15 of its width. An M’s stem has a square top and runs through, and its middle stroke, cut on the slant, stops. Alike, 2 strokes whose lines meet between 15° and 60° are cut along the line that halves the angle between them, as at the top of an A and the bottom of an M’s V, and each reaches max(0, reach / 2 - reach cos) past that line. There, reach is the stroke’s pull compensation and cos the cosine of its line to the halving line. At a blunter join, as at an L’s corner, the longer runs through. Cut along a halving line there, each would end in a point at the corner’s outside, and the 2 tips and their pull compensation piled 4.34 fills on TS-13D’s white frame, where the columns along their centre lines laid 4.17.

Where a stroke stops. A stroke that stops keeps its side of the other’s near side, moved into the other by reach (1 - cos), the cosine of the angle between the 2 lines: its own stitches’ pull compensation already reaches the rest across the line where they meet. That stub is part of the stroke’s end when it lies within the flat end’s 20° along the stroke, and it does for a stroke wider than reach / tan 20°, 1.1 mm at the default 0.4 mm. A wider stroke then ends on the other’s side, and the overlap is the other’s own pull compensation, whose stitches run along the stopping stroke at a square join and reach 0.4 mm past that side. Restored as rails of the stroke, the stub made TS-13D 4.55 fills thick where it is 4.23, the lettered patch 4.09 where it is 4.00 and TS-13B 3.90 where it is 3.85, and no measure showed a gap either way, so the stub is left out until a sew-out shows fabric where 2 strokes meet (MC-8). The stroke that stops sews first, and ends under the other without a push compensation.

Columns. Each stroke’s piece is the part inside its band, cut so. The pieces must cover the part but for 1 % of it. A piece’s rails are its 2 sides from its end least far along the stroke to its end farthest. An end whose points lie within tan 20° of the width along the stroke is flat, and any other ends in a point, as a column ends along an edge within 20° of square (REQ-AUTO-016). A rung lies square to the stroke at every corner of either rail. A flat end on the slant gets one more, past its corner farther in by twice the corners’ spread along the stroke, so that the stitches between pair no more than 1.5 of one rail with 1 of the other. A rung 0.1 mm past the corner notched the A’s feet. The middle line has a point every half width, so that a stroke’s thin tips measure as the centre lines’ do. A piece’s points within 0.001 mm of a corner of the part are that corner, so that a rail runs along a hole’s edge where the hole does (REQ-AUTO-084): without that the A’s counter fell to 7 %.

When a part keeps its centre lines’ columns. The rule runs only on a part that wasn’t cut from a broad one and reads as 2 strokes or more that meet. Its centre lines must give 2 columns or more, none of them a ring. Its columns are kept only when none of them runs (REQ-AUTO-016). Strokes are straight only: a curved stroke, as a C’s or an S’s, or a D’s bowl, keeps today’s columns, and curved strokes are a candidate for a later step.

Measuring the lean. A stitch leans by the angle between it and the square to its stroke, over the zigzag top stitches 0.4 mm long or more, leaving out the 2 at each end of a column. Those follow the column’s end. A flat end 19° on the slant measured 31° with the zigzag’s own 10° at 6 mm, which says nothing of how the column lies along its stroke.

Measured with the cases’ plans before (main at M13.36) and after, built at opt-level 1. Small Font’s M at 10 mm measures 3.71 fills and its A 3.31, alike:

DesignM’s most leanA’s most leanThickest, fillsAt 3 fills or more, mm²Jump threads

TS-13D’s one rise sits at the stacked emblem’s white ring, not on a stroke. The white frame now sews as strokes, with one bar through at each corner, and the ring’s column starts elsewhere. Its area at 3 fills or more fell by 16.5 mm². The emblems’ white frames, refused as columns before, sew as strokes, and TS-13B’s M no longer says that its stitches lean. The 4 mm word runs along its letters’ middles, as before. Every other test picture sews as it did. Taking the strokes from the centre lines instead fails: the M’s middle stroke is free for only 1.5 widths, too short to fit a line to.

A column’s ends

The owner reviewed 18 drawings sewn by automatic digitizing on 2026-11-21. Of the sunrise she wrote “main issues are the blue bottom band”. That band is 5 mm tall, and a circle cuts its ends. It sews as a column whose stitches spread like a fan at both ends, over the border beside it. Of the mug she wrote “biggest issues is handle”, a curved band that meets the cup. M13.60 measured every end of every automatic column, to find whether a column’s stitches can lie square to it up to its end and stop at the shape’s own edge.

A column’s end today

The set has 63 designs. Besides the 18 review drawings, it holds the repository’s 28 SVG fixtures and 6 traced documents (the 4 dogs, the pear and the round patch), 6 traced photos kept locally, and the digitized halves of TS-13A to TS-13E. At each end, the stitches whose middles lie within one column width of it are measured. A stitch’s lean is the angle between the line from one needle point to the point after next and a line square to the column’s way, which is the mean of its 2 rails’ ways beside the stitch. Taken over 2 stitches, the satin’s own zigzag cancels, and a square column leans 0. An end’s kind comes from its outline: square, slanted (more than 20° from square), curved, round, pointed, or a column shorter than 3 widths, which is mostly a traced fleck. Needle points are measured against the drawn shapes of the column’s colour, less how far past its rails the column sews in its middle.

Kind of endEndsMedian of each end’s most leanEnds with a stitch over 20°The same with (a), below
Square1844.8°8 %4.8°, 8 %
Pointed, a taper to a point4410.7°9 %11.0°, 11 %
Round3815.8°34 %15.8°, 34 %
Slanted, the edge straight3728.5°62 %18.9°, 50 %
Curved, as a circle cuts it2124.3°67 %20.0°, 48 %
A column shorter than 3 widths3414.4°32 %13.3°, 27 %

What lies past an end, nothing, another colour or a shape of the same colour, changes these little. No end leaves fabric bare: 0.04 % of the area within a width of the ends on average. No needle point lies more than 0.7 mm beyond the drawn shape past the column’s own reach in its middle, and 21 ends of 358 reach 0.2 mm or more farther. 17 of them are traced pictures and sheets, and 4 are round-capped lines of the drawings, whose columns end square past the line’s end, as REQ-AUTO-003 says. (a) moves none of them.

The fans come from how a part’s column ends (REQ-AUTO-016, centre-line columns). An end edge more than 20° from square ends at its farthest corner, so one rail runs on along the end edge to meet the other there. The rung nearest that end runs square to the mean of its 2 rails’ ways, and one of them is the end edge. The rung leans by half the end’s slant, and the stitches between it and the corner spread like a fan. Each of the sunrise band’s 25 stitches within 5 mm of an end leans more than 20°, up to 37°. Their ends lie 0.4 mm, the band’s pull compensation, over the border ring, which sews before the band. A band with a 45° end leans 32° there.

The mug’s handle is a stroke whose square ends lie 1 mm inside the cup. It sews before the cup, which covers its ends, and they lean 8° to 9°, as its middle does. What looks ragged where it meets the cup is the comb of short stitches along the inside of its bend, which M13.58’s step is about.

Corners of drawn lines belong here too. The review set’s zigzag, a line 1.5 mm wide with sharp corners, sews each corner as one column turning: its stitches fan round the inner corner, up to 57.7° along the line, and SC-W0208 names 6 stretches, 2 at each corner. Those are 6 of the 14 SC-W0208 of the whole set. A line drawn with round joins, the shield’s edge, turns its 90° corner as a bend and isn’t warned of.

Each candidate was prototyped and the whole set planned with it. The rows count the 324 ends of columns longer than 3 widths and the set’s 63 designs.

  • (a) Square up to the end. At an end whose last stretch runs straight, both rails within 20° of the column’s way for one width before it, the end’s slanted or curved edge becomes part of the rail that stops first, and rungs square to the way every 0.5 mm replace the column’s own rungs from where either rail leaves the way to the end’s farthest corner. The stitches lie square and grow shorter into the corner. The way at the end is the mean of the 2 rails’ ways over their last half width, past an end edge folded into one rail. The sunrise’s band leans 6.8° at most where it leaned 37°, a 45° end 9.6° where it leaned 32°, and no needle point lies farther past the drawn shape than before. Square ends, tapers to a point and columns that bend into their ends are left as they are: a letter C cut along its radii keeps its stitches. Most slanted ends of the traced dogs and photos are left too, since their columns wander before their ends.
  • (b) Run under the shape of one’s colour. Of the ends that meet a shape of their own colour, those of drawn shapes lean 3° to 9°. A part’s column ends under the column it meets by its pull compensation (REQ-AUTO-016), and a split shape’s columns sew after its fill and reach over it (REQ-AUTO-019). Nothing measured is left for (b) to mend, so it isn’t proposed.
  • (c) A cap along a slanted end. With (a) the fabric at an end doesn’t show, so a cap isn’t needed.
  • (d) Mitred corners. A line whose centre line turns by more than 60° at a corner drawn sharp, the bound a part’s lines are cut at (centre-line columns), sews as one column per leg, each ending on the corner’s bisector, from its inner corner to the miter’s point, and squared as (a) squares a slanted end. A line narrow enough to walk its middle once (REQ-AUTO-075) walks its whole centre line first and then sews the legs’ tops back from its far end, so that no travel stitch crosses a leg. The zigzag doesn’t give SC-W0208, and a needle point lies on each miter’s point, where today the nearest lies 0.23 to 0.37 mm short of it.

Measured with M13.59’s prototype, its shapes that swell and taper filled whole, only 3 pointed ends of the drawings stop being columns: the lake, a leaf and the crescent’s tips. The sunrise’s band stays a column, so each finding above holds with M13.59. M13.58 changes how a column spaces its stitches in a bend, which (a) and (d) don’t touch.

What gets worse

  • With (a), 2 ends of traced pictures lean more: one of the running dog’s (39° to 53°) and one fleck of a photographed cat. The area above the cap rises by 1.0 mm² on TS-13C and 0.75 mm² on a dog, and a cat’s thickest spot from 3.91 to 3.94 fills.
  • With (d), the zigzag’s thickest spot rises from 2.36 to 2.81 fills at its corners, under the cap, and it sews 43 stitches more. The 2 legs’ needle points meet along each bisector, where a sew-out must show whether a seam opens.

The pictures looked at (the band, the zigzag, the letter C, a dog) show the fans gone. They still show the band 1.5 mm tall above the sunrise’s 5 mm one fanning by 2 stitches, since its end is too short for a straight stretch, and a short stitch of each leg crossing at the zigzag’s lower points.

What is built

The owner chose (a) and (d) on 2026-11-22, as rules with no setting (REQ-AUTO-177, REQ-AUTO-178, stitchcraft_engine::auto::ends). A setting would only keep the fan. A shape that should sew otherwise can be expanded and drawn by hand (Expand).

  • Square ends. Each end of a part’s column that isn’t a ring is squared as (a) says, before the column is drawn with its rails and rungs. An end edge within 20° of square stays the column’s end, the bound at which a part’s column ends along an edge, and its stitches lean with it. The sunrise’s band 1.5 mm tall ends so: a circle cuts it 15° from square, and its stitches lean 15° and 17°, none over 20°.
  • Each end on the rails as they come. Each end is measured on the column’s rails as they come, and the end edge one end joins to a rail is no corner of the other’s. A corner of a rail is an end edge folded into it only when it lies nearer that end than the rail’s other end. Measured one end after the other, a short column’s start took its finish’s joined edge for its own fold or for a bend: a leg of a line 5 mm wide leaned 43.5° at its start, and the middle leg 10 mm long of a U-shaped line 5 mm wide leaned 41°. The running dog’s end that leaned 53° in phase 1 now leans as on main.
  • Mitred legs. An open stroke drawn with straight segments and a miter join, whose rails pair its points one for one, is cut at each corner turning more than 60°. Each leg is a column of its own, named after the subpath with ~leg1, ~leg2 and so on from its start. A line that walks its middle once sews that walk as a running stitch under the subpath’s own name, then its legs’ tops from the far end back with no underlay. A wider line sews its legs from its start, one after the other, with an underlay for each, and the next leg starts where the last ended. Curves, closed lines and corners whose miter its limit cuts square keep one column.

Measured on the 63 designs again with the build, on main with M13.58’s smooth satin edges and with both rules:

Drawn lines 3 mm and 5 mm wide, turning 60°, 90° and 120°, were added to the set. A turn of 60° keeps one column. At 90° and 120°, main sews each corner as one column whose stitches are no longer shortened, up to 3.78 fills thick and with 0.5 to 1.0 mm² of fabric showing within 3 mm of the corner. Mitred, no fabric shows there, the thickest spot is 2.95 to 3.95 fills, and the area above the cap falls from 5.75 to 1.0 mm². A turn of 60° keeps its knot at the inside of the corner: 3.83 fills on the line 5 mm wide, where it was 2.63 before M13.58. A parallelogram slanted 45° at both ends leans 9° and 13° where it leaned 32° and 34°. A middle leg 1.2 to 2 widths long between 2 sharp corners leans 3.7° at most, as the mean of 2 stitches.

At the zigzag’s inner corners the 2 legs’ stitches meet: 13 or 14 stitches have their middles within 1 mm of each, against 5 to 7 when it sewed as one column. The shortest stitch there is 2.1 mm, and the zigzag’s shortest is 0.41 mm, longer than the machine’s 0.3 mm. Its needle points lie 0.03 to 0.09 mm past each miter’s point, against 0.2 to 0.37 mm as one column. Its thickest spot is 2.81 fills, where main with M13.58 sews 3.50.

What got worse: the sunrise’s thickest spot rose from 3.91 to 3.98 fills, though its area above the cap fell from 7.0 to 5.25 mm². The photographed cat’s area above the cap rose by 0.5 mm². A column 0.59 mm wide of a photographed building reaches 0.2 mm farther past its curved end, and a fleck of the cat 0.73 mm wide has 5 stitches over 20° where it had 2, its most lean the same. The rules leave curves as they are, and the wavy zigzag fixture’s cusp sews 3.73 fills as on main. The sew-out at the next machine checkpoint sews the bands and lines as main plans them and with M13.60 (machine testing).

A column’s point

The owner reviewed 18 drawings sewn by automatic digitizing on 2026-11-21. Among the biggest issues she named “the points for the rays”. The sunrise’s 7 rays are wedges 2.2 mm wide at their base and 14.7° at their tip, and each sews as a satin column that ends blunt. M13.62 measured every column end that a drawing brings to a point, and prototyped ways to sew it as a point without sewing thicker.

A column’s point today

An end is a point by the column’s rails as drawn:

  • The rails meet. The chord across the end is under 0.3 mm or a quarter of the column’s median width.
  • Neither rail has an end edge folded into it, as a column’s ends finds one.
  • The 2 rails don’t both turn by 60° or more within 3 widths of the end, which makes a round end.

The point’s angle is the angle between the rails over their last 1 mm. Along the line from the tip to the middle of the rails 2 mm in, the drawn width and the sewn width are measured 0.25, 0.5, 1 and 2 mm from the tip. The sewn width is the spread across of the column’s top stitches clipped to a band 0.2 mm each side of the line, half the zigzag spacing. Only the zigzag’s diagonals cross the line itself.

The set is the review set’s 18 drawings, the repository’s fixtures and traced documents, 6 traced photos kept locally and TS-13A to TS-13E, 63 designs. It has 36 pointed ends. 29 of them show, on fabric or on another colour. 7 join a shape of their own colour, the mitred legs of a line among them. The rays’ angle is 14.7°, the traced dogs’ points 27° to 85°. The photos have none.

Distance from the tip0.25 mm0.5 mm1 mm2 mm
A ray, drawn0.060.130.260.52
A ray, sewn0.85 to 0.900.90 to 0.961.06 to 1.111.31 to 1.37
The 29 points, sewn wider than drawn, median0.840.770.820.79

Each point sews about 0.8 mm wider than drawn at every distance. That is the automatic column’s pull compensation, 0.4 mm on each side (auto_pull_compensation_mm), which every pair of needle points gets whatever its width. The pair at the tip has no length and stays a point. The next pair lies about 0.2 mm in, widened to 0.85 mm. The column ends in a bar with one stitch out to the tip. The other parts of an end don’t make it blunt:

  • No stitch near a tip is shorter than the machine’s 0.3 mm. The stitches into a tip are 0.41 to 1.56 mm long, since the compensation makes each pair long.
  • A column that starts at its point sews its lock there, 8 needle points within 1 mm of a ray’s tip. A column that ends at its point sews the end’s lock there.
  • The centre walk of a narrow column stops 0.6 mm short of a tip it starts from.
  • A column’s ends squares slanted and curved ends and leaves points as they are.

The points lie on other thread. Within 1 mm of a tip the thickest spot sews 1.7 to 3.95 fills, 3.07 at the median, most of it the fill or overlap under the column.

A hold on the compensation and the cap

M13.59 first limited each side’s pull compensation to 40 % of the stitch’s width. It made 5 designs’ thickest spots rise by up to 0.09 fills and a traced dog with its details sewn last sew 4.008 fills (req_auto_103, release only, which runs at opt-level 1 with --include-ignored). A held stitch is a part of today’s stitch. The hold lays less thread at a point. Measured with the cap and the warning set to 9 fills, so that the cap takes nothing, the dog’s spot sews 5.069 fills on main and 5.040 held. Its neighbour 0.5 mm away sews 4.915 and 4.482, since the pointed column there lays 0.56 fills of thread where it laid 1.00. The cap then runs as usual. On main it took the shared column’s top points down to 1.10 fills to bring the neighbour under 4, which held the first spot at 3.918. Held, the neighbour needed less, the cap took that column only to 1.33 fills, and the first spot stayed at 4.008. The other rises are the same: the cap is greedy and takes a column’s top points only above 4 fills, so a point that lowers one spot changes where the cap stops at another. A rule at points has to be measured after the cap, on the release-only dog cases too.

The candidates, measured

Each candidate was prototyped and planned on the set. The rows give the 29 points that show and the 57 designs other than the photos. A point’s excess is its sewn width less its drawn width.

  • (a) Pull compensation that tapers with the column. Held to a share of the width, 40 % as M13.59 had it, the compensation falls at every column narrower than 1 mm, and a point sews 1.8 times as wide as drawn. Held to 20 % it changes every column under 2 mm, and 6 designs’ thickest spots rise by up to 0.16 fills. Tapered by distance, each side’s compensation is the full 0.4 mm times the pair’s distance from a tip over a length. Columns change in the last stretch before a point and nowhere else. Over 2 mm a ray sews 0.30 to 0.40 mm wide 0.5 mm from its tip, where it sewed 0.90 to 0.96, and 0.66 to 0.78 mm 1 mm in, where it sewed 1.06 to 1.11. The across stitch 0.2 mm from the tip falls under 0.3 mm, and the engine leaves it out, so the tip is one stitch to the pair 0.6 mm in. Over 1 mm the point sews twice as wide as over 2 mm, and a dog reaches 4.000 fills.
  • (b) A run to the tip. The column stops where the drawing is 0.5 mm wide and a running stitch goes on to the tip. The column’s blunt end moves 1 to 2 mm back from the tip, and up to 7.5 % of the fabric within 1 mm of a tip shows.
  • (c) Fewer, longer stitches into the tip. Where the drawing is narrower than 0.5 mm, the pairs lie the spacing times 0.5 mm over the width apart, 1.5 mm at most. The pairs keep their compensation, so the end stays as wide, with fewer stitches. Needle points within 1 mm of a tip fall from 208 to 95, and fabric shows. With the taper the engine’s shortest stitch already drops the across stitches under 0.3 mm, so (c) adds nothing that (a) needs.
  • (d) A test by symmetry. Telling a point from a slanted end by how far each rail turns from the column’s way agreed with M13.60’s kinds at 9 of 14 traced ends that meet, since traced outlines wander. Every end whose rails meet is a drawn point of the shape, a slanted end’s farthest corner as well, so the taper applies at each, and M13.60’s cases pass with it.

The build, measured again on main with M13.57’s columns that reach 0.4 mm under a later satin column, sews the set as the prototype did. With the taper over 2 mm, on each set:

  • The 64 designs planned by the command line, the wedges of the sew-out piece among them: the area above the cap falls from 1,208.75 to 1,197.0 mm², the photos’ from 28.75 to 25.5, and 25 of 319,181 stitches go. The 38 points that show sew 0.17 mm wider than drawn 0.5 mm from their tips at the median, where they sewed 0.78. The bird’s 4.37 fills is main’s own.
  • The engine’s 16 designs’ area above the cap falls from 104.5 to 100.25 mm². The 2 badges rise from 3.96 to 3.99 fills, TS-13A from 3.95 to 3.99.
  • 18 sheets of traced letters sew 572.0 mm² above the cap, where they sewed 571.5. One sheet’s thickest spot rises from 3.97 to 3.99 and 2 fall. The 2 above 4 are main’s own, at 4.18 and 4.44.
  • The release-only dog cases, run at opt-level 1: the area at 3 fills falls from 50.25, 64.0, 52.75 and 91.0 mm² to 49.5, 60.5, 49.0 and 84.75. The thickest spots move from 3.99, 3.91, 3.92 and 3.99 to 3.96, 3.91, 3.99 and 3.98. With details last (req_auto_103) no dog sews above 4.
  • On the command line’s set the mug rises from 3.33 to 3.47 fills, its base layer’s route doubled at a spot, and the running dog in 6 threads from 3.92 to 3.99. No design sews above 4 that sewed at 4 or under.
  • The cases of a column’s ends, the traced letters’ (REQ-AUTO-085), the shapes that swell and taper and the small details pass. TS-13A, TS-13B and TS-13C sew their points narrower, and TS-13B sews 3 stitches fewer.

The pictures looked at (the sunrise’s rays, the wedges, the mug’s saucer, the rules’ leaf, TS-13B’s m, a dog) show each point drawn to its tip. The travel stitches across the sunrise’s sky are as on main.

What is built

The owner chose the taper over 2 mm on 2026-11-22, as a default with a setting (REQ-SAT-032, REQ-AUTO-183):

  • A satin column has point_taper_mm, a setting of StitchCraft’s own, 0 by default, one length or one for each end (satin columns).
  • Automatic digitizing sets it on each satin column from auto_point_taper_mm, 2 mm by default, unless the shape sets it. A person who wants the blunt end of before sets auto_point_taper_mm to 0, for a design with --set or for one shape in its settings. At 0 every pair is widened by the whole compensation and a point ends blunt.

With a rule and no setting, a person couldn’t sew the old end if the sew-out shows a point that pulls in. A setting off by default would leave the rays blunt.

The build sews the set as the prototype did, design by design. A column’s lock at a point follows its last stitches back into the column (locks). Of the 18 points that sew a lock within 1.5 mm of their tip, every lock needle point lies within 0.06 mm of the drawn shape, where on main they lay up to 0.30 mm outside it. A leaf 12 mm long and 3 mm wide sews its locks 0.04 mm outside its outline at most, against 0.21 mm at 0. A lock past a narrowed tip would show as a knot. What looks like one in the pictures at the leaf’s and the saucer’s tips is the last stitch into the tip with the lock on it, inside the shape.

Open points

  • The cap’s greed. Several thickest spots now rest just under the warning’s limit: the running dog in 6 threads at 3.99 fills, the 2 badges and TS-13A at 3.99, a sheet of letters at 3.99. The area above the cap falls on every set. The cap takes a column’s top points only above 4 fills, one spot after another, so a point that lowers one spot changes where it stops at another (a hold on the compensation and the cap, above). A cap that looks at the spots it leaves just under 4 would settle these. That is a step of its own.
  • The mug’s base layer. Its thickest spot rises from 3.33 to 3.47 fills, where its base layer runs twice over a spot. The base layer’s route changes with the saucer’s narrower tips.
  • Wider points. Wedges wider than about 22° at their tip are filled with their outline by the rule uneven, and their points are drawn by the fill’s outline. Only narrower wedges and the tapering ends of longer parts are columns with points.

Neighbours

The shapes of a traced picture meet. VectorCraft’s Abutting method traces each area with a hole where the next one lies, and its Overlapping method, like Inkscape’s Trace Bitmap with its Stack option, traces each colour whole, over the colours under it. Sewn as drawn, either sews badly where shapes meet, and digitizing mends it in 5 steps, in this order (stitchcraft_engine::auto::neighbours, M13.2, M13.12 and M13.18).

  1. Background (REQ-AUTO-009). The lowest shape is the picture’s background when it is an automatic area with a shape on another path drawn on it, and its outlines cover the box around the design to within 0.5 mm of each side, its holes and notches aside. The box is the one around the defining points of every shape’s path, the background’s own included. So a disc in the corner of a square, lowest but covering less of the box, is part of the picture. A shape on its own path is the same object’s: the readers make a fill and its stroke 2 elements on one path, and a design of one shape with a stroke sews it. Where a shape of a tightly cropped picture touches its edge, the Abutting method cuts a notch in the background instead of a hole. A part of the box its outlines leave is a notch when the areas drawn on it fill it, to within twice the shapes’ tolerance, and it runs along the outlines at least as far as along the box’s sides, measured 0.5 mm inside them. A part that runs further along the sides is no notch. A disc or a rounded square that reaches the sides leaves such parts in its corners, and sews as the picture, though the shapes in its corners fill the rest. The cost of this rule is a background cut away along a whole side, by a bar down the side say, or cut in two by a band from side to side, which is sewn. The background is left out with SC-W0906 before anything is digitized. With a StitchCraft effect, it sews as that effect says. Finding it has the work budget to itself, and a background that needs more is sewn, which SC-W0906 says. What shows of the background inside the picture once the layers are flattened (step 2) is sewn: its parts that come no nearer than 0.5 mm to the box’s sides. A trace that stacks its colours draws the background whole under the picture, a white disc on a badge included, which a trace whose colours abut draws as a shape of its own. Such a part sews as the background’s own area, and SC-W0906 counts it.

  2. Layers (REQ-AUTO-023, M13.12). Digitized as drawn, a stacked colour’s stitches would be chosen for its whole shape, though most of it is hidden. Taken as it shows, a broad area with details on it splits into narrow columns between them, traced either way, as a flower’s middle does round its dots. A digitizer fills such an area whole and sews the details on top. So before anything is digitized, the layers are flattened, in 3 parts (auto::neighbours::layers).

    • What shows. The automatic areas drawn after an area are taken from it where they overlap it by more than twice the shapes’ tolerance, as the order measures an overlap (Sewing order). Rounding leaves hairlines where edges drawn as one meet, as round a disc drawn a hair larger than the disc on it. A part of what is left that is narrower than twice the shapes’ tolerance everywhere, measured as a narrow part is, is such a hairline and is dropped. An area that they cover whole sews nothing (SC-I0904), and so does one of which only hairlines are left.
    • Details. Where what shows of an area is wider than its widest satin column somewhere (auto_max_satin_width_mm), the area is digitized whole under its details, the automatic areas that fill one of its holes. They fill the hole when what they leave of it is no wider than twice the shapes’ tolerance, or smaller than the area’s smallest area. The hole is closed, and the area’s stitches are chosen by its width with the hole closed (Stitch types by width). An area, or a part of one, no wider than a satin column everywhere, such as a letter or an outline, keeps its holes, and the shapes in them sew beside it. Closed, it would be a broad fill under them. Specks in a hole aren’t details. When only specks fill a hole, the hole is left open, and the specks merge into the area round them (step 4), which closes it. A hole is filled from the parts whose boxes meet its own, and costs the work of those parts alone.
    • Moved back. The area sews before its details, so that they lie on it. It moves back in paint order past them, past the automatic areas whose part that shows doesn’t overlap it, and past the other shapes whose boxes don’t come within twice the shapes’ tolerance of its box. It never moves across a stop, nor when it has a stop after it. The areas move the largest first, so that an area moves before the areas inside it. Once all have moved, a hole whose details don’t all sew after its area is left open: sewn after them, the area would cover them. The area couldn’t move back past them, or another area that has one of them for a detail moved back past it. On a red square with a white ring on it and the red showing again as a bullseye in the ring’s hole, the ring moves back before the red, the bullseye being its detail, and the red’s hole is opened again round the ring.

    The overlaps (step 5) then cut the area away under its details, the overlap inside their edges. So a trace that stacks its colours sews as one whose colours abut. An area whose region changed is given its new outline, and one whose region is what it was keeps its path as drawn. SC-I0908 counts the areas that sew only what shows of them and those digitized whole under their details.

    Each area has the work budget to itself at each step of the flattening, and again when it is given what it sews. It costs a unit for each shape and each part it looks past, and the work of those near it. An area that needs more work for a step does without it. One that needs more to find what shows of it is sewn as it is drawn, in its place, but for the background, which is left out whole. The other areas’ holes are found without it. When finding an area’s details needs more work, its holes are kept open. When its move does, it keeps its place, and the holes whose details then sew before it are opened again. When giving it its region does, it is sewn as it is drawn, and isn’t counted as digitized whole. SC-I0908 names each. SC-E0009 names an area whose region the region code fails to join, a bug, which is treated the same way.

    The test pictures (Testing) sew alike traced either way. An emblem 60 mm wide, a navy square with a white disc, a red star on the disc and 3 gold bars, on white, sews the same shapes with each thread. The navy square is one tatami fill of 1,848.5 mm², sewn first. The white disc is one fill of 380.7 mm² as its layers are flattened, sewn after it: the disc’s 531 mm² less the star, which it then reaches under. The bars sew as satin columns 3.00 mm wide, as drawn, and the star as one fill. The stitches of the 2 traces differ by about 1 %, because the traces draw their colours in different orders. A button, a yellow disc 24 mm across with 7 orange dots in a black rim 2.5 mm wide, sews the same shapes with each thread either way, and as many stitches, 1,925, 1,845 of them in the same places. Its yellow is one fill of 445.9 mm² under the dots, each dot a fill too, and its rim a satin column 2.50 mm wide. tests/auto_pictures.rs keeps the pictures sewing so.

  3. Thin lines (REQ-AUTO-090, M13.18). A trace of a photo draws soft shading and the strands of fur as thin lines between patches. Each sewed as a running stitch between 2 fills, which ended their rows on it: the traced dogs sewed 15 to 34 shapes so, and those lines caused most of their stitches astray, their slivers and their jump threads. A digitizer sews such a strand as part of the patch it lies in. So once the layers are flattened, and before the stitches of any area are chosen, a part of an automatic area that is narrower than the narrowest satin stroke everywhere, and is no speck, is a thin line when its width varies along its centre lines by auto_min_line_unevenness of its width or more, measured as a column’s unevenness is (The measures). It merges into the automatic area it shares the longest edge with, which may be the rest of its own area, the edge measured as a speck’s is (step 4). Every area gives up its thin lines first. A line then goes into what its neighbours keep. On a tie it goes into the area that sews first. A thin line that doesn’t touch an automatic area sews as a line. The thin lines of a shape that turns auto_fill_uneven off sew as lines, as with the logo style, and so do those of a shape that names its stitch. An area whose every part merges sews nothing. SC-I0915 names each line merged, by its area, and where it went (auto::neighbours::lines). The bound of 0.05 is StitchCraft’s own. The dogs’ 80 thin lines vary by 0.09 to 0.46 of their width, and TS-13B’s window lines, drawn with an even pen 0.3 and 0.6 mm wide, by 0.01. The rule goes by the line’s width and how it varies, never by what the picture shows. Each area has the work budget to itself to find its thin lines, and each line to find where it goes. One that needs more keeps its lines, and SC-I0915 says so. The measure of thread astray paints the picture with each line merged in its neighbour’s colour (auto::as_merged), since the neighbour’s thread covers it by the rule.

    Lines merge only while the picture keeps its likeness (REQ-AUTO-095, M13.44, auto::neighbours::lines::parts). A line that doesn’t touch another piece of its colour is a part of the picture by itself, as the likeness measure counts parts, and merged into a neighbour of another colour it takes its colour away, as with a twig or the pale rim of a petal. The lines of one colour that touch each other are one group. A group that touches a piece of its colour that is no speck merges freely, since its colour still shows there. Any other group that is a part costs the picture’s likeness 1 of its colour’s parts, divided by the number of colours. The groups that share an edge with an area merge the cheapest first, those of the colour with the most parts, and of 2 as cheap the smaller, while their cost stays within auto_min_likeness of the picture’s likeness. The rest sew as lines, and SC-I0915 says so. A part is defined once, by auto::is_part, and the measure imports it. It is 0.85 by default since M13.45. At 0 nothing is measured and every line merges (Merging by likeness).

  4. Specks (REQ-AUTO-008). An automatic area, or a part of one, smaller than 1 mm² is too small to sew: it would sew a knot. It is merged into the automatic area it shares the longest edge with, which may be its own. A speck that fills a hole goes into the area around it, and no hole shows where it was. One that rounding left a hair from the rest of its own area rejoins that. On a tie, it goes into the area drawn first. The edge 2 shapes share is how much of the area’s rings lies within twice the shapes’ tolerance of the speck. A speck that doesn’t share an edge with an automatic area is left out, and so is one whose neighbours need more work to find than the budget allows it: put back as drawn, it could overlap a speck merged in beside it. SC-I0905 names each speck, by its area rounded to a hundredth of a square millimetre and kept under 1 mm², and where it went. A line filled because it is too wide for a column (M13.1) is an area too, so a part of it that small is a speck.

  5. Overlaps (REQ-AUTO-007). Pull draws stitches in from the edges of their shape, so shapes that only meet show fabric between them. Each automatic area reaches its overlap, 1 mm, under each area, satin column, filled line or part’s line or column sewn after it, from where it touches that shape, and no further than that shape as it is sewn. Under an area it reaches that far along its rows and less across them, half as far under a narrow part, and not where an area sewn before it already sews (Less thread where areas overlap). The areas are taken from the last sewn to the first, so each shape is taken as it is sewn: across a line narrower than the overlap, an area reaches on under the area beyond it. Shapes touch where their edges meet within twice the shapes’ tolerance, so a gap that rounding left between edges drawn as one closes too. Along an edge where a shape runs on near the area without touching it, the area doesn’t reach, and fabric shows between them as drawn, but for the overlap round the end of where they touch. A satin column covers the area between its rails as they are sewn, turned to run the same way. Where an area or a part’s line or column sewn after the area covers part of it, as the details on an area digitized whole do, the area is cut away under that shape, the overlap inside the shape’s edge, and that part is sewn once. Sewn whole, it would sew twice there, stiff and thick. A part of the area that such shapes cover whole sews nothing, however near their edge it lies. So does an area they cover whole. A trim or a stop after such an area has no place, as after any element that sews nothing (SC-W0505). SC-I0901 counts only the shapes that sew. A part’s line or column covers its part, and isn’t cut away itself. It sews nothing when the areas, and the lines and columns of other shapes’ parts, sewn after it cover the part whole, and it sews whole when they cover the part in places. A satin column or a line digitized from a stroke and sewn after an area goes over it, and the area is sewn whole under it. Cutting a fill round small letters adds stitches and can leave gaps (Inkscape forum). SC-I0904 counts the areas that reach under others and those cut away. It also names each area cut away whole, which sews nothing. The areas are named in the order they are sewn.

Only automatic areas change. A shape with settings is sewn as they say, and a satin column or a line digitized from a stroke or a part keeps its stitches: a column is sewn past its rails by its pull compensation, 0.4 mm (Settings by width), and by the overlap past a rail that a shape sewn after it runs along (Neighbours that overlap). A fill that bounds no area is left to its fill, which says so. The areas are taken from the last sewn to the first, so that each meets the shapes after it as they are sewn. Each area has the work budget to itself for its specks and for its overlaps, and costs the work of the shapes near it and a unit for each other shape it looks past, not the work of the whole design: what a shape sewn after it paints is found once, with the work budget to itself. An area whose overlaps need more keeps the region it has, and a speck that needs more is left out. SC-I0904 and SC-I0905 say so. A satin column whose area needs more is not reached under, which SC-I0904 says too. The regions are combined by one function, which cuts the rings of both where they meet and keeps the faces by their winding numbers (normalize::region::ops).

Measured. The test pictures (Testing) are sewn as drawn, each shape a tatami fill, and digitized, and the edges where their shapes meet are measured with the thread pulled in by 0.4 mm, the pull the overlap is there to make up for: the share of the edges that the top stitches’ thread covers once each is pulled in where the thread turns (edge coverage, stitchcraft_testkit::metrics).

Test pictureAs drawnDigitizedStitches as drawnStitches digitized
Stripes, 3 abutting98.9 %100 %2,9943,221
Badge, abutting89.1 %97.2 %3,7613,764
Badge, stacked100 %97.2 %3,9813,764
Badges in a row, abutting94.2 %99.9 %4,9474,867
Badges in a row, stacked100 %99.9 %4,8724,867

The abutting badge’s shapes, sewn as drawn, leave a tenth of their edges bare. Digitized, they overlap, and 97.2 % is covered. The stripes’ edges are covered as drawn too, but by the running stitches the fills travel on along them, under their rows, not by the rows: digitized, the rows themselves overlap. The stacked badge as drawn sews its lower colours whole under the upper ones, which covers its edges with 217 more stitches, 6 %, sewn twice. Digitized, it sews the abutting badge’s stitches, every one. The row of badges, each the badge at three fifths of its size and sewn in the order below, covers its edges as the badge does, and stacked it sews the abutting row’s stitches. Without pull, every edge is covered as drawn and digitized alike, to within a hundredth. tests/auto_pictures.rs keeps the numbers from falling back, and MC-8 tells whether 0.4 mm is the pull on the machine.

An outline round a fill

A tatami fill’s rows end at its edge, and the thread pulls each row’s end in. On a small shape with points, such as a star 5 mm across, the ends of the rows are most of what shows, and the points sew blunt. A digitizer runs a stitch round such a shape after filling it, which draws the edge as one line. auto_fill_outline does that for a design (M13.27, REQ-AUTO-051), read of its lowest automatic shape as the base layer’s settings are.

Each automatic area then sews a running stitch along its edge, in its own thread, in an element of its own straight after the area, named after it with #2. The edge is the area’s as the layers and the specks leave it, before the area reaches under its neighbours: what shows of it. Where a shape sewn after the area touches the edge, the area lies under that shape by the overlap, and a run along the edge would lie under that shape’s stitches and thicken them. So the outline leaves out the edge within auto::TOUCHING of each shape sewn after the area, and of each other element of the area’s own shape, whose columns and lines are the same shape going on. A line digitized from a stroke goes over an area, and hides no edge. The edge is cut into pieces no longer than 0.5 mm, and a piece shows when its middle lies in none of those shapes. The pieces that show in a row are one line, a ring where the whole edge shows. A line shorter than 3 mm is left out, since 2 stitches beside a neighbour’s edge sew a knot.

The setting is on by default since M13.28. M13.27 left it off, for the jumps it cost a traced photo. Shown the badge’s stars with and without it, the owner said that outlines were better all along. The Auto effect’s dialog has a row for it (Settings).

An outline’s stitches follow a round edge. A running stitch keeps to its path within its tolerance, which is 0.2 mm unless a shape sets it. Stitches 2.5 mm long stray 0.16 mm from a circle 9.6 mm across, so the badge’s moon was outlined with 12 sides. An outline has a tolerance of auto::OUTLINE_TOLERANCE, 0.1 mm, unless its shape sets running_stitch_tolerance_mm. Its stitches are then shorter where the edge bends, never shorter than the machine’s shortest stitch, and the moon has 18 sides.

An outline is sewn with as few jumps as its lines allow (REQ-AUTO-052). A traced area’s edge is hidden wherever a neighbour sewn later touches it, often for a few millimetres, and each hidden stretch parted 2 lines by a jump. The outline now sews through a hidden stretch of BRIDGE, 25 mm, or less, along the edge and under the shape that covers it, at a stitch for each 2.5 mm. The longest hidden stretch of a ring stays out, because sewing it would join nothing. Under a fill sewn after the area the limit is 12 mm (the outline under a later fill). Of an outline’s lines, the one with an end nearest where the next element starts sews last and ends there. An open line is turned round for that if need be, and a ring starts at its corner nearest that place.

What the outline costs, measured on 2026-11-15 with the setting off and on:

PictureStitchesJumpsThread changes
Alpine Night, the demo’s badge5443 to 557593

Dog on turf, 9 threads11158 to 1226191 to 9712
Running dog, 6 threads11330 to 1247979 to 828
Running dog, 9 threads13115 to 14417106 to 10912

An outline sews in its fill’s thread straight after the fill, and doesn’t change the thread. Before the 2 wins the traced dogs’ outlines brought 19 to 30 jumps each, and they bring 3 to 6 now. Sewing through every hidden stretch, whatever its length, brings 0 to 3, for 400 more stitches on each dog: 25 mm is where the stitches spent stop buying jumps. The jumps that are left come from a fill joined from 2 parts of one shape, whose 2 outlines each leave out the edge between the parts, from a ring and a line of one outline that lie far apart, and from hidden stretches longer than 25 mm. Each jump is a thread that a machine with one needle leaves to cut by hand. Since M13.17 the needle runs under the shapes sewn later wherever a hidden way exists (runs in place of jumps).

Covering the fabric

The fabric’s colour can’t be chosen. It may be black, so a design has to cover the fabric by itself. The measure of bare fabric (Sewing order, REQ-AUTO-025) passes a patch under 1 mm², and its disc needs 0.45 mm of bare fabric on the grid, so a sliver 0.4 mm wide between 2 shapes passes too. On the traced dog such slivers lay between strands of fur and beside the nose, and on dark fabric each was a dark line. M13.16 of the roadmap made the measure stricter, and built one underlay under the whole design and a wider overlap between neighbours to meet it (REQ-AUTO-027 to REQ-AUTO-029, REQ-RND-003).

The stricter measure

stitchcraft_testkit::fabric::cover paints the picture as the measure of bare fabric does (REQ-AUTO-027).

  • The design is what the picture paints, with the slits between its shapes that are narrower than a thread: the painted places closed with a disc 0.4 mm across. A hole a thread wide or wider stays fabric.
  • Its edge, the outermost 0.2 mm of the design, isn’t measured. A fill’s first row lies up to a row’s spacing inside the outline, and beyond the outline is fabric anyway.
  • Bare is the part of the rest that the top stitches don’t cover, and open the part that the thread of every role leaves uncovered. The base layer is underlay: it shows in a bare place, and it doesn’t make the place covered.
  • A gap is a place where a disc 0.35 mm across fits on bare places, 7 pixels of the grid. A strip of fabric 0.4 mm wide holds 7 or 8 pixels by where it lies, so this disc fits in every such strip, and the 9 pixels of the older measure’s disc fit in none.
  • The base layer lies under a place inside the shape that one of its pieces fills (below).
  • The rim is the band along the design’s outline that the base layer’s offset leaves without a base layer, 0.4 mm wide by default. A part of the design narrower than twice that is all rim.
  • A sliver is a gap no larger than auto_base_sliver_mm2, 0.4 mm², whose every place has the base layer under it or lies in the rim: its part without the base layer lies wholly within that band. Over the base layer its thread shows in place of the fabric. In the rim a sliver lies at the outline and reads as part of it.

A picture passes when no fabric shows inside its design, counting the base layer:

  • Every gap is a sliver. Fabric shows in a larger gap, and in one with a place further inside than the rim that the base layer isn’t under.
  • With a base layer, the top stitches leave no more than auto_base_bare_percent, 0.5 %, of the design bare: the slivers and the places narrower than a thread together.

A design without a base layer has nothing under a gap. It passes with no gap at all and with no more than 0.15 % of its design bare, twice what a picture without gaps leaves. A design that turns auto_base_layer off is told that fabric may show (SC-I0911). Cover::faults gives the verdict, by the settings of the design.

Measured on 2026-11-10 at 8b8b23bd, before the step, when a picture passed with no gap and 0.15 % bare:

The stripes failed on their bare share alone. Of the red stripe’s 160 rows only 93 had a needle point on its outer side, and the others turned 0.8 or 1 mm short of it, which left notches narrower than a thread along the 2 outer sides. A turn between 2 rows was a stitch shorter than the machine’s shortest and was dropped. Automatic fills now keep their turns as stitches (tatami fill, REQ-FILL-TAT-014).

No fabric shows on any of them. The test pictures, the stripes among them, and the dogs’ pieces don’t leave a gap. tests/auto_cover.rs holds their cases. The whole dogs are cases of tests/auto_photos.rs, which runs in the release build. That file records each dog’s row of this table, and its case fails when a number changes. A dog whose slivers grow then shows before it comes to a limit.

These are the numbers since M13.21, which fills the dogs’ ragged patches (which stitch a shape gets). The limits were set at M13.16, when the dogs left 19 slivers and 0.07 to 0.35 % of their designs bare. Those slivers lay beside thin strands of fur. A strand that sews as a running stitch is as wide as its thread and can’t reach under a neighbour, so the overlap can’t close the fabric beside it. A thin column’s end sewn further under its neighbour closed 4 of them and laid 19 more stitches astray, so it isn’t built (below). The owner’s decision of 2026-11-14 leaves a gap this small to the base layer. 3 of the 19 reached into the rim, each by no more than 0.42 mm from the outline. Both limits are StitchCraft’s own, set from the dogs: their slivers were 0.09 to 0.335 mm², and the dog on turf in 9 threads left the most bare. They are settings (Settings), and MC-8 confirms them on black fabric or changes them.

One underlay under the whole design

Ink/Stitch has this as its Knockdown Fill tool. Its documentation says that the tool can make a global underlay and that the offset is then usually negative (fill tools as read on 2026-11-10). What it does, read in lib/extensions/knockdown_fill.py at d59c9ab and told in our words (ADR-0012):

  • Outlines. It takes the area each selected element sews. For a fill that is its shape, grown by its expand, and for a satin column the area between its rails as sewn, its pull compensation included. A running stitch counts as a band 0.3 mm wide along its path, and a ripple as a band of that width along each line of its stitches. A zigzag stroke counts as a band as wide as it sews, and a clone as what its elements make.
  • The outer shape. It unites the outlines and offsets the union by the offset asked for, outwards when positive and inwards when negative. The join style, round or mitre or bevel, shapes the corners that the offset makes, with a mitre limit of 5 unless another is given, and never under 0.1. It then drops points that lie within 0.3 px, 0.08 mm, of the line between their neighbours.
  • Holes. Each piece of that shape becomes one path. With “keep holes”, on by default, the path has the piece’s holes, and otherwise only its outer ring.
  • Place. The paths go before the first selected element in drawing order, so they sew first.
  • Stitches. Each path is a fill whose row spacing follows the stitch length asked for, 3 mm by default: row_spacing_mm is the length times √3/2, written to 4 decimals, with max_stitch_length_mm the length and staggers 2. Its underlay sews at fill_underlay_angle “60 -60” with the same length and spacing, and underpath and underlay_underpath are off. Rows at 0°, 60° and −60° that far apart and staggered over 2 rows put the needle points on a lattice of triangles as long a side as the stitch.
  • It can also frame the selection with a rectangle or a circle that the selection is cut out of, for embossing. StitchCraft doesn’t take that.

Run as a black box at d59c9ab on small shapes drawn for it, it gives these numbers, which StitchCraft’s cases match where they share a setting:

StitchCraft’s base layer (REQ-AUTO-028) is that fill, made by automatic digitizing itself:

  1. When. A design with automatic shapes gets one, unless auto_base_layer is off. Shapes with settings are sewn as they say and aren’t part of it.
  2. The outer shape is the union of what is painted by every automatic element that sews, once the background is left out, the layers are flattened and the specks are merged (Neighbours). An area paints its region and a line the area its stroke covers. The union is closed by half a thread, grown by 0.2 mm and shrunk by as much with round joins, so that a slit narrower than a thread between 2 shapes is part of it, as it is part of the design that the measure reads.
  3. Offset and holes. The shape is offset by auto_base_offset_mm, −0.4 mm, with round joins. Ink/Stitch’s join styles differ only at the corners an offset makes, and a setting can follow when one is asked for. Its holes are kept unless auto_base_keep_holes is off. A piece smaller than the smallest area is left out, and so is one with no room for a row of the base layer’s stitches. A part of the design narrower than twice the inset has no base layer. It sews as a line or a column, and a column still sews the underlay of its width, which shapes its edges.
  4. Stitches. Each piece is a tatami fill with the parameters Ink/Stitch’s tool writes, at the stitch length auto_base_stitch_length_mm, 3 mm. StitchCraft’s tatami fill has each of them, so no stitch code is new, and the nightly differential can sew Ink/Stitch’s own knockdown path beside it.
  5. Underlay all through. Every stitch of the base layer has the underlay’s role, its top rows too. The measures go by a stitch’s role. They take a row that has the top’s role for cover, and they don’t find it astray where it shows.
  6. Thread. It is the thread that paints the largest area of the picture, the one painted first of 2 as large. On the pear, the dogs and the badges that thread is also the first one sewn, and the base layer doesn’t add a thread change. The digitized halves of TS-13A, C and D start in another thread and change thread once more.
  7. Order. It is the element stitchcraft:base, its pieces after the first named #2 and so on. It sews before every automatic element, at the place of the first one, and the order of Sewing order leaves it there.
  8. The fills on it sew no underlay of their own: digitizing turns their fill_underlay off. On the running dog in 9 threads the fills’ own underlays were 601 of its 11,996 stitches on 2026-11-10.
  9. Said. SC-I0909 says the base layer’s area, pieces and thread. A base layer whose shape needs more work than the budget allows is left out with SC-W0910, and every fill then keeps its underlay. A design that turns the base layer off is told that fabric may show (SC-I0911). That is information and no warning, since the design sews as its settings ask. M13.21’s specification planned that code for the message that says which rule chose a shape’s stitches, and it takes the next free one.

What it costs on the running dog in 9 threads, by Ink/Stitch’s counts above at an offset of −0.6 mm, and how much of a gap 3 directions of thread 0.4 mm wide would hide, as it sewed before M13.30 gave it one layer (Less thread where areas overlap):

Neighbours that overlap

Digitizing guides extend the colour sewn first under its neighbour by 1 to 1.5 mm on large areas and about 1 mm on small ones. mySewnet’s manual says that compensation doesn’t prevent gaps between neighbours, and that overlap does. The owner brought both to this step on 2026-11-09. Before it an area reached 0.4 mm under the shapes sewn after it, and a satin column sewed 0.4 mm past both rails, whatever lay beside it (Neighbours). REQ-AUTO-029 changed 3 things:

  1. auto_overlap_mm is 1 mm. An area reaches that far under each shape sewn after it, from where it touches it and no further than that shape, as today.
  2. A satin column sews under its neighbour too, by the rail. Along a rail where a shape sewn after the column runs for more than half the rail’s length, the column sews past that rail by the overlap. Ink/Stitch’s pull_compensation_mm takes one value for each rail, and Ink/Stitch can sew such a column.
  3. Pull compensation is a setting of its own, auto_pull_compensation_mm, 0.4 mm. A rail with no shape sewn after it beside it gets that. A column then doesn’t grow wider over a colour sewn before it or over fabric. A rail along a hole kept open has none (REQ-AUTO-084), and keeps none when the other rail sews past a shape: each rail keeps the length it had, one value for each.

An area is cut away under a shape sewn after it that covers part of it, the overlap inside that shape’s edge. A shape narrower than twice the overlap then lies on the area whole.

A column’s end under another column stays as long as it was: it reaches its pull compensation under that column, not the overlap. Sewn 1 mm longer, the ends of thin columns laid 19 more stitches astray on the dogs, and the end of a column 4.4 mm wide skewed its last stitches and left 3.2 mm² bare (the piece blaze of tests/auto_cover.rs). By the owner’s decision of 2026-11-14, the small gaps at such ends are left to the base layer.

Measured before the step, the setting as it then was bounded what the overlap could do. Raised, it also widened every satin column on both sides, which laid stitches astray, so these are not the step’s numbers:

Picture, digitized0.4 mm, today1 mm1.5 mm
Traced pearno gap, 0.083 % bareno gap, 0.074 %no gap, 0.072 %
Traced dog on turf, 6 threads12 gaps, 0.517 %no gap, 0.049 %2 gaps, 0.074 %
Traced dog on turf, 9 threads17 gaps, 0.773 %2 gaps, 0.136 %no gap, 0.046 %
Traced running dog, 6 threads7 gaps, 0.275 %3 gaps, 0.215 %no gap, 0.057 %
Traced running dog, 9 threads8 gaps, 0.322 %one gap, 0.070 %no gap, 0.041 %

At 1 mm the pictures sewed 2.2 to 4.7 % more stitches, and at 1.5 mm 3.9 to 9.5 % more. At 1 mm one gap of the running dog in 6 threads was new, 2.0 mm² at (40.9, 42.4): the skewed end of the blaze’s column.

Less thread where areas overlap

An overlap is thread under thread. M13.29 measured that the overlaps cause most of the thread where a traced picture sews thick (How thick a design sews), and the owner’s first concern on 2026-11-16 was that thickness. REQ-AUTO-067 (M13.30) takes away the part of an overlap that the pull doesn’t ask for (auto::neighbours::under):

  1. The overlap follows the rows. The thread pulls a row in at its ends and not sideways. So an area reaches its overlap, 1 mm, along its rows, and its pull compensation, 0.4 mm, across them. What it sews under a shape is every point of that shape within an ellipse with those half axes round a point where the 2 touch. Across its rows the overlap is never wider than along them, and without pull compensation it is 0.01 mm wide.
  2. Half as far under a narrow part. Under a part of an area that is narrower than 3 overlaps, the overlap is half: 0.5 mm along the rows, and across them no more than that. A part is narrow where no disc 3 overlaps across fits on the area. The areas on both sides of a part just under 3 mm wide then leave a third of it free, where 2 whole overlaps filled a part 2 mm wide between them. This is the owner’s idea of 2026-11-16, and digitizers work so by hand: one guide extends the lower colour 1.5 mm under bold areas, 1 mm under fine details and 0.3 to 0.5 mm under delicate lettering (Stitch Era’s magic wand, a retailer’s article).
  3. One overlap under a shape. Where 2 areas would both sew under a shape sewn after them, only the area sewn first sews there. This is the owner’s idea too: one overlap then lies under the shape at that place, where 2 lay at the corner of 3 shapes. The areas are taken from the first sewn to the last. A piece of an overlap that this cuts off from what shows of its area is left out.

Under a satin column, and under the part that a running stitch or a column sews along the middle of, an area reaches its whole overlap whichever way its rows run, as before. A column’s stitches don’t cover its ends and its bends as a fill’s rows cover an area, and a running stitch covers a thread’s width of its part. Where what an area sews across a line’s part meets a shape beyond the line, the area reaches on under that shape from there. Of the 2 areas beside a line of fur, the one sewn first then sews the line’s part and 1 mm under the other, and the other gives the part up: their overlap is whole, whatever the line’s width.

When parting an area’s overlaps needs more work than the budget allows, the area sews its whole overlap, and SC-I0904 says so. SC-W0913 tells of a place at the warning’s limit of thread, 4 fills by default.

Where the thread lay. Before the step, the running dog in 9 threads had 629 mm² at 3 fills or more. Each stitch of its plan was put to what it sews, and the thread summed over each window of 1 mm, as the measure sums it. The mean window of the thick area had 4.0 fills:

Running lines0.18
Lock stitches0.11
Travel stitches0.03

Of the thick area, 61 % had the overlaps of 2 areas or more on it, and 20 % those of 3 or more. An outline lay on an overlap in 59 %, and an overlap under a column or a line in 33 %. 2 columns joined in 6 %. Locks made half a fill or more in 7 %, and travel stitches in 1 %. A third of the thick area lay between 3.0 and 3.5 fills, because a straight edge between 2 fills is at the limit by itself. The upper fill and the overlap are a fill each, and the ends of their rows a quarter each. The base layer adds 0.3 to 0.45, and the upper fill’s outline a quarter.

What was measured. Each change was built as a switch and measured alone and with the others on the standard pictures. These are the running dog’s numbers in 9 threads. The bare share is what the top thread leaves of the design (covering the fabric). The last column is that share once every top stitch is pulled in by 0.4 mm where the thread turns, with the gaps that then open:

The owner also asked on 2026-11-16 whether the overlap could sew every other row. A fill sews its rows out and back, so a row can’t stop short alone: it was built as rows that reach on past the area at every other turn. With the other rules it measured the same thick area as the overlap by the rows, 393 mm² against 395, and left twice the fabric bare. It was left out, and nothing in the fill changed.

Half the overlap under columns and under the parts of lines took 5 to 8 points more off. It left 6 slivers on the dog on turf in 9 threads, where an area no longer reached across a line of fur, and on the running dog in 6 threads it opened a gap of 0.6 mm² beside a column, which fails the check of the cover. A column that reaches 0.4 mm past its rail takes 3 % off a dog and reverses REQ-AUTO-029. Leaving the outline out along an edge that an area sewn before reaches under takes 13 to 17 points more off, the largest change left. The owner chose the outlines (An outline round a fill), so that is hers to decide. The area sewn last keeping a shared place, in place of the first, opened 4 to 8 gaps once the thread was pulled in: the first area’s rows then end on its own edge with the shape. Without the overlap across a line’s part, the 2 areas beside a line of fur overlapped by 1 mm less the line’s width, and the running dog left 0.184 % bare once pulled in. With both areas keeping the line’s part, the cover was as before and the dogs had only 11 to 16 % less thick area.

The base layer has one layer. The overlaps were not all of it. The base layer sewed as Ink/Stitch’s knockdown fill does, in 3 layers 60° apart. Each layer sews along the piece’s edge from the end of one row to the next, so 3 in 10 of the base layer’s millimetres of thread lay along its own edge: 894 of 2,995 mm on the running dog in 9 threads. There it measured 1.2 to 1.5 fills, and 2.3 at its worst, under the ends of the fills’ rows and under the outline. It measured 1 fill or more on 106 mm² of that dog’s 1,841. Every automatic fill has the rows of one angle, auto_fill_angle. So the base layer now sews one layer, its rows across the fills’ rows: the underlay a fill is usually given, once under the whole design. The setting auto_base_rows at lattice sews the 3 layers as before, for a fabric with pile.

Measured and left out: the base layer cut away under the overlaps, which leaves 43 to 58 % more thick area, since each layer of each piece then sews along its new edges, beside each edge between 2 fills. The base layer cut away under satin columns leaves 2 to 5 % more and a gap with nothing under it. With underpath on, the needle moves from row to row inside the base layer and not along its edge, which leaves 1 to 2 % more. 2 layers leave 10 to 17 % less than 3, and one layer 22 to 29 % less, whichever way its rows run. The check of the cover gives each of these the same result: it knows where the base layer’s shape lies, and not where its thread does.

A part whose one satin column would crowd its stitches is filled (REQ-AUTO-068). What was left at 6 fills or more was no overlap and no fill. A fill’s own thread reaches 2.5 fills nowhere on the dogs. A satin column of a small ragged part does: a column lays 1.25 fills where it runs straight, and each dog had 34 to 40 mm² where one column alone laid 2.5 or more, up to 6.6. A column sews as many needle points on one rail as on the other, so where one rail is short its stitches meet: a part 3 mm across of the dog on turf sewed 19 stitches from a rail 5 mm long to one of 0.8 mm. A part that would sew as one column is now filled when the column would sew more than 2 mm of one rail to 1 mm of the other. A part of several columns isn’t measured, since a column that ends under another is cut on the slant and fans there by the way it is drawn: measured on every part, the bound filled every T and the letters of TS-13B. A part with a hole stays a column too, as the white round TS-13D’s star does: its fill would sew in pieces round the star. Measured by the whole rails’ lengths, the bound filled almost nothing: the dogs’ columns crowd at one bend.

What it changed, as --auto sews each picture, before the step and after it:
