The overlaps alone left the dogs 363, 378, 412 and 491 mm² at 3 fills or more. With the base layer in 3 layers and the rest as it is now, they have 268, 320, 292 and 400.

The step’s aims were no place at 6 fills or more and half the area at 3 or more on each dog, with the cover as it was. No picture has a piece of 1 mm² or more at 6 fills or more, the smallest that the measure counts, though single spots of 4 pictures measure 6.1 to 7.0. The dogs have 55 to 64 % less area at 3 or more. The cover is as it was by its check. No gap lies where the base layer isn’t under it, and the bare share is lower or as it was. The dog on turf in 9 threads has one sliver of 0.18 mm², and the running dog in 6 lost its one. What is worse:

  • The dog on turf in 9 threads changes thread 13 times where it changed 12, and the step’s aim was no more than before. It was traced, by sewing each of that dog’s 19 crowded parts as a column again. The dog changes thread 12 times with the parts of 2 grey shapes at the top of the picture as columns, which sew 3.9 and 2.7 mm of one rail to a millimetre of the other. It also does with 2 parts of a tan and a pale grey shape lower down as columns, 4.9 and 5.9 mm to a millimetre. One part alone doesn’t do it. The cause is rule 2 of the sewing order: a line sews after the fill it borders, and a line beside a column sews when its thread does. Each part that became a fill makes the lines of other threads that touch it wait for its thread. The search for the fewest changes is exact, so with these fills every order within rules 1 and 2 has 13 or more. The twelfth change comes back only by sewing 2 crowded columns again, or by letting a line sew before a fill it borders, which M13.22 measured: more stitches astray and more slivers. Neither was done.
  • The dogs’ satin columns are fewer: 10, 13, 6 and 11 where they were 25, 31, 21 and 28. The parts that sewed as short bent columns sew as flat fills, which changes the look of the fur close up.
  • 16 stitches of the dogs show on another colour for 1 mm or more, where 14 did. Each of the new ones was traced: a row of the area sewn first lies on the part of a running line 0.5 to 0.8 mm wide, beside the line’s thread, where the area beyond lay before. No cheap change takes them away. The line’s thread is 0.4 mm wide and its part wider, so a row of one of the 2 areas lies on the part beside the thread, or the fabric shows there. With both areas sewing the part, as before the step, the dogs kept all but 11 to 16 % of their thick area, and with neither sewing across it the running dog left 0.184 % bare once pulled in.
  • The dog on turf leaves 2 and 1 more jump threads. One is a jump inside an outline in 2 pieces. The others are moves that were under the 3 mm an element’s end may lie from the next start, and are 3.0 to 4.0 mm now that an end moved by up to 1 mm.
  • Once the thread is pulled in by 0.4 mm at every turn, 3 of the dogs leave 0.1 to 0.7 mm² more of their area with no thread at all on it, of 1,640 to 1,940 mm², and one 0.15 mm² less.
  • A fill’s base is thinner. Whether a fill lies flat on the fabric over one layer is for MC-8 to sew: nothing here measures it.

A straight edge between 2 fills that crosses their rows still measures about 2.9 fills by itself: the upper fill 1.0, the ends of its rows 0.25, the overlap 1.0, the ends of its rows 0.25, the base layer 0.1 and the outline 0.25. The 178 to 280 mm² that the dogs keep at 3 fills or more are such edges with a column or a third shape beside them. 2 parts could still go. One is the outline along an edge between 2 areas, which the owner chose. The other is the overlap itself, which costs cover. The demo’s badge was not measured again.

Less overlap under a part’s column

An area reaches its whole overlap, 1 mm, under a satin column that digitizing makes of a part of another shape and sews after it. A column narrower than 2 mm then lies whole on the area, and with its own underlay and top stitches the spot sews 3 fills (rungs placed and less overlap). auto_column_overlap_mm (M13.55, REQ-AUTO-157, REQ-AUTO-158) sets how much the area keeps there instead, from 0 to 2 mm (auto::neighbours::under):

  • Where such a column covers the area, the area is cut away up to the setting inside the edge of what covers it. Where an area sewn after it meets the column, that edge is the area’s, and the area is cut away its whole overlap inside it.
  • Where the area meets such a column, of its own shape or of another, it reaches the setting under it, whichever way its rows run.
  • A wide column whose stitches split (wide columns) is such a column too, and the area under it keeps the setting there as well.
  • Empty, the default, the setting is the area’s overlap, and a value above the overlap reads as the overlap. The area is then taken as before the setting, step for step, and every design sews as it did.

The setting leaves every other overlap as it was. Under an area, under a part’s running line, and under a satin column drawn as a stroke, a person’s own or lettering’s, the area reaches as far as before. The base layer, the cap, and a column’s reach past its rails stay as they were. A part’s column that sews before an area beside it reaches past its rail under the area instead, and the setting doesn’t apply there. The sewing order chooses that freely where the 2 don’t overlap once the layers are flattened. On a test piece of navy and orange fills edged by columns of the other colour, it sewed half the columns before their fills, for one thread change fewer.

The thread pulls a column’s stitches in by its pull compensation, 0.4 mm by default, which they sew past both rails. So an area that lies less than that under the column may show fabric beside it once sewn, and 0.4 mm is the least that the pull argues for. The setting goes down to 0 so that a sew-out can show the edge. An expanded design (REQ-AUTO-142) keeps its areas as the setting cut them and drops the setting.

Measured on every picture of the set at the default and at 0.6, 0.4 and 0.2 mm, in that order in each cell. The area at 3 fills or more and the thickest spot are as fabric::thickness measures them. The bare fabric is fabric::cover’s, with no stitch pulled in:

The badge, the round patch and the marked patch sew alike at every value, since no part’s column edges their areas. The likeness doesn’t change on any picture, the stitches change by less than 1 %, and the jump threads by one at the most. The default’s numbers are those M13.53 measured on main.

The numbers show 4 things.

  • Less thread where columns edge fills. At 0.4 mm the area at 3 fills falls on 9 of the 10 traced pictures, most on the patch, whose letters are columns over a field, and rises 0.5 mm² on the coleus. On the running dog in 6 threads, the goose and the castle it changes by 1.3 mm² or less, within this measure’s noise, which M13.53 put at 8.5 mm². At 0.2 mm it falls further on all but the running dog in 6 threads, which rises 2.8 mm² and gains a stitch astray.
  • The thickest spot falls 0.25 fills on the patch and 0.1 to 0.2 on the pear. It rises 0.06 to 0.12 on the dog on turf in 6 threads, under the cap’s limit at every value.
  • The bare fabric can’t say how little is enough. The measure lays every stitch where the plan puts it, and no thread pulls in. It doesn’t find a gap at any value, as it found none with no overlap at all in M13.53. Its small rises, up to 0.18 mm² on the running dog in 9 threads, are slivers between an area and a column as planned, which the pull can only widen. A sew-out has to show where fabric opens.
  • The cost to plan is one more pass of geometry for each area beside a part’s column. The dogs took 6 to 23 % longer. The photos’ times varied by up to half between runs in no order, which is the noise of a machine shared with other builds.

The sew-out (machine testing) sews a test piece of fills edged by columns 2, 4 and 6 mm wide at each value, and the running dog in 9 threads at the default and at 0.4 mm.

The overlaps’ work

VectorCraft stops an export that runs past its budget (an export that finishes). A traced dog needs several times that budget, so it sews only from the command line, and on a traced dog the overlaps of this page took 71 to 75 % of the work. M13.66 measured that work by stage and by function, and made it cheaper without changing a stitch: the bar is the same machine file, byte for byte.

The work measured. Measured on 2026-11-23 with callgrind, as the perf job counts (xtask/src/pictures.rs), on a build of main f1689168:

  • Nearly all of it is geometry. Each union, intersection, difference and growth of a region is one arrangement of its rings, snap rounded on a grid (normalize::region::snap). The rounding alone takes half of a dog’s whole run, and growing regions takes 31 %.
  • Allocating and copying memory take about 10 % of a dog’s run, inside that geometry.
  • near_later compares the bounds of 2 shapes first, so a pair far apart costs no geometry. Work repeated on the same input is 1.9 % of the region operations’ time on a dog, so a cache would win little.
  • Every union of an area’s neighbours took each neighbour whole. A dog’s large areas lie beside most of its small ones, so each small area paid for the points of every large one beside it. On one dog thinned to 25, 49, 73 and 97 of its shapes, the overlaps took 0.41, 1.84, 5.57 and 15.0 billion instructions, close to the cube of the number of shapes.

The window. Only what lies near an area can change what it reaches under or where it is cut away (REQ-AUTO-205). So each region an area combines its neighbours in takes them only within a window (ops::windowed):

What is combinedIts window
The shapes beside the areathe area’s bounds, grown by 3 overlaps, 4 times TOUCHING and 0.5 mm
The shapes beyond a line’s part the area crossesthe bounds of what it crosses, grown by 4 times TOUCHING and 0.5 mm
The places the area reaches from, for each kind of shapethat kind’s bounds, grown by the overlap the area keeps under it and 0.5 mm
The overlaps that areas sewn before it already sewthe bounds of its own overlap, grown by 0.5 mm

Parts wholly inside the window are taken as they are. Those beyond it, or only touching it, are left out. One across the edge is clipped to the window, ring by ring, with the window’s sides as the clipping lines. The area is cut away up to an overlap inside the shapes beside it and reaches up to 2 overlaps past its edge. Each step keeps only what lies at least 0.5 mm inside its window, and there the clipped regions are the same sets as whole ones. Each point the window looks at costs a unit of work, so a far part of a neighbour costs a look and no geometry.

There is no setting, because the window sews every design as it sewed before.

The regions, bit for bit. An edge the window cuts ends at a new point, which the grid rounds, so inside the window the edge may turn by less than a grid cell, 2^-35 mm. A crossing on it may then round to the next cell. Of the 885 regions the overlaps leave in 84 designs (the golden files’ sheets and plans, the 18 review drawings, the repository’s SVG fixtures with --auto and its VectorCraft documents), 882 were the same bit for bit. The 3 of the shield had 5 points one cell apart, with as many points, and its machine file was the same. Ways that keep every region exact were measured too:

Way, measured on 2026-11-23Regions the sameDog on turf, 6 threadsRunning dog, 9 threadsPearTS-13A
Clipped to the window, as built882 of 885-35.1 %-30.3 %-8.5 %-1.2 %
Whole parts whose bounds meet the window885-14.5 %-8.7 %-5.9 %-0.1 %
Whole parts, with every part within 10 mm of one kept8850.0 %-0.3 %0.0 %0.0 %

Only the last is exact whatever the drawing. Snap rounding bends an edge through each hot point it passes, anywhere along its length, so a point left out or added a cell from a kept edge can change it. The last way leaves out only groups whose bounds lie apart, as an intersection already does (ops::kept), and so saves nothing. The second keeps whole parts, and a dropped part a cell from a kept one could still move it. The clip keeps the most work off and moves a point by a cell at most. A machine file’s points lie on a grid of 0.1 mm. A cell can change one only if a value that the planner rounds or compares lies within a cell of its edge. That can’t be ruled out for every drawing, but a drawing nudged by as little would sew differently too. The golden files of the sheets, the plans and the traced pictures (REQ-AUTO-206) are what check it, and none moved.

PictureMain, millionsWith the window
TS-13A2,228-1.2 %
TS-13B1,798-3.8 %
TS-13D1,688-2.1 %
TS-13E1,151-0.3 %
TS-13F6650

Dog on turf, 9 threads17,299-27.5 %

The counts are of a local build, which counts within 4 % of CI’s, and the perf job’s records follow CI’s. TS-13C crashes valgrind on that machine, and CI counts it. The dogs’ overlaps fell by 39 to 47 %, and the thinned dog’s to 0.30, 1.38, 3.57 and 7.89 billion. Every run took as much memory as before, or less.

Other ways. The rounding redoes its bookkeeping for every edge in each round. Counted on a dog, most of its rounding does 3 rounds, and doing only the edges that changed would save about one of them, some 4 % of a dog and nearly nothing on TS-13A. The split into parts by the rules takes 7 % of a dog and 22 % of the pear. Both wait for a step of their own.

The dogs and VectorCraft’s budget. The count inside VectorCraft can’t be read on the machine the step was measured on, which has no WebAssembly target. On the sheets CI’s compatibility job counts 4.16 to 4.57 times the native count. By that ratio the dogs take 447 to 621 % of their budget with the window, against 650 to 885 % before. The rest of a dog’s run, with no overlaps at all, would still take 188 to 250 %. So cheaper work alone doesn’t make a dog export inside VectorCraft. With the heavy budget that StitchCraft’s RFC proposes (P10, not yet raised upstream), the dogs would take 49 to 68 % of it, against 72 to 98 % before. The pear takes 82 to 90 % of its budget, against 89 to 98 %.

Room on the sheets. TS-13A spends 54 % of its work on its fills’ stitches and 10 % on the overlaps. The window takes 1.2 % off it. CI’s compatibility job counted its export at 84.1 % of its budget with the window, against 84.8 % on main, and TS-13B to TS-13D at 72.8, 75.6 and 64.2 %, against 75.3, 77.6 and 65.8 %. The gate stays at 85 %, and a step that adds work to TS-13A’s fills needs to take it off the fills. CI’s perf job counted the dogs at 11.8 to 14.8 billion instructions and the pear at 2.0 billion, and their records follow.

On black fabric

A preview is drawn on unbleached cotton or on white. Settings::on of the renderer names another fabric colour, a docs shot names it with its fabric key, and the user page of automatic digitizing shows the pear and the dog on black fabric beside cream (REQ-RND-003). The realistic style’s shading is checked on black, where a shadow drawn darker than the fabric can’t show.

Cover isn’t all that black fabric asks. A third photo of the owner’s dog, in glasses, traced in 12 threads on 2026-11-10 and no fixture yet, loses its black tail and chest and the top of its head on black fabric: black thread on black fabric, with nothing to outline it. The measures here don’t read that, and it isn’t in a step yet. The same trace fails the older measure, with a bare patch of 1.74 mm² in the glasses.

Settings of the cover

The base layer’s 4 settings, the 2 limits of the measure, the overlap and the pull compensation are rows of the Settings table.

The base layer lies 0.4 mm inside the design’s outline. The step first built it 0.6 mm inside, the pull compensation and half a thread. 5 of the dogs’ 19 slivers then reached into that band, where nothing lay under them. The owner’s decision of 2026-11-14 is 0.4 mm, on 3 grounds. Ink/Stitch’s contour underlay of a satin column lies 0.4 mm inside each rail by default (contour_underlay_inset_mm). A digitizing guide’s worked example puts its edge run 0.4 mm inside the edge (Embroidery Legacy, as the owner’s research of that day read it). And it is our pull compensation: the base layer lies as far inside the outline as a column sews past it. Ink/Stitch’s Knockdown Fill does nothing of its own at its edge, and its documentation doesn’t give an offset for a global underlay. The value is StitchCraft’s own.

Measured at both offsets, the base layer’s thread lies nowhere outside the design. The table gives how much of it no top thread covers within 0.4 mm of the outline, on a shape of another colour than its own:

Picture, digitizedAt −0.6 mmAt −0.4 mmOf that in the outermost 0.2 mmNearest the outline at −0.4 mm
Traced pear00.02 mm²00.21 mm
Traced dog on turf, 6 threads0.06 mm²0.55 mm²0.13 mm²0.05 mm
Traced dog on turf, 9 threads0.07 mm²0.70 mm²0.18 mm²0.05 mm
Traced running dog, 6 threads0.02 mm²0.15 mm²0.06 mm²0.10 mm
Traced running dog, 9 threads0.02 mm²0.23 mm²0.04 mm²0.18 mm

These are places that the top thread leaves bare at either offset: the bare shares are the same. At −0.6 mm fabric shows in them, and at −0.4 mm the base layer’s thread. The dogs’ stitches astray, moves and jump threads are the same at both, and 3 of the 5 slivers still reach into the band. The measure paints each stitch where the plan puts it, so it can’t tell how far real thread pulls a shape’s edge in, and whether the base layer then shows beside it. MC-8 sews the dog on black fabric with the base layer on and off to see that, and to confirm the defaults (machine testing). A dark base layer under a white fill may show through it, which only a sew-out tells.

How thick a design sews

The owner asked on 2026-11-15 whether so many overlaps would be too thick for the needle after several colours. Her machine is a Brother PE800 with one needle. A design digitized automatically puts thread on thread on purpose. Each area reaches 1 mm under the shapes sewn after it, and the base layer lies under all of them. An outline, when the design sews one, lies on a fill’s edge. The measure of the bare fabric counts covered pixels and can’t tell 1 layer from 6. So a finished plan is measured for its thickness too (stitchcraft_plan::thickness, REQ-AUTO-060 to REQ-AUTO-062).

The 2 measures

A plan is measured where the machine sews it, each needle point on the machine’s grid of 0.1 mm. The engine’s own positions have digits that no file shows. Measured on those, the warning of VectorCraft’s export differed from the one stitch export gives for the same file (the compatibility gate on 2026-11-16). The grid is laid from the plan’s first needle point (M13.35). The same stitches measure the same wherever they lie. On the machine’s own grid, a test sheet drawn on a page measured otherwise than its machine file, and the last pass (The last pass) changed the two otherwise. A coordinate is rounded to a millionth of a millimetre first. Shapes drawn on round numbers put needle points half way between 2 points of the grid, and without that such a point goes to one or the other by the last digits of its position.

Both are sums over a window of 1 mm², on square cells 0.5 mm wide. Each cell is 10 pixels of the grid that the bare fabric is measured on, so the 2 pictures can be laid on each other. The measures have a value at each spot, a corner of the cells, whose window is the 4 cells that meet there. Spots so lie half a window apart. The cells are this coarse because planning measures every design. On cells of 0.2 mm the measure took 80 % as long as planning a test sheet, and VectorCraft stopped the export of TS-13A for the instructions it ran. On these cells it takes 2 to 3 %.

Thread, in fills. Every stitch is a ribbon of thread 0.4 mm wide, whatever its role, since the needle goes through underlay and travel stitches as it goes through top stitches. A window’s thread is the length of the ribbons in it, times 0.25 mm, over its area. A fill with rows 0.25 mm apart, which is StitchCraft’s fill by default, so measures 1, and the number reads as “this many fills deep”. A satin column measures 1.25, because it lays 2 stitches every 0.4 mm. The base layer measures 0.3.

The sources count objects on each other, and a fill is the object here. Counted in layers of thread, one of our fills is 1.6 layers, because its rows lie closer than a thread is wide. The fill that the sources call standard has rows 0.38 to 0.4 mm apart, so ours is the denser one. To read a number of this page in layers of thread, multiply it by 1.6.

Needle points per mm². Each stitch’s needle point counts once. A lock’s are left out, since every design ties its locks on purpose. This measure finds what the first one misses: short stitches and turns bunched in a small patch, which cut the fabric and break thread on their own.

A square 15 mm a side that the engine fills 1, 2, 3 and 6 times measures as many fills 2 mm inside its edge: 1.03 times the layers on average, since the needle also travels under its rows, and 0.89 to 0.97 times at the least.

The limits

MeasureWarningFailingWhere the number is from
Thread, in fills46Brother’s troubleshooting table names “three or more overlapping stitches” as a fault of the design, and the warning started at 3. From M13.46 it starts at 4 (The cap and the warning). Embrilliance’s density map turns red at 6 layers
Needle points per mm²1530Our own. The rows of one fill end 5 to a square millimetre along its edge, so these are the edges of 3 and of 6 fills on each other

A place counts when 1 mm² of it in one piece is at the limit or over it: 4 spots beside each other, each standing for the quarter of a square millimetre round it. 3 rows that cross in a point are no fault.

No source publishes a measured limit for a home machine, and the sources disagree. One vendor wants all layers together to be about 1 fill, a retailer finds 2 full designs on each other too much for a PE800, Brother says 3 and Embrilliance 6. So the 4 values are starting values for a 75/11 needle and 40-weight polyester thread. They are settings of the shape, auto_thick_warn_fills and the 3 beside it. A machine profile’s values change only with a sew-out report, and these have none yet. MC-8 sews a ladder of squares to confirm them or change them.

The warning

Planning measures a design that has an automatic shape, against the limits of its lowest one. When a place is at a warning’s limit or over it, planning says SC-W0913 once, and the design is written all the same. No design is refused for its thickness until a sew-out shows where one fails. The message gives the most of each measure and where, the 3 shapes with the most thread at the thickest spot, and the areas at the 4 limits. A design drawn by hand is not measured: its author chose every layer.

stitch preview --style thickness draws the heat map of a machine file (REQ-RND-004). Thread under 2 fills is grey, and thread of 2 fills or more yellow. From 4 fills it is orange, and from 6 red.

The user page shows the running dog’s.

A plan is measured once, and the measure keeps the stitches it went through. What lies at the thickest spot is found from those: only a stitch whose box reaches within 1 mm of the spot is followed again. A limit above the largest value has nothing over it, and the spots are not searched for it. Counted in processor instructions on the sheets TS-13A and TS-13B, the measure takes 1.9 % of planning. Before these 3 savings it took 2.7 %.

The badge is measured on its PES file, and its area is that of a disc 50 mm across. The lettered patch sews no outline, because its letters are satin columns.

On TS-13B the letters of one word measure differently, and that is what sews. In the word 15 mm tall the base layer has a piece under the M, the A, the T and the H, whose strokes sew as 2 to 4 columns each. It has none under the C, which sews as one column with an underlay of its own. A piece of the base layer is the letter moved in by 0.4 mm, and it is kept only where a disc as wide as the base layer’s rows are apart, 2.6 mm, fits on it. The strokes are 2.7 mm wide. A disc fits only where 2 strokes join, and the C is one stroke with no join. The smaller words have no base layer, and a letter is thick there where its columns meet: the M has 4 columns and 3 joins. In the word 4 mm tall the M and the A still sew as columns, and the other letters as lines of running stitch.

Every standard picture is over the warning’s limit somewhere, and a quarter to a third of each traced dog is. Each part of the stack has its own share:

  • The 1 mm that areas reach under their neighbours causes most of it. Without any overlap the running dog in 9 threads has 155 mm² at 3 fills or more where it has 629 mm², and the other dogs 141 to 144 mm². Among small patches the overlaps of several neighbours lie on each other.
  • The base layer makes a design thinner. It replaces an underlay under each fill, which is more thread: with the base layer off the dogs have 28 to 34 % more area at 3 fills or more.
  • The outline adds 18 to 28 % on the dogs. It lies on the edge, where 2 fills already do.

At a dog’s thickest spot 5 layers or more lie on each other, each with 0.3 to 3.2 fills: small patches whose overlaps cover each other whole. The dogs also have 46 to 104 mm² with 15 needle points or more to a square millimetre, in patches 1 to 2 mm across whose rows are short and whose turns lie beside each other.

The runs in place of jumps lie under later fills and add little: 1 to 12 mm² at 3 fills or more on a dog, 2 % at the most. Each dog’s thickest spot stays within 0.1 of a fill.

What would take thread away

This step measures and changes nothing that sews. Each row was measured on the running dog in 9 threads by changing one setting, and the bare share is what the top thread leaves of the design (covering the fabric), 0.000 % then, on 2026-11-16:

ChangeAt 3 fills or more, mm²Bare, % of the designStitches
As sewn then6290.00014,380
No outline (auto_fill_outline off)5160.00013,076
No overlap (auto_overlap_mm 0)1550.40912,849
Overlap of 0.5 mm4370.01213,638
Smallest area 4 mm² (auto_min_area_mm2)5670.00013,671
Smallest area 10 mm²3230.13811,321

Halving the overlap takes away 30 % and still covers. Merging patches under 10 mm² takes away 49 %, and changes the picture. 2 more ways needed code. One cuts an area’s overlap away where 2 fills already lie under it, as other embroidery software removes hidden stitching. The other sews the overlap with rows twice as far apart. Fills with rows 0.4 mm apart would lower every number of this page by 37 %. M13.30 built and measured both, with the bare-fabric measure checking each change (Less thread where areas overlap).

The thickest spot

M13.30 left thickest spots of 5.5 to 7 fills, and the owner read them as what will sew: “5.5 to 7 just won’t work”. She is right to. The area at 3 fills or more says how much of a design is thick, and the thickest spot says whether any of it is too thick to sew. A report gives both. M13.35 aims at no spot of 1 mm above 4 fills on any test design (REQ-AUTO-073), and its cases list each design still above that with its thickest spot, a list that only shrinks.

Each design’s 10 thickest spots were split by element and by kind of thread on 2026-11-16. On the sheets and the pear one shape lays 2 to 4 fills by itself at the thickest spot, in 2 out of 3 such spots or more:

  • A satin column fans. Its stitches keep their spacing on the outside of a corner or a bend, and on the inside they meet. TS-13A’s blue strip sews 25 stitches from 5 mm of one rail to 1 mm of the other, 3.6 to 4.6 fills by itself. Where 2 columns of one letter join, both sew whole, 2.3 and 1.9 fills in the M.
  • Lock stitches pile up. A half stitch lock is 4 stitches of up to 1 mm, about 1 fill where it lies. The pear’s stem ties in and off at one end, 1.9 fills of locks, and at TS-13B’s thickest spot a fill, its outline and a line lock, 2.75 fills.
  • A narrow column walks its middle twice. The centre walk goes there and back, 0.5 fill under the column’s 1.25.

On the traced dogs every kind adds a little at the peaks. At their spots of 4 fills or more a column gives 1.0 to 1.6 fills, the overlap 0.85, a fill’s rows 0.45, its turns and travel 0.6 to 0.8, the outline 0.3 to 0.4 and the locks 0.2 to 0.45. In a third of those spots 5 elements or more meet, each with 0.3 fill or more.

2 rules for single shapes come first. An automatic column moved its crowded needle points in (REQ-AUTO-071, until Smooth satin edges made it the crowded setting): a point closer than 0.35 mm to the last one left on its rail moves in by 25 % of its stitch, the next by 50 %, as digitizing programs shorten stitches in a sharp turn. A rail’s points lie 0.4 mm apart on a straight column, so no straight column loses any of its width. A larger distance takes more thread away only because it thins every column’s edges. And everything digitizing makes locks in steps as long as the reference machine’s shortest stitch, 0.3 mm (REQ-AUTO-072), as advised for small lettering. Steps of 0.4 mm did nearly as well on the sheets, but where 3 areas meet at a corner the first stitch is short, the half stitch there is short too, and 0.4 mm steps made the corner 4.15 fills where it was 3.76. Measured with the case’s plans:

Stitches and jump threads stay as they were. The bare share of the dog on turf in 9 threads grew from 0.00038 % to 0.00041 %, and one jump thread of TS-13E’s digitized half is no longer sewn down, neither traced. The plans here sew each design where it draws its shapes, so a spot can differ from stitch plan --auto’s by up to 0.8 fill, as the 0.5 mm cells fall.

Alone, the locks took TS-13B from 5.65 to 4.58 fills and the pear to 5.35, and the columns took TS-13A to 4.73. No outline round fills halves the dogs’ area at 3 fills or more and moves their thickest spots little. It isn’t built.

The last pass

The rules of single shapes can’t bring the dogs to the cap: 5 to 8 elements meet at their thickest spots, each with a little thread, and no rule of one shape knows the sum: the finished plan does. So a plan of a design digitized automatically is measured once, and where a spot sews above the cap a last pass takes thread (REQ-AUTO-074, auto/thin.rs). Taking a needle point out of a run takes no thread away, since its 2 stitches become one as long, and cutting a run leaves a jump thread. Thread leaves a spot only where the stitching doubles back on itself, or where the end of a row can move. The pass looks at the stitches within 2 mm of a spot above the cap and takes, in this order:

  1. travel that goes out and comes back within 0.1 mm of where it was 2 needle points before: those 2 points go, and the stitch before them runs on to the one after;
  2. a lock’s stitches beyond one back and forth, where it has 4 or more;
  3. the base layer’s row ends, where a row turns to the next within 1 mm and the 2 rows run nearly opposite ways: the end and the next row’s start move back along their rows by the overlap (auto_overlap_mm), or by half of it;
  4. the other shapes’ top row ends, moved the same way.

A row’s end moves only where it is hidden at both places: the top stitches of the elements sewn after it lay 1 fill or more at its old and new points. The pass measures them from the plan’s end, so that thread taken from a later shape no longer hides an earlier one. The pass skips a change that would leave a spot it lays thread in thicker than both the cap and what it was. It skips one that would make a stitch shorter than the machine’s shortest (a lock’s 0.2 mm into or out of a lock point) or than the turn it replaces, or longer than the machine’s longest. It keeps every top stitch that shows, and it doesn’t make a jump thread. It keeps the measure up to date stitch by stitch (Thickness::add and remove) and hands it to SC-W0913, so that the design is measured once. SC-I1119 says what it took.

A narrow column walks once

What the last pass left at the thickest spots was mostly satin columns: a column’s zigzag of 1.3 to 2.8 fills and its underlay of 0.6 to 0.8, over a fill’s rows that run on under it. The pass can’t take a centre walk’s way back without turning the column. So a column narrower than 2 mm, which has only a centre walk under it, walks its middle once (REQ-AUTO-075, center_walk_underlay_repeats 1), as digitizing guides give a narrow column one run down its middle. The walk then ends at the column’s far end, and the top stitches sew back to where it started. The order of a part’s columns knows that: the next column is the one with an end nearest the start of such a column. A shape that sets the repeats keeps them. Measured with the cases’ plans on 2026-11-17, the last pass in both columns:

The designs sew 5 to 100 stitches fewer. A jump thread starts or ends elsewhere where a column now ends at its start, so a dog has 2 more or 2 fewer. The lettered patch has one more, and one fewer that no later stitch sews down. Where 2 columns of one shape join, the one that ends under the other reaches its pull compensation, 0.4 mm, past the chord between them. Ending it at the chord took TS-13A to 4.15 and TS-13B to 3.79 fills, and changed the dogs by 0.01 or less. The thread pulls such an end back by about as much, which opens the join. The joins are left as they were.

A column that walks once starts at its start (REQ-AUTO-086, start_at_nearest_point off). Started at the point of it nearest the needle, it ran along its middle under itself to its start before its walk, and its top stitches came back to where it began. The run, the walk, the lock and its last stitches met at one spot there. That spot moved with the shape sewn before: the lettered patch’s M measured 4.32 fills at its V, and 4.48 when the A before it ended 0.4 mm further left (M13.36). Starting at its start, the patch’s thickest spot is 4.20 fills and the traced pear’s 4.20, where they were 4.33 and 4.55. With the rules of holes and edges too, the traced dogs’ are 4.53, 4.49, 5.07 and 5.63 fills, where they were 4.53, 4.51, 5.07 and 5.89, and their area at 3 fills or more is 130.5, 156.5, 142.0 and 207.75 mm², where it was 132.25, 160.0, 146.25 and 209.75. It costs jumps. The dogs in 9 threads leave one jump thread more each. TS-13B’s digitized half leaves 44 where it left 42, as the 6 mm word’s A and T jump to the far end of a column, and of TS-13C’s 13 one is sewn down where 5 were.

The outline under a later fill

An outline sews through a hidden stretch of its edge up to 25 mm (an outline round a fill), under the shape that hides it. Where a fill sewn later hides the stretch, the run lies along the edge where that fill’s rows end over the area’s overlap, beside the fill’s own outline. The dogs’ thread piles up along those edges between 2 patches. The owner chose on 2026-11-16 that an outline’s thread may be left out only where a fill sewn later covers it, so that what shows doesn’t change. So a stretch that a later fill hides, in part or whole, is sewn through only when it is 12 mm or shorter (REQ-AUTO-076, FILL_BRIDGE in auto/neighbours/outline.rs). Each stretch left out costs a jump, and a short one lays little thread. Measured with the probe’s plans on 2026-11-17:

DogThickest, fills, walked onceNowAt 3 fills or more, mm²NowJump threadsNow
On turf, 6 threads4.534.531641547374
On turf, 9 threads4.514.511921818184
Running, 6 threads5.155.071841745861
Running, 9 threads6.005.892652347274

The sheets, the pear and the lettered patch don’t change. Leaving out every stretch under a later fill took the dogs’ area at 3 fills or more down by 12 to 19 %, for 3 to 11 jump threads more each, and took the dog on turf’s thickest spot up to 5.09: the last pass could no longer move row ends that the stretch had hidden. With no outline at all, which would change what shows, the dogs’ thickest spots are still 4.50 to 5.62.

The thickest spots left

The cap of 4 fills isn’t met. TS-13B and TS-13E’s digitized halves are under it, at 3.99 and 3.42. The other sheets, the pear and the lettered patch sew 4.17 to 4.55 fills at their thickest spots, where 2 satin columns of one shape join, where a column wider than 2 mm lies over a fill’s rows, or, on TS-13C, where 7 elements meet. The traced dogs sew 4.51 to 5.89. At their thickest spots 5 to 9 elements meet, each with 0.3 to 2 fills: columns that join, the rows of the fills under them (the rims), the turns and travel of small fills, and the base layer. No rule tried here takes enough of that without opening fabric or showing less of an outline. The lowest honest cap now is about 4.5 fills on the sheets, the pear and the lettered patch, and 6 on the dogs.

With the ragged thin lines merged into their neighbours (M13.18, Neighbours), the traced dogs sew 4.69, 4.80, 4.62 and 5.63 fills at their thickest spots, and 141.5, 153.25, 130.5 and 187.5 mm² at 3 fills or more. The dogs on turf sew thicker, at new spots where a satin column or a fill lies over 3 or 4 other fills.

The cap at the end of planning

Sewing a picture’s thin lines as lines (M13.44) and sewing details last (M13.40) change the order the blocks sew in. A changed order moves where fills start and end and which shape reaches under which, so a spot far from the change can pile up thread. No rule about a line or a detail foresees that. Each step tried planning twice and keeping the thinner plan, which doubled a traced photo’s export. The owner asked on 2026-11-18 for one general cap at the end of planning instead, which every design meets whatever its shapes and their order (REQ-AUTO-099 to REQ-AUTO-103). Its value is the setting auto_thick_cap_fills, since MC-8’s sew-out will set the real limits. It was 4 fills when M13.35 built it, and is 3.25 by default since M13.46 (the cap’s default). The numbers of this section are M13.35’s, measured with the cap at 4.

Why the last pass stops. The thread at every spot above 4 fills was split on 2026-11-18, in pieces of 0.2 mm, by kind. A piece of top thread counts as hidden where the top stitches of the shapes sewn after it lay a fill or more:

DesignSpots above 4Top that showsTop under later shapesBase layerLocksTravel
Badges349 %45 %3 %3 %0 %
TS-13A, digitized half539 %57 %4 %1 %0 %
TS-13C, digitized half1336 %60 %3 %0 %0 %
TS-13D, digitized half249 %46 %2 %3 %0 %
Traced pear443 %49 %4 %3 %0 %
Traced dogs19 to 8240 to 45 %47 to 54 %3 to 6 %1 to 3 %0 to 2 %
4 other traced photos142 to 25940 to 52 %43 to 54 %3 %1 to 2 %1 %

No other underlay lies there. The last pass takes travel that doubles back, locks beyond one back and forth, and row ends. Of the hidden top thread it takes only a row’s end where the next row starts within 1 mm, moved back along the row. A satin column’s stitches cross the column, each the next one’s turn, so a column that ends under another column of its shape, or under a later shape, keeps all of them. The middle of a row it can’t shorten. It never takes top thread that shows, and part of that covers nothing that other stitches don’t already cover: a column’s points crowded in a bend or a join, a fill’s short rows bunched at a narrow tip.

One rule. A needle point taken out of a run joins its 2 stitches into one. That takes thread only where the run turns at the point, and it doesn’t leave a jump thread. So the cap looks at the finished plan alone, its stitches with their roles and elements, and its measure. Where a spot sews above the cap, it takes needle points out of the stitches that lay thread there, when other stitches already cover the places those stitches covered. A stitch covers the places within half a thread, 0.2 mm, of it, as the bare-fabric measure paints it. The kinds go in this order, each through the plan from its end:

KindA needle point goes whenWhy it comes here
Underlay and traveltop stitches sewn after it cover all of the joined stitchNobody sees it, and the thread above it already lies flat on the fabric
A shape’s top under later shapesthe top stitches of shapes sewn after it cover the places its 2 stitches coveredNobody sees it either, but it is the overlap that keeps fabric from showing between 2 shapes when the fabric pulls
A shape’s own topits own other top stitches and those of later shapes cover the places its 2 stitches coveredIt shows, so it goes last, and only where it is doubled

Through the plan from its end, a later shape loses its thread before an earlier one is judged under it, as in the last pass. The joined stitch is no longer than the longest stitch of its element and kind, top or not, so no stitch is longer than its shape already sews, and no shorter than the shorter of the 2 or the machine’s shortest. A lock’s needle points, and the one after a lock, stay. A point stays when taking it would leave a spot that the joined stitch lays thread in thicker than both the cap and what it was. Each needle point the cap leaves is one of the plan’s, in the same order. The jumps, the trims and the thread changes are those of the plan. The cap reads roles, elements and places, whatever the picture shows. It takes needle points only of the elements that digitizing made. A shape with settings of its own keeps the stitches they give it, and its stitches cover and lay thread like the others. A test sheet’s half as drawn sews so. SC-I1119 says what the last pass took and then what each kind of the cap’s gave, and what is left above the cap (The cap and the warning).

The cap is stitchcraft_engine::auto::cap. It goes through the needle points whose stitch in or out comes within 1 mm of a spot above the cap, and judges cover by the top stitches within 2.5 mm of such a spot, found once. Places farther off count as not covered, and that keeps the points of a long stitch. Each kind goes through the plan up to 4 times, and stops once a time takes nothing or no spot is above the cap. It keeps the last pass’s measure up to date with the pass’s own helpers, so the design is still measured once. It works on copies.

Its work (REQ-AUTO-101). The cap has the work budget once. A needle point goes only once it has been judged whole, so a cap that runs out of work keeps the points it took until then. The plan they leave is measured again with the budget’s work once more. SC-I1119 then says what the cap took before it ran out. A cap that runs out before it takes a point leaves the plan as the last pass left it, and says so.

Until this fix the last resort judged every column point near a spot above the warning’s limit, in full, for each point it took: its cover and the measure with the joined stitch. Its work grew with the number of those points times the number it took, so it grew with the square of the design. A point can lower the thickest spot only when its 2 stitches pass within 1 mm of it and lay thread there. The cap now judges those points alone. Each point taken then costs the work of the points round its spot. The traced dog in 9 colours, planned in the logo style, needed 598 million units against the budget of 500 million, 587 million of them in the last resort. The cap ran out and took nothing, and the dog sewed 7.1 fills thick where the photo style sews 4.0. It now takes 22.5 million units, 12 million of them in the last resort, and sews 4.6 fills thick, the plan the old cap made with no limit on its work. 4 columns far apart take 4 times the work of one, where they took 15 times.

Measured on 2026-11-20 on the set of Choosing a style, 28 pictures planned in both styles, against main at a1887433. 52 of the 56 plans sew byte for byte as before, and 2 planned in neither then, refused with SC-E0101 by a bug since fixed: a column along a hole kept open read its 2 rails’ pull compensations as one length. The 4 that change are those whose cap ran out of work, all in logo. The area is what is left at the cap, 3.25 fills or more:

No cap of the set runs out of work any more. In photo the same dogs sew 3.9 to 4.0 fills: the last resort stops when no column point lowers the thickest spot, so logo keeps more of a photo’s thread.

Until M13.46 the cap took a satin column’s top stitches too. Where a column’s points crowded in a bend, the third kind could take one. The stitch left then crossed part of the column only. On TS-13C’s face outline it lay 14° from square, where REQ-AUTO-017 holds the columns’ rungs to 10°. Since M13.46 the cap takes none of a column’s top stitches (The cap and satin columns).

A joined stitch no longer than the longer of its 2 left the dogs at 4.02 to 4.18 fills: the next row’s start lay too far for the stitch into a turn. With the element’s own longest stitch as the bound they come to 4.00.

Measured with a prototype on the plans of the cases, on 2026-11-18. The jump threads, the thread changes, the slivers and the likeness stay as they were on every design. The cap as built measures the same but for 3 designs, which differ a little: TS-13C’s digitized half comes to 3.96 fills and 95.75 mm² at 3 fills or more, the badges to 19.50 mm² and TS-13D’s half to 50.75 mm².

TS-13B and TS-13E’s digitized halves and the other test pictures are under 4 fills already, and the cap leaves them as they were. Of the 4 traced photos, which aren’t in the repository, the coleus keeps one spot at 4.22 fills, where 9 elements meet over a run of travel that no later top stitch covers end to end. The cap takes 1 to 2 % of a photo’s stitches, and fewer of the others’. The bare share rose on TS-13A, the magnolia and the building by about 0.001 % of the design, where a joined stitch’s ribbon misses a corner that the pixels of the bare-fabric measure catch.

Without the third kind, the badges stay at 4.40 fills, TS-13A at 4.16 and the pear at 4.16. What holds those spots is a shape’s own top thread laid twice over the same places.

With the orders the later steps make. The cap has to work for whatever order a step chooses. So each design was planned again with the thin lines kept at a likeness of 0.85 (M13.44, auto_min_likeness, its branch at 16d729db) and with the details sewn last and no guard (M13.40, at ed6b6421), each from its branch before it merged:

The jump threads, the thread changes, the slivers and the likeness stayed as each branch had them. With the details last, the dog on turf in 6 threads rose to 5.49 fills and the running dog in 6 to 5.03, and the cap brought both to 4.00. The goose, the building and the coleus kept a spot at 4.01 to 4.12 fills in one order or the other.

Which cap every picture meets. 4 fills when M13.35 measured, on every design of the repository in each of the 3 orders. Of the 4 traced photos outside it, the magnolia meets it in each order and the others come within 0.22 fills. With the cap at 3.5 the badges, TS-13A and D and the pear come under it, but the lettered patch stays at 3.70, TS-13B at 3.74, TS-13C at 3.68 and the dogs at 3.61 to 3.98 (sweep of the prototype, thickest spots only). At 3 the thickest spots stay where they were at 3.5, while the area at 3 fills or more falls to 10 to 20 mm² on the dogs, for 976 to 1,461 needle points fewer. That trade, and what it does to the bare share, was not measured, and MC-8 decides it.

What it costs. Counted in processor instructions, the cap took 1.8 % of planning TS-13A’s digitized half, 0.8 % of TS-13C’s, 0.4 % of the pear’s and nothing of TS-13B’s, which has no spot above the cap. It looks only at the stitches within 2.5 mm of a spot above the cap, found once, as the last pass does, and it takes the last pass’s measure. The prototype that made a cover of every top stitch, and found the spots again each round, took 11 % on TS-13A.

Decisions for the owner.

  • The cap’s value: 4 fills by default. Every test design and the traced dogs met it, and nothing lower was met by all of them. Recommended until MC-8 sews the ladder, and taken. M13.46 lowered it to 3.25 (the cap’s default).
  • The third kind takes a shape’s own top stitches, which show, where its other stitches cover the same places. Without it 3 designs stay above 4 fills. Recommended, since what shows doesn’t change colour and the bare share moves by 0.001 % at most.
  • The traced coleus, goose and building keep a spot at 4.01 to 4.22 fills in one order or more, where no needle point may go by these rules, and SC-W0913 names it. Recommended as it is: a rule for that one spot would be tuned to one picture.
  • The last pass runs before the cap, as now. Its row ends move rather than go, which the cap can’t do. Whether the cap alone would do as well is a measurement for the build phase.

The cap and satin columns

The owner asked on 2026-11-18 whether her machine can sew designs this thick. The trade’s rule of thumb is that one layer of satin over a fill sews well, and that 2 dense layers on each other risk broken needles and thread. In this page’s measure a fill under a satin column is 2 to 3 fills, so a cap of 4 is at the edge. With the cap at 3 the area at 3 fills or more nearly went, but a ring of satin got a ragged inner edge and a satin letter showed specks of fabric where its strokes join (M13.46).

Why. The cap’s test of cover checks whether the places along a needle point’s 2 stitches lie within half a thread of other top thread. A column’s stitches lie 0.4 mm apart or closer, nearer still at an inner edge or in a bend. The stitches beside any of its points pass that test. The test reads places along stitches. It misses the column’s edge, which its needle points make, and the angle of its stitches, since a place on a stitch says nothing of either. Traced on TS-13C’s and the lettered patch’s digitized shapes with the cap at 3, the cap took 95 points of columns:

KindPoints of columnsWhat the joined stitch does
A shape’s top under later shapes49It runs 45° across the column, half of them more, where a later shape covers part of it. Where that shape’s edge pulls in, it shows
A shape’s own doubled top46A point on one edge goes, and its 2 stitches across become one stitch of 0.4 mm along the other edge: a notch in the edge

The same cap took 158 points of fills’ top stitches and 7 of underlay, which change nothing that shows. At 4 fills the cap changed the columns of 11 of the 24 designs measured, by 1 to 10 points, in the same 2 ways. The last pass moved no column’s stitch.

The rule. Below the warning’s limit the cap takes no needle point of a satin column’s top stitches (REQ-AUTO-112), and above it only as a last resort (Above the warning’s limit). The column keeps the edge and the angle that digitizing gave it. Its underlay and travel are the cap’s to take, and so are the stitches of the fills under it and beside it. A column is an element that digitizing made whose stitch type is satin, which planning knows from the stitches each element generates. The rule reads that and the stitches’ roles, and no picture, shape or limit of its own.

A column’s edge. Lowered to 3.5, the cap left TS-13C’s face columns covering 0.982 of the edges under them, once pulled in, where REQ-AUTO-007 holds them to 0.99. The last pass and the cap took the overlap that the face’s and the eyes’ rows lay under the columns, which keeps a column’s edge covered when the fabric pulls the column in. The last pass moved those rows’ ends back from under the columns, and the cap took 44 points of them by its second kind, where it took 5 at 4. Either alone left the edges at 0.982 or 0.983. Both read a column’s stitches where they are drawn. So since M13.46, below the warning’s limit (REQ-AUTO-114):

  • the last pass counts no satin column’s thread as hiding a row’s end;
  • the cap counts a column’s top stitch as covering a place only as the fabric pulls it, shortened by the overlap at each end where the thread turns by 10° or more, as REQ-AUTO-007 measures;
  • where taking a point makes a stitch beside it turn where it ran straight on, the part that the pull then moves in must stay covered.

Judging every covering stitch as pulled, the fills’ rows too, kept the overlap under every shape, but left the cap nearly nothing to take: at 4 the dogs sewed up to 5.63 fills. Only a column pulls in so hard that its edge needs the rows under it.

Where a joined stitch lies. With the cap at 3.25, a stitch the cap joined across a bend of the ear’s rows lay beside the shape for 1.7 mm, on another colour, where REQ-AUTO-026 allows 1.5. And on TS-13E a stitch it joined measured 0.262 mm on the plan’s own points, off the machine’s grid, at least 0.3 mm on the grid, where REQ-PLAN-002 asks every stitch to be as long as the machine’s shortest. So a joined top stitch lays its thread only where its shape’s own top thread or a later shape’s lies, and its length is checked on the plan’s points as well (REQ-AUTO-100).

The cap’s default

With these rules every design was planned with the cap at 4, 3.75, 3.5, 3.25 and 3, and each was held to every requirement, with auto_details_last off (M13.48). 3.25 is the lowest at which the cases of every requirement pass. At 3 the cap joins the base layer’s row ends, hidden under the top, across the rows that REQ-AUTO-028 holds straight. A bare share that rises far under its limit doesn’t count against a cap, and the spots that satin columns hold are reported, not counted. The numbers are under Above the warning’s limit, with the cap’s last resort.

Above the warning’s limit

Left whole everywhere, the columns held spots at up to 5.25 fills on a traced dog with the cap at 3.25, and the badges at 4.40. Main’s cap, which took their points, kept every repository design and dog at 4.00 or less. The owner’s question was whether her machine can sew these designs, so this step can’t raise the thickest spot. Above the warning’s limit, 4 fills (auto_thick_warn_fills), a spot is too thick to sew well, and that outweighs a column’s edge (REQ-AUTO-112):

  • The last pass counts a column’s thread as hiding a row’s end where the turn lays thread in a spot above the limit.
  • The cap’s 3 kinds go first through the needle points near such a spot, judging a column’s stitches as drawn, without the checks of a column’s pulled edge and of where a joined stitch lies. Then they go down to the cap with every rule.
  • What is still above the limit then loses top points of the columns whose stitches lay thread there, one at a time. The candidates are the points that lower the thickest spot by half as much as the best one, or more. Of those the cap prefers one whose joined stitch runs across the column, turning less than 45° from the stitch into the point, and then the one that turns least. It stops once no spot is above the limit.
  • No change takes a spot at the limit or under it above it (REQ-AUTO-074, REQ-AUTO-100). The check read only the thickest spot along the stitches a change adds, so a stitch across a spot of 7 fills could raise a spot of 3.75 beside it to 4.25. Each spot held to what it was blocked changes that add a hundredth of a fill anywhere above the cap, which left the dogs at up to 5.1 fills, so the check is at the limit.

The least change alone took more than it had to. A point whose stitches barely lower the spot changes a column for nothing: on the dog on turf in 6 threads, where 2 columns of one shape meet over a fill at 4.36 fills, it took 2 points that lowered the spot by less than a hundredth before the 3 that brought it under 4.

Measured on 2026-11-19, main against this step, each at its defaults. The plan with the cap at 50 fills has every top needle point of a column, and the points it has that this plan lacks are the points taken. A notch is a new stitch of a column that turns more than 45° from the one it replaced, and a leaning stitch one that turns 10° to 45°:

The cases of REQ-AUTO-102 hold the bare share within its limit and the slivers, jumps, thread changes and likeness to those of the cap at 50 fills.

Of the 4 traced photos outside the repository, the magnolia keeps a spot at 4.22 fills, the coleus at 4.16 and the goose at 4.37, where main’s cap held them to 4.00, 4.12 and 4.00. Each is a spot of fills alone, 4 to 9 of them, whose top thread shows. Before the cap the goose’s spot was at 4 fills or less, and it rose during the cap. That isn’t traced further here.

M13.47 and M13.49 are steps on the joins of traced letters that the owner parked on 2026-11-19, since words on a patch sew as marked text in a built-in font (roadmap). Their measures of joins are used on this page.

On the 2 running dogs the thickest spot is now one of fills alone, 3.92 and 3.98 fills, above the cap of 3.25. The cap took what its rules let it there. Their top thread shows, and the cap takes a shape’s own top only where it is doubled.

The cap and the warning

The cap is what planning aims at. The limits of REQ-AUTO-061 are what a person acts on. A spot that satin columns hold stays above the cap of 3.25 on 10 of the 20 repository designs. The warning used to name the cap there, and it started at 3 fills, which nearly every design has somewhere, so it was said of nearly every design. Since M13.46 (REQ-AUTO-113):

  • SC-I1119 says what the last pass and the cap took and what is left above the cap, whenever a spot was above it, also when nothing could be taken.
  • SC-W0913 warns at its own limits and never names the cap. Its limit of thread is 4 fills, where it was 3. One layer of satin over a fill measures 2 to 3 fills and sews well, and 2 dense layers on a fill measure 4 or more. The failing limit stays at 6.

With the cap’s last resort no digitized design of the repository and no dog sews above 4 fills, but the marked patch, whose lettering the cap covers since M13.50 (the cap and lettering). TS-13A, D and E still warn, at places of their halves as drawn, which the cap doesn’t touch.

The cap and lettering

The owner compared, on 2026-11-19, a patch’s word sewn from its traced letters with the same word marked (text in pictures) and sewn in a built-in font. She chose the marked text for words on patches: its joins are clean and it leaves fewer jump threads. The cap is for her machine, and it didn’t reach lettering. A lettering element has settings of its own, the font’s, and the cap takes needle points only of the elements digitizing made. M13.50 measured how thick lettering sews and what may go (REQ-AUTO-119 to REQ-AUTO-124). The rules hold for any text, any built-in font, any size it sews at and any background.

How thick lettering sews. Every letter and digit of the 2 built-in fonts was planned at 3 heights each, alone and on a field, an automatic fill 3 mm round it, as a patch’s field lies under its word. So were 4 made-up words, the lettered patch with its word marked, and TS-13B’s 4 words marked. Measured on 2026-11-19 with M13.46’s cap, which doesn’t reach lettering:

Letters, 62 a setThickest, fillsMedian of the letters’ thickestAt 3 fills or more, mm²At 4 or more
Small Font, 3.6, 7.6 and 15.2 mm, alone3.72 to 4.282.95 to 3.212 to 160
Small Font, on a field4.87 to 5.353.96 to 4.15175 to 1,3611.0 to 14.8
Barstitch Bold, 12.5, 22.5 and 32.5 mm, alone4.99 to 5.363.74 to 4.00172 to 2023.5 to 6.2
Barstitch Bold, on a field6.31 to 6.674.60 to 5.001,296 to 3,820189 to 257

The marked patch sews 4.53 fills at its thickest spot and 75.25 mm² at 3 fills or more, TS-13B’s words 4.16 and 11.0. At a letter’s thickest spot lie, on average:

Thread at the thickest spotSmall FontBarstitch Bold
The top stitches of the columns that meet there: 2 at 55 to 85 % of the spots, 1 or 3 at the others1.8 to 2.12.3 to 2.6
The columns’ own underlay0.48 to 0.750.33 to 0.40
The font’s running stitches, which run between a letter’s columns and fill its narrow parts0.21 to 0.290.45 to 0.99
Locks, where the thread jumps to the next letter or part0.1 to 0.50.1 to 0.5
The field’s top stitches and the base layer, on a field1.15 to 1.241.16 to 1.19

A hand-digitized font overlaps its columns where they join on purpose. Thread pulls a column narrower, and columns that only touch part and show fabric between them. The overlap that pull compensation gives is what keeps them joined, and on narrow strokes it is enough without more (Wilcom’s help on digitizing custom fonts; a retailer’s article puts it at 0.2 mm on stable fabric and 1 mm or more on knits, Hooping Station). Barstitch Bold sets 0.3 mm of pull compensation and Small Font 0.2. So the 2 columns at a join are the font’s own drawing, and the overlap within its pull compensation must stay.

What may go, by geometry. The cap takes a needle point out of a run and joins its 2 stitches. That takes thread where the run turns, and none from the middle of a straight run.

ThreadWhat became of itRule
The field and the base layer under the lettersA fill’s rows run straight under a letter. The cap takes none of them. Cut away under the letters, as an area is under a shape digitized from an area, the field turned its rows at every letter’s edge, and those turns piled up. Cut 0.5 mm inside the letters’ edges, Small Font at 15.2 mm went from 4.87 fills to 6.23 at its thickest, with 14 times the area at 4 fills or more, and cut 1 mm inside, to 5.70 with twice the area. A field sews whole under lettering, as an area does under a column digitized from a strokenone
A column’s own underlayTagged top today, since a font’s column sews whole. The cap read it as the column’s surface. Tagged underlay, the cap’s first kind takes its turns where top stitches sewn after it cover themREQ-AUTO-120, REQ-AUTO-122
The font’s running stitches under a later columnThe cap’s second kind, as for any shape’s top under later shapes, once the column covers as its own pull compensation pulls itREQ-AUTO-119, REQ-AUTO-121
LocksThey stand where the thread jumps, and the owner cuts those threads by hand. The last pass keeps one back and forth of each, as for any designREQ-AUTO-119
A column’s top under another columnNot the cap’s: the overlap is the font’s. Taken by the second kind where wholly under a later column, it took little more than the rules above, and the column’s end under the other is what pull compensation needsnone

A column’s underlay stays where no other column lies over it. It holds the column’s top up and keeps its edges sharp, and taken wherever its own top hid it, it went at every column’s end. Where 2 columns overlap the other’s top holds them. Taking the underlay out of each column more than its pull compensation inside another, in the plan rather than by the cap, didn’t lower a letter’s thickest spot by more than 0.1 fills: a centre walk or a contour lies along its own column’s middle, and seldom inside the other.

Whose lettering. Lettering that automatic digitizing places is digitizing’s: the text a marker names in a picture (M14.5) and a text object that automatic digitizing letters (M14.7). It is in the cap’s reach (REQ-AUTO-119), its columns are columns (REQ-AUTO-112), and a design of lettering alone is measured with the default limits. A lettering object a person places sews by its own settings, as every element with settings does. The cap leaves it as it is, and SC-W0913 tells of a spot above the warning’s limit (REQ-AUTO-124). Planning knows the lettering it may change by a mark the lettering passes give the elements they place, Element::lettered: Lettered::Digitized from the passes of marked text and text objects, and Lettered::Set where a person sets text in a font. The elements’ ids aren’t that mark, since a person may name a shape so, and no file holds it. A design with lettering of either kind and no automatic shape is measured by the rules’ default limits. With nothing in the cap’s reach, its plan is only measured.

Underlay tagged. A lettering column sewn whole tags as underlay every needle point the satin generator joins before its first top stitch (REQ-AUTO-120), the underlays and the needle’s way to them included. Other columns sewn whole keep every stitch tagged top. Tagged on every column, the underlay changed TS-13D by 9 stitches, since the cap’s rules for digitized columns were measured with their underlay tagged top. That is a step of its own.

How far a column covers. A lettering column’s stitch covers a place only as its own pull compensation pulls it in, at each end where the thread turns: pull_compensation_mm and pull_compensation_percent of the stitch’s length, the larger of the 2 sides’ each, and 0.2 mm at the least (REQ-AUTO-121). It covers so even where the cap judges other columns’ cover as drawn, above the warning’s limit.

The last resort and lettering. Above the warning’s limit, M13.46’s cap takes a satin column’s top stitch as a last resort. On lettering it does not, and the last pass moves no end of a lettering column’s stitches back along it (REQ-AUTO-123): where 3 columns cross at 7.8 fills, it narrowed a column by 2 stitches. Measured on the 744 letters and the words with the rules above and with the last resort, against the rules alone:

Moving today’s letters 0.05 mm, the noise of these measures, bares 12 joins more and makes the line between 2 columns longer at 10. The rules’ 16 barer joins are 0.0025 mm² more at the median and 0.06 mm² at the most, on Barstitch Bold’s 6 and e at 32.5 mm on a field. In the stitched previews the last resort ragged the inner edge of an S and an 8 and changed the angle of an X’s crossing, a Z’s corner and an M’s top. The owner chose lettering for how it looks. And on a field it leaves the thickest spots above the limit all the same, where the field and 2 columns’ tops make 3.0 to 3.8 fills by themselves.

No column’s top stitch moved on any letter, word or picture. The cap took 3,936 mm of the font’s running stitches under later columns, and the last pass shortened the locks at jumps. It took 3 needle points of a column’s underlay. The prototype took 790 mm of underlay, since it counted any later element’s top over it, the font’s running stitches too, where the build counts another column’s only (REQ-AUTO-122). So Barstitch Bold on a field sews 0.2 to 0.3 fills more at its thickest than on the prototype. The jumps, the jump threads, 210 in all, and the trims are those of today on every design, and the plans have 0.8 % fewer stitches. Planning every design together took 5.0 s, against 4.5 s today, on the shared machine at opt-level 1. A set of 62 letters alone had no cap to run before, and now takes 7 to 95 ms more.

The look of the 1,444 joins, by M13.49’s measures of the stitched letters, against main moved 0.05 mm:

Joins worse, of 1,444Moved 0.05 mmBuilt

The built letters bare no join by more than 0.03 mm², and 0.0003 mm² at the median. All the worse joins are Barstitch Bold’s, 6 of the barer ones on its e and 4 on its 6. At 32.5 mm on a field the cap took 10 needle points of the e’s running stitches under its columns, and 8 of the 6’s. M13.47’s lean changed at 328 joins, 242 of them less: it reads the running stitches, which lie hidden under the columns. The previews of 17 letters in each font at 2 heights on a field, and of the marked patch, look as today’s.

The rules leave Small Font as it was. Its thickest spots are 2 columns’ tops, the field and the base layer, and a centre walk along each column, and none of those may go. The marked patch keeps its spot of 4.53 fills, where 2 columns meet over the field between the M and the A.

Decisions for the owner.

  • Lettering that digitizing places is in the cap’s reach, and a person’s own lettering is warned of and never changed. Recommended, since the rule doesn’t read the font, the text or the size.
  • The last resort leaves lettering’s columns whole (REQ-AUTO-123). Recommended, since it changed the letters’ look on the previews and still left the spots on a field above the limit. The other choice is M13.46’s rule for every column, which took the marked patch to 4.38.
  • The field sews whole under lettering. Recommended: cut away, it turned its rows at the letters’ edges and sewed thicker.
  • Small Font on a field stays at 4.9 to 5.4 fills at its thickest joins, and the marked patch at 4.53. What MC-8 shows decides whether a word on a patch needs more. A lighter field under the letters would be a setting of the field’s, in a step of its own. M13.50 doesn’t add one.

Columns stacked on columns

The owner’s limit is 4 fills, the warning’s (SC-W0913), and the cap of 3.25 keeps automatic designs under it by taking needle points out. 2 kinds of design still ended above the cap or the limit. Under --style logo, since the cap’s last resort was fixed (#327), 4 traced pictures ended at 4.6 to 5.5 fills, where --style photo sews them at 3.9 to 4.0. And since M13.8 a drawn document is digitized automatically by default, and the drawn fixture went from 2.74 to 3.85 fills. M13.57 measured what lies stacked there and prototyped 6 ways to take it away. The build phase made the rule, its setting and the message (REQ-AUTO-167 to REQ-AUTO-169), measured again on main as it was after M13.58, M13.60 and M13.61.

How it was measured. Every document of the set was planned with main’s engine at de4f7934, under the default style, whose values are the photo style’s, and under the logo style. The set is made of the repository’s documents and fixtures with the traced dogs, pear and patch and the drawn fixtures among them, the test pictures traced by M13.42, and 13 traced photos of the owner’s, which stay outside the repository. 4 fixtures of effects and ribbons have no automatic shape and were left out, which leaves 41 designs. At each spot of 3.25 fills or more, the stitches in the spot’s window were split by shape and by layer: a fill’s top, the base layer, a column’s top, a column’s underlay, a part’s running line, travel and locks. A spot’s cause is the top thread that lies there, counting a shape that lays 0.3 fills or more. The table’s rows name the causes. The split adds up to the measure’s value within 3 %.

The designs are counted once for each style, 82 in all. Under the default style the whole set has 32.4 mm² at 4 fills or more, and under the logo style 1,176.7.

The table shows 4 things.

  • 2 columns’ tops on each other make 67 % of the area above the cap, and 92 % of the area at 4 fills or more. They lay 2.2 to 3.0 fills of top thread between them. Where they meet, the one sewn first sews the whole overlap, 1 mm, past its rail under the next (neighbours that overlap, REQ-AUTO-029), and the next lays its pull compensation, 0.4 mm, back over it. That is 1.4 mm of 2 columns’ stitches on each other along every join.
  • One column over a fill spreads over the most designs, but it lays 1.3 fills of top on 1.4 of the fill’s, and seldom reaches 4. The fills on the base layer sew no underlay of their own, so what lies under such a column is the fill’s top, its overlap under the column and the base layer.
  • The drawn fixture is neither. Its thickest spot, 3.85 fills, is the cyan ring, which digitizing reads as one closed satin column. Its stitches crowd the inner rail: 2.33 fills of its own top, 0.86 of its underlay and 0.51 of the base layer. There is no fill under it, and no candidate below changes it. It stays under the limit.
  • A part’s running line over a fill adds 0.9 fills to 2.3 of the fill’s in a few places. The cap takes those, and they seldom reach 4.

Why the cap leaves it. The cap takes needle points in this order. First it takes underlay and travel that later top stitches cover, then a shape’s top where later shapes cover it, and last a shape’s own top where it is doubled. Its limits, each with its reason:

  • A satin column’s top points stay below the warning’s limit (REQ-AUTO-112). Taken, a point leaves a notch in an edge or a stitch that leans across the column: M13.46 found 95 such points on 2 designs with the cap at 3.
  • A column’s underlay is tagged as its top in the plan. The cap leaves it as well. The rules of REQ-AUTO-112 were measured that way, and an underlay keeps a column’s edges in place.
  • Above the limit, the last resort takes columns’ top points one at a time, each one that lowers the thickest spot by half as much as the best one or more, and none that takes a spot at the limit or under it above it. It stops when no point does. Where 2 columns’ tops lie on each other, a point taken from one leaves the other’s thread, and its joined stitch runs along the rail through the same spot: the spot hardly falls.
  • Lettering’s columns are never in the last resort (REQ-AUTO-123, M13.50), since thinned letters showed.
  • The base layer’s rows are held straight (REQ-AUTO-028): with the cap at 3 it joined their ends across the rows, and so the cap is 3.25.

The candidates. Each was a prototype in the engine, measured on the designs where it and main’s engine were both measured in full. A design whose plan a candidate didn’t change keeps main’s numbers.

  • a. The area cut away 0.4 mm under a part’s column, M13.55’s auto_column_overlap_mm at the value its own numbers support.
  • b. No fill underlay under a column. Moot: the fills on the base layer sew no underlay of their own.
  • c. A centre walk in place of the column’s usual underlay where a fill lies under it.
  • d. The cap opening a column’s spacing evenly along the whole column, never unevenly, down to half its density, where the column lies on a fill.
  • e. The logo style sewing a part’s column as a running line where it would end above the cap.
  • f. Found by the measuring: a column reaches its pull compensation, 0.4 mm, past a rail under a later satin column, and not the overlap. Under a later fill it reaches the overlap as before.

A design counts as made worse by more than 0.02 fills, 1 mm², 0.1 mm² of bare fabric, 1 stitch astray, 1 jump thread or thread change, or half a point of detail.

The candidates compare as follows.

  • a lowers the area at 3.25 fills by half on the default style, but sewed 7 designs thicker there, a traced label from 3.98 to 4.73 fills.
  • b has nothing to take.
  • c hardly moves a spot, and the round patch, whose columns a person drew, sewed 5.02 fills where it sewed 4.54.
  • d opens the columns’ spacing where 2 meet, and its stitches lie farther apart: the previews show lighter, striped columns on the badge and the lettering. It sewed 4 designs thicker, a traced dog from 4.62 to 4.93 fills.
  • e takes most of the area at 4 fills away, but a running line in place of a column leaves the fill’s edge bare: 3 times the bare fabric and 2.5 times the stitches astray.
  • f takes 68 % of the area at 4 fills or more away under the logo style, and half the area at 3.25 under the default, and sews no design thicker, with no stitch astray, jump thread or thread change more. Its cost is bare fabric, up to 2.7 mm² more on 9 designs of 30, where the bare-fabric measure doesn’t pull stitches in. On the traced goose and building it changed less than 0.02 fills.
  • a and f together took the logo style to 188.0 mm² at 4 fills, but sewed 2 designs thicker, a traced dog from 4.62 to 4.83 fills, and 5 under the default style.

Previews of 3 unlike designs, a traced dog and a lettered badge under the logo style and a drawn badge under the default style, were drawn before and after f and d and looked at. f looks as main’s engine does, and the spots at 4 fills or more are mostly gone from the thickness preview. d shows lighter, striped columns. The pictures are of the owner’s photos and stay outside the repository. The docs’ picture of 2 columns meeting is planned from docs/fixtures/joins.svg, at the default and at 1 mm (where satin columns meet).

The rule. M13.57 builds f (REQ-AUTO-167). It is a default with a setting, auto_column_reach_mm (REQ-AUTO-168), 0.4 mm by default, from 0 to 2 mm, where 1 mm sews as before:

  • It acts on the cause the measure shows, a column’s reach under a later column, with no second plan, size limit or guard for a picture. On main at de4f7934 it didn’t sew any design thicker on either style, and didn’t add a stitch astray, a jump thread or a thread change.
  • The bare-fabric measure doesn’t pull stitches in, so it can’t say how little reach is enough on fabric, and it rose on 9 designs of 30 under the logo style. The setting is the control if the sew-out shows fabric between 2 columns. The rule goes on the next machine checkpoint’s list (machine testing).

It doesn’t take the logo style under the limit: 20 designs of 39 stay above 4 fills. What is left there is the same 2 columns’ tops, which the last resort doesn’t thin, and lettering’s columns, which it leaves whole. So the logo style also says so (REQ-AUTO-169).

As built. A column that digitizing made reaches auto_column_reach_mm past a rail along which a satin column sewn after it runs for more than half the rail’s length. Along any other shape it reaches the overlap, and a rail along both reaches the smaller. Setting 1 mm sews exactly as main did. The build was measured again on main after M13.58, M13.59, M13.60 and M13.61, which changed what sews there. The set is 37 designs. 31 of them are the first table’s that still exist, with the lettered patch the cases draw. The other 6 are the traced photos that M13.58 and M13.60 measured. The 9 local photos of the first table were deleted and are left out. TS-13A to TS-13E plan the same with the rule, so their golden files don’t move.
