On that main, 2 columns’ tops on each other still make 91 % of the logo style’s area at 4 fills or more. At 0.4 mm the logo style keeps a quarter of that area, and the default style 59 % of its area at 3.25. Measured before M13.59 with the prototype, 0.3 mm took a little more, 167.2 mm² at 4 fills where 0.4 mm left 182.5, but bared fabric on 10 designs, and 0.6 mm left 308.5 mm². M13.59 fills more shapes whole and changed these totals by less than 2 %. Bare fabric rose on 9 logo designs, by 2.61 mm² at most, on the snap badge, whose count of gaps stayed 2. Its realistic preview shows its joins closed, and main’s navy showing through between 2 of its rings is gone.

One design’s thickest spot rose. The traced pear, under the default style, sews 3.95 fills at its stem, where 2 satin columns lie side by side, and sewed 3.86. Its area at 3.25 fills or more fell from 3.5 to 2.75 mm². On main that spot sews 4.38 fills before the cap, above the warning’s limit. There the cap pulls 2 crowded points in and takes 6.3 mm of doubled top stitches. With a column 0.4 mm under the other, the spot stays under 4 before the cap, and the cap, which leaves a column’s top stitches below the limit, takes 1.7 mm. The cap acts only above 4, so a spot it used to lower to 3.86 can now rest at 3.95. That is an open point for a later step on the cap. No design sews above 4 fills that sewed at 4 or under it on main.

The message. When a design ends with a spot at the limit or above it, and a shape digitizing made lays thread there and has auto_prefer set to satin, as the logo style sets it, SC-W0913’s hint reads:

Sew a test piece of it first. stitch preview --style thickness draws the thick places. The logo style sews more of this design as satin columns, which lie on each other there: --style photo fills more of it, which often sews thinner.

The hint is SC-W0913’s own, not a new code: the warning already names the place and what lies there. It names the photo style, which sewed every design measured thinner. It doesn’t name auto_column_reach_mm, which is on by default.

Shortened stitch ends. Automatic digitizing pulls a column’s crowded stitch ends in (REQ-AUTO-071, REQ-AUTO-085), and M13.58 measures a rule that keeps every stitch full length unless a spot would sew above 4 fills. Rule f takes 0.6 mm off a column’s width along a join, and so a few crowded ends with it: 24,026 where main pulls in 24,448 under the logo style. It lowers the thickest spots where columns meet, which gives M13.58’s rule more room there. d pulled in far fewer ends, 16,529, because its stitches lie farther apart, but it showed as lighter, striped columns on every preview. The drawn fixture’s ring is a case for M13.58: its inner rail’s ends are pulled in today, and sewn full length they lay more thread at its thickest spot.

The cap below the limit

The cap aims at 3.25 fills (auto_thick_cap_fills) and the owner’s limit is 4 (SC-W0913). Since M13.58 the cap takes a satin column’s points only where a spot would sew above 4: it pulls crowded points in, then takes points out as its last resort (smooth satin edges). The fixes of 2026-11-22 each left spots resting just under 4 where main had them lower, and none above. M13.64 measured where designs rest between the cap and the limit, and why, and prototyped 6 rules. The owner chose c and e, built in M13.64’s second phase (what was built).

How it was measured. A probe in the prototype wrote one row for each plan after the cap, from the plan measured afresh. Each spot of 3.25 fills or more was split by layer, and the spot was given one cause, the first that fits. The causes are a spot still above 4, a column’s top where the spot was above 4 before the cap (“the cap stopped at the limit”), a column’s top under 4 all along, lettering, shapes sewn by their own settings, and fills alone. The set is 67 designs under the default and the logo style. It holds the 18 drawings of the owner’s review, the repository’s 30 SVG fixtures and 6 traced documents, 6 traced photos of the owner’s, which stay outside the repository, M13.60’s corners, M13.62’s wedges, M13.58’s edges piece and the bends and turns drawn for the Ink/Stitch comparison. The 5 TS-13 sheets were planned whole by stitch testsheet and as their digitized halves by the engine, with the engine’s 16 designs of REQ-AUTO-073, 18 sheets of traced letters and the 4 dogs with details last (req_auto_103). The CLI ran at opt-level 1, as did the release-only dog cases. Numbers are on main at acdda98f.

Where designs rest, and why. Under the default style the set has 224.5 mm² of spots at 3.25 fills or more. 64 % of it is a column’s top under 4 all along, 10 % a column’s top where the cap stopped at the limit, and 26 % fills alone. Under the logo style it has 1,500.5 mm², of which 65 % is a column’s top under 4 and 25 % a column’s top where the cap stopped at the limit. 7 % is still above 4, and 3 % is fills alone. The engine’s set is 79 % and 21 % columns, the letters 72 % and 22 % columns and 6 % above 4, the dogs 64 % and 16 % columns and 20 % fills. Lettering is 0.25 mm² on one design. So columns hold 3 quarters of the area or more: the cap leaves them whole under 4. Fills alone are what the cap’s own kinds can’t take without baring fabric. M13.63’s rule for a fill’s row ends over a line sewn before it acts there, and they are counted apart here. Under the 2 styles, 15 plans of the set rest between 3.9 and 4 fills.

The cap stops at the first spot it can’t lower. The pull-in and the last resort each work on the thickest spot and stop when no point lowers it. Every other spot above 4 is then left as it is. On TS-13A the half set by hand sews 5.31 fills, which the cap may not touch, and the whole sheet keeps 39.50 mm² at 4 fills or more where its hand-set half has 39.25 alone. Going on to the next spot instead takes the whole sheet to 35.25. On the letters it takes the 2 sheets above 4, at 4.44 and 4.18 fills, to 4.00, and under the logo style 3 of the 4 traced dogs from 4.09 to 4.16 fills to 4.00 or under, the fourth from 4.17 to 4.09. It moves nothing on a design whose thickest spot the cap lowers.

What smooth edges cost. A pulled-in point is a stitch end inside the column’s edge, a second row where several sit side by side. The pull-in was prototyped down to 3.75, 3.5 and 3.25 fills everywhere, each point moved by its first share only below 4. Under the default style, on the 61 designs without the photos, the area at 3.25 fills or more and the points pulled in were:

Pull-in down toArea at 3.25 or more (mm²)Points pulled in (shown)Logo style areaLogo style points

Each square millimetre taken off moves 5.7 points from 4 to 3.75, 4.9 from 3.75 to 3.5 and 12.9 from 3.5 to 3.25 (4.6, 4.1 and 8.2 under the logo style). A point “shown” is one whose new end no later shape covers: 93 %. The cost shows on the review’s heart banner. Pulled in down to 3.5, 34 points along the banner’s inside edge end a quarter of the column short. They show as red teeth along the edge, the second row of ends that M13.58 took away, for 15.2 to 11.8 mm² and a thickest spot of 3.85 to 3.84 fills. The lettered patch’s M shows a notch, a pit 0.045 mm², where a point moved by its second share. REQ-AUTO-085 limits only the first share.

Knots at tight turns. Where a column turns tightly, its inside crowds into a knot: a 5 mm line turning by 60° sews 3.83 fills, a 3 mm band whose end hooks back 3.71 and the zigzag’s cusp 3.73. A point there lies closer to its rail’s neighbour than half the spacing, 0.2 mm, which is where the rails’ lengths differ by 2.5 times or more, the outside being opened to 0.5 mm at the most (REQ-AUTO-170). Pulling in only such points, and points whose new end a later shape covers, below 4 and down to the cap, the corner sews 3.09 fills, the hook 3.24 and the cusp 3.24. The banner, the shield and the pear keep their edges. At 0.1 mm the hook stays at 3.27, and at 0.3 mm the banner gets 4 points and the shield 2: gentle bends start there.

The candidates. Each was planned on every design of the set. The last column names each design whose thickest spot rose.

All but d and d2 go on past a spot the cap can’t lower. a, b and c hold their pull-in so that no spot above 4 gets thicker: without that hold, pulling in down to 3.25 took a letters sheet from 4.44 to 6.16 fills, and with the hold on every move of the cap, not only on the pull-in’s, the traced dogs rose from 4.16 to 6.40. Taken before the cap’s work above 4, the pull-in below it ran a letters sheet out of work. d sets short_stitch_distance_mm on automatic columns, 0.1 and 0.167 mm, so that points closer than that move in as they are made. Its moved ends crowd again a quarter of the way in: the 60° corner rose from 3.83 to 3.95 fills and a traced dog under the logo style from 4.16 to 4.77. d2 is the Ink/Stitch comparison’s advice (notes/inkstitch-check-work, section 2): sew a sharp turn as 2 joined pieces. M13.60’s mitre cuts only the corners of a line drawn straight, so it reaches the 60° corner, 3.83 to 3.45 with 43 more stitches, and no curve, cusp or closed line: the hook and the zigzag keep their knots. The pull-in at knots lowers the corner further, to 3.09, as one column with the same stitches. The last resort taken down to 3.25 as well took 133.0 to 38.0 in the default style and adds little to the pull-in’s 42.5: it takes visible column points, and isn’t proposed.

What must not get worse. Candidate c moves no point of the banner, the shield, the pear under the default style or the review’s mug. The engine’s suites of smooth edges, column ends, points, traced letters, columns, cover, one shape and small details pass, and REQ-AUTO-172’s bound holds at 204 points. What fails is what records the change: REQ-AUTO-073’s list, whose badges fall from 3.99 to 3.98 fills, the lettered patch from 3.95 to 3.86, TS-13C from 3.93 to 3.89 and TS-13D from 3.79 to 3.75. The release-only dog cases, run at opt-level 1, keep every thickest spot or lower it, 3.91 to 3.77 on the dog on turf in 9 threads. Their recorded area at 3 fills falls on all 4, from 8,475 to 8,150 on the running dog in 9 threads, which leaves one sliver more. Bare fabric is unchanged on the engine’s sets. The 6 photos fall under the logo style from 4.67 to 4.36 fills on the goose and from 4.48 to 4.36 on the cat. With all 67 designs, the default style’s area falls from 158.5 to 139.5 mm² and the logo style’s from 1,295.0 to 874.2, its area above 4 from 27.8 to 1.2. Plan times moved within the noise of a shared machine. With c the cap’s own measure agreed with the plan measured afresh on every design. With the knot taken at 0.1 or 0.3 mm, or the pull-in down to 3.25 everywhere, one traced dog under the logo style ended thicker than the cap’s measure said, 4.35 against 4.02: an update of the measure during the pull-in misses something there. The build re-measures after the pull-in below the limit, and keeps it only where no spot ends thicker.

The rule (c and e). The cap goes on to the next spot above the limit when no point lowers the thickest one (REQ-AUTO-194). Then, below the limit and down to the cap, it pulls in a column’s crowded points at its tight turns, closer than half the spacing to their rail’s neighbour, and points whose new end a later shape covers. Each moves by its column’s first share only, and no spot above the limit gets thicker (REQ-AUTO-195). Both come with a value of the existing setting auto_short_stitches, knots, which a person sets. This step adds the rule and its fix as that value: by default nothing sews differently from main. above_limit, the columns of M13.58, stays the default for now, and crowded gives the columns of before M13.58 (REQ-AUTO-196). The default waits for room in VectorCraft’s export budget, as the next paragraphs say.

What was built

The cap runs its pull-in and its last resort as before, each stopping at the first spot where every point fails to lower it. Where a column of its is sewn with knots, it then runs both again, going on past such spots. Main’s first run is kept as it was so that a large letter sheet doesn’t run the last resort out of work, as the measuring found. Going on, they look only at the spots that have a top stitch of their points’ shapes near, from the stitches the cap already files by cells of a millimetre for their cover. For a spot, the points looked at are those at either end of such a stitch. Among them are all the points with a stitch within 1 mm of the spot that the cap looked at before, in the same order. The spots wait in order of their thread, thickest first. A spot whose thread changed goes back in its new place. Going through every spot of the measure for each spot passed by, and every point for each spot, cost TS-13D, whose hand-set half sews far above 4 fills, 25 times its plan’s work. Then come the pull-ins below the limit, on the columns sewn with knots, of points near a spot above the cap. A point is a knot when its rail’s neighbour lies closer than half the column’s zigzag spacing, compared as lengths are, so a neighbour exactly 0.2 mm away makes none. Its new end is covered when a top stitch of another shape sewn later covers the first 0.1 mm of its way back. The cap’s measure is held during these pulls: no spot that a moved stitch lays thread in ends thicker than the larger of what it was and the limit. The measure is kept up to date as above the limit, with no second measure of the plan: the test sheets’ work has no room for one. A first build measured the plan afresh after the pulls and would undo them where that measure was thicker. On the engine’s set and the 67 designs it found nothing to undo, and the cases of REQ-AUTO-195 check the kept measure against the plan measured afresh.

The pass costs work where it moves points, and the test sheets’ exports in VectorCraft have little room: the gate of compat/vc-contract/tests/contract.rs fails a sheet above 85 % of the instructions VectorCraft allows an export, and TS-13A uses 84.8 % on main. So the cap was made cheaper where it already worked. The measure keeps the thickest spot it found until a change takes thread from that spot or makes another as thick, so the cap’s checks of whether a spot is above a level no longer go through every spot after each change. A change only tried, to see how much it would lower a spot, keeps that spot too. The cap finds its candidate points for each zone in one pass over the plan, not one pass for each kind. Measured with callgrind at opt-level 1, with knots on, TS-13A takes 3,219 million instructions where main takes 3,228, TS-13B 2,534 where main takes 2,566 and TS-13E 1,582 where main takes 1,592. Every sheet sews byte for byte as before these changes. The 18 letter sheets took 27 s where main took 29 s.

That held CI’s native perf records, but not the gate: inside VectorCraft, compiled to WebAssembly, TS-13A’s export took 9,261 million of the 10,609 million instructions allowed with knots by default, 87.3 %, where it takes 8,993 million on main. The native savings from keeping the thickest spot between changes were not there in the WebAssembly build, whose instructions count differently, while the pull-in’s own work was. That is why the perf job passed and the gate failed. So knots stays a value to set. REQ-AUTO-194 alone by default failed the gate too: TS-13A’s export took 9,127 million instructions, 86.0 %, while natively at opt-level 1 it took 3,175 million where main takes 3,228. So the fix comes with knots as well. With above_limit, the default, the cap runs only what main runs and sews byte for byte as main, in 3,140 million instructions at opt-level 1 on TS-13A. Going on past spots costs TS-13A about 35 million natively, 1.1 % of its work. The default flips to knots in a step of its own once a test sheet has that room in VectorCraft’s export budget. With knots TS-13A takes 87.3 % of it, and with REQ-AUTO-194 alone 86.0 %. It has to fall under 85 %, where main takes 84.8 %: from 87.3 %, about 2.7 % of its work. M13.66’s window on the neighbours’ overlaps gives about 1.2 % of that, and the rest has to come off the fills’ stitches or off the pull-in’s own work in WebAssembly. The tables below measure the setting’s effect: the set planned with knots.

Open points. Where a design still rests just under 4 after c, a column lies whole over a fill: TS-13A’s strip of 2.56 fills over 1.00 of fill and 0.25 of base sews 3.99, and the traced running dog 3.98. No point there is crowded. Taking that column’s points, or the fill’s rows under it, shows, and is a step of its own.

Likeness

Each merge of automatic digitizing takes a jump thread or a thread change away: a speck (REQ-AUTO-008) and a thin line (REQ-AUTO-090) sew as part of their neighbour. A merge can also take what makes a picture read, such as an eye or a stripe. The rules go by geometry and can’t tell an eye from noise. The likeness measure checks afterwards what the geometry kept (REQ-AUTO-093, stitchcraft_testkit::fabric::likeness).

The measure

The trace is the design as drawn, before anything merges, painted as the bare fabric paints it at 0.05 mm on a grid round the design alone. The stitches are painted on the same grid: each pixel has the colour of the last top stitch whose thread, 0.4 mm wide, covers its centre. Underlay and travel stitches lie under the top stitches, as locks do, and are left out.

  1. A colour’s parts are its pixels that touch at a side or a corner.
  2. A part counts when it is as large as the smallest area sewn by default, 1 mm², and a disc a thread across fits in it. Smaller, it is a speck, which the rules merge or leave out, and no stitch of its own sews it narrower.
  3. A part’s core is its pixels that a disc a thread across, centred on them, fits in.
  4. A pixel is shown when the stitches have its colour within half a thread of it.
  5. A part is kept when half of its core or more is shown.
  6. A colour’s likeness is the share of its parts kept, and the picture’s is the mean of its colours’.

A share of matching pixels is ruled by the large patches. An eye of 1.44 mm² on a field 20 mm wide is 0.36 % of the picture, and its loss moves that share by less than the stitches’ edges move it. Each part counts once, whatever its size, and so the eye’s loss halves the likeness of that picture of 2 colours. Each colour counts once too, so the few dark parts of a face weigh as much as a colour’s many pieces of shading. A change that keeps every part keeps the likeness, however it moves the stitches.

The core leaves out a strip half a thread wide along a part’s edge, where its neighbours’ rows end. A strand 1.4 mm wide that a running stitch sews along its middle shows its colour on 0.8 mm of its core’s 1 mm, and is kept. The parts of the traced dogs are shown on less than a fifth or more than three fifths of their cores, so the line at a half moves no count.

A traced photo has hundreds of bits of texture smaller than 1 mm². Counted, they ruled the measure as the large patches rule a share of pixels: 476 of a traced magnolia’s 972 parts that a thread fits in are such bits. Parts that digitizing merges as specks show when the smallest area is raised.

The limit

The pictures in the repository keep a likeness of 0.75 or more (REQ-AUTO-094), and each case records its picture’s number, so that any change that takes a part away shows. The limit is the lowest likeness of those pictures, the dog on turf in 9 threads at 0.779, rounded down to a twentieth. The traced dogs and the pear set it among photos, and the lettered patch and the test sheets’ digitized halves among drawings.

Before M13.44 the local flowers, bird and building kept 0.43 to 0.48, under the limit. Thin lines are half of their parts, and the rule of REQ-AUTO-090 merged them all. With their thin lines sewn as lines they keep 0.93 to 0.99. Merging now keeps the picture’s likeness (Merging by likeness).

The before and after sheet shows the share of detail lost, 1 less the likeness, in percent, from the same function (before and after sheet).

Merging by likeness

Thin lines merge only while the picture keeps auto_min_likeness of its likeness (Neighbours, REQ-AUTO-095, M13.44). The setting is 0.85 by default since M13.45. At 0 every line merges, as before the rule.

It was merged at 0, off, because keeping lines can make a spot sew thicker far from any line. A line kept sews as a running stitch, and the sewing order then puts the blocks of a colour in another order. A fill that sewed first at a spot can then sew over others there. On one photo measured at 0.85 the thickest spot rose from 5.60 to 7.03 fills, at a place 3.6 mm or more from every line kept, where the block order had changed. Merging any quarter of the lines kept there left 7.03 to 7.62 fills. Merging all of them put that spot back, and the thickest spot moved to another place at 7.55. On another photo the thickest spot moved too, and the block order changed there as well. Where a reorder piles thread can’t be told from a line, so no rule about lines prevents it, and planning twice to check costs a second plan. The answer is one cap on thickness at the end of planning that every design meets, whatever its order (M13.35). With the cap merged, the owner turned the setting on by default (M13.45). Details last gives the numbers of both settings on.

The measurements below are evidence of what the rule does, at a bound of 0.85 and as each picture sews with the setting off. No picture is the rule’s goal. The photos marked * are local. Thickest is the thickest spot in fills, and the area is at 3 fills or more, in mm².

The running dogs and the drawings sew as on main, and the dogs on turf keep 3 and 4 lines. Their strands of fur lie among many pieces of their colours, so each is a small share of its colour. The flowers’ twigs, the shadows along their branches and the pale rims of their petals are most of what their colours have there, and they sew as lines: the photos keep 0.78 to 0.85, where they kept 0.43 to 0.48. That costs them 50 to 236 jump threads more. The goose’s thickest spot rises by 1.43 fills and the magnolia’s by 0.14, and the coleus’s falls by 3.07.

Merging counts only what it loses itself. The measure also counts the parts that specks and stitches astray lose. It also counts as parts some lines that are too narrow for a disc a thread across as merging measures them on their outlines, but not on the measure’s grid. So a picture keeps less than the bound: at a bound of 0.8 the goose kept 0.782 and the castle 0.728, under the limit. With no line merged that is a part, the castle keeps 0.918. A bound of 0.85 kept every picture measured at 0.75 or more, so 0.85 is the value to raise it to.

2 other ways were measured and not taken. Counted colour by colour, each colour keeping 0.75 of its parts, merging kept more lines on the dogs, 6 and 8 on the dogs on turf, which left them 5 and 7 jump threads more, and the running dog in 6 threads 0.39 fills thicker. Counted over the picture, a colour with many pieces gives up its lines for a colour with few. The other way merged first the lines closest in colour (CIEDE2000) to the area each would go into. At 0.8 the magnolia kept 0.802 and the coleus 0.793 so, the same as with the cheapest first, with 30 and 71 jump threads more. It moved what is lost from dark lines on dark patches to the pale rims of petals on pink ones.

Sewing order

Automatic elements sew in an order StitchCraft chooses (stitchcraft_engine::auto::neighbours::order, M13.3). The order is chosen after specks are merged, so that a speck merged into an area is no element of its own, and before areas reach under their neighbours, which follows it. VectorCraft’s Auto Grouping draws each shape together with the shapes inside it. Sewn in paint order, a picture traced with it changes thread at nearly every shape, and each change is a stop to rethread a home machine. The order comes from 6 rules.

  1. What overlaps keeps its paint order (REQ-AUTO-010). Of 2 shapes whose paint overlaps, the lower sews first, or it would cover the upper one. Paint order is the design’s, with each area digitized whole moved back before its details (Neighbours). An area paints the region it sews once the layers are flattened, and a satin column the area between its rails as they are sewn. Lines paint the areas their strokes paint as drawn, and a part’s line or column the part. Shapes that only meet, as abutting paths do, may sew in either order. Rounding leaves a sliver no wider than twice the shapes’ tolerance between edges drawn as one, and shapes overlap only where their overlap is wider. Where 2 lines 0.1 mm wide cross, they overlap.
  2. A line after the fill it borders. A line that touches a fill without overlapping it sews after the fill and covers its edge. Its running stitch is as wide as its thread. It can’t reach under the fill, and a fill sewn after it lays the ends of its rows on it. Rule 1 comes first. A line that rule 1 already sews before the fill, through the shapes it overlaps, keeps its place. M13.63 plans that it gives way where it costs a threading (each thread threaded once).
  3. The fewest thread changes (REQ-AUTO-037, M13.22). Within rules 1 and 2 the elements sew in batches of one thread. Once a thread is sewing, every element of it that is free to sew does, and those they free: an element is free when every element that must sew before it has. Sewing more of a thread now never costs a change later, so an order is a row of batches, and the row that changes thread least is searched for. The base layer sews before the first automatic element, so its thread is sewing when that element’s run starts. Threads are the same when their colours are, as plan assembly counts thread changes. A PES file gives different colours different palette entries while they last (REQ-THREAD-002), so 2 colours are one thread to the machine only past the palette’s 64 entries. An element sewn in several threads (M10.5), as a tartan fill is and a gradient fill will be, moves as one. Its colours stay together, in the order its generator sews them. It is a batch of its own, which costs a change unless the thread it starts in is sewing. The batch of the thread it ends in follows it (REQ-AUTO-024). Its threads are read before it is generated, from its shape and settings.
  4. A satin column after the fill it borders, where that costs no thread change. A satin column that automatic digitizing made covers the fill’s edge when it sews after the fill. Sewn first, it reaches its overlap under the fill (Neighbours), so the fabric is covered either way. Of the rows with the fewest changes, the search takes the one in which fewer columns sew before a fill they border. The column then waits for a later batch of its thread when the row has one after the fill. Satin columns with settings go by rule 2.
  5. Details last, where that costs no thread change (REQ-AUTO-096 to REQ-AUTO-098, M13.40), when the design turns auto_details_last on. An eye, a nose or a mark on a patch sews after the shapes round it, so that no fill’s rows lie over it (Details last). Of the rows with the fewest changes, the search takes the one in which fewer details sew before a shape round them, as it does for rule 4, and a detail waits for a later batch of its thread when the row has one. A line or a column round a detail goes by rules 2 and 4.
  6. The nearest next. Of the free elements of a batch, the next is the one whose box lies nearest the box of the element sewn last. On a tie it is the one whose box’s centre lies nearest, and then the one drawn first. Each element then starts and ends at its points nearest its neighbours (REQ-GEN-003).

The search (stitchcraft_engine::auto::neighbours::order::fewest). Finding the fewest thread changes under rules of what sews before what is hard in general, and a design has few threads. The search keeps rows of batches as far as they go, each with the elements it has sewn and the thread it leaves sewing. It goes on from the row with the fewest changes so far and still needed, one for each thread that has elements left. Its first whole row then has the fewest changes of any order. Of 2 rows that sew the same elements and leave the same thread sewing, it keeps the one with fewer changes, then the one with fewer columns before their fills, then the one found first. It tries the threads of the free elements in paint order. A design then sews the same on every run. Each run of elements that nothing moves across is searched on its own, and starts in the thread the run before it left. Where elements with settings of a run sew before its first automatic element, the base layer sews between them and it, which the search doesn’t count. order/fewest/tests.rs compares the search with every order of 300 small runs drawn at random.

tests/auto_order.rs checks rules 1 to 4 together on random designs of squares and lines, wherever the shapes fall.

Elements with settings keep their order among themselves, and automatic elements move round them within the rules. The parts of a shape sewn as several elements, such as a line’s satin columns, keep their order too, so that a trim after the shape still comes after its last part. --sort-colours lets the elements with settings move as well (REQ-AUTO-011), whether or not --auto is given. It is for a design drawn in an order that suits drawing it rather than sewing it. The switch is the command line’s. VectorCraft’s exporters order the automatic elements when their auto option is on, and sew the elements with settings as drawn. An element with a stop after it keeps its place: the elements drawn before it sew before it, and those drawn after it after it. A user sets a stop for a reason, such as placing appliqué fabric, and it comes where it was drawn. SC-I0907 says when the order isn’t paint order, with the thread changes of each, those inside the elements sewn in several threads among them. M10’s routing tools don’t start from this order. They route satin columns and running stitches drawn by hand as Ink/Stitch’s tools route them, and write the route into the document (routing tools). Each element that automatic digitizing makes sews its underlay just before its own top stitches. The rules leave underlays_first off (ADR-0018).

Shapes are near when their boxes come within twice the shapes’ tolerance of each other. An automatic area’s box is round its region, and a narrow part’s line’s round the part. Any other shape’s box is round its path’s points, grown for a stroke by half its width times √(L² + 1) for a miter limit L. That holds the corners of a miter clipped at its limit, as a satin column’s rails clip it. Other strokes grow by half their width times √2, which holds a square cap’s corners. Rule 4 measures with the same boxes. The order finds a shape’s paint only when the shape is near an element that moves.

Work (REQ-AUTO-012). Each element has the work budget to itself to find how it lies beside the shapes drawn after it. It costs a unit for each of them, and the work of those near it. One that needs more keeps its place in paint order as an element with a stop after it does: nothing moves across it. An element after it that one before it frees still waits its turn after it, and each element sews once. A shape whose paint can’t be found within the budget keeps its place beside the shapes near it. Finding the rows of batches has the budget once for the whole design. It costs a unit for each element looked at and 1,000 for each row kept. The default budget keeps 500,000 rows at most. From the run that needs more on, the elements sew as they did before M13.22. Each thread sews while an element of it is free, then the thread of the first free element in paint order, and every satin column sews after the fills it borders. Placing the elements has the budget once more, and costs a unit for each free element looked at each time an element is placed. An order that needs more sews the elements not placed by then in paint order, after the others. SC-I0907 says each. SC-I0904 says it of a satin column instead, whose area the areas sewn before it then don’t reach under either. A fill’s generator needs the work of its region too, and says it of a fill (SC-E0004).

Measured. The test pictures, sewn as drawn and digitized as Neighbours sews them.

Rule 1 costs the dogs nothing: with it alone each thread sews once, which is 5 changes in 6 threads and 8 in 9. Rule 2 costs 3 or 4 changes. A dark line of fur sews after the lighter fills it borders, and the dark fills under those sew before them. The dark thread then sews twice. Every column after its fill would cost up to 2 more, which rule 4 saves. Taking each thread while it lasted, as the order did before, cost 4 to 16 changes more than the rules need. With the running lines free to sew before the fills beside them, the dogs change thread 5 and 8 times, but lay 29 stitches astray where M13.21 laid 21, and leave 4 slivers where it left 2: a fill sewn after a line ends its rows on the line. So rule 2 stays a rule. A trace drawn colour by colour changes thread less in paint order than as sewn, 5, 9, 7 and 12 times on the dogs, because its lines then sew before the fills beside them.

Fewer thread changes leave more jump threads. The machine cuts the thread at a change and not at a jump, so a thread that sews once jumps where it changed before. With M13.22’s order the dogs jumped 84, 91, 79 and 108 times, where they jumped 82, 94, 81 and 112 times, and left 77, 78, 70 and 95 jump threads to cut by hand, where they left 71, 73, 64 and 84. Later steps moved these counts, and tests/auto_photos.rs records each step’s: since M13.48 the dogs leave 60, 62, 49 and 64 jump threads, with the runs under the shapes sewn later (M13.17, runs in place of jumps). The traced pear and the test pictures change thread once for each thread after the first, as they did. The digitized halves of the TS-13 sheets change thread 2, 4, 4 and 6 times, where they changed 5, 4, 5 and 8 times. TS-13B’s black sews twice: its base layer and letters first, and the lines of its windows after the fills they border. The search keeps 87 to 3,584 rows of batches on the dogs, and 12,313 on a traced plant of 972 elements, of the 500,000 the default budget allows.

What shows (REQ-AUTO-025, REQ-AUTO-026). Digitized, a picture’s stitches should cover the fabric wherever it has a colour, and lay thread only where it has that thread’s colour. stitchcraft_testkit::fabric measures both on any design and its plan, a photo traced into shapes as well as a test picture. It paints the picture on a grid of 0.05 mm as a viewer paints it, each place in the colour of the last shape drawn over it, the background left out but where it shows inside the picture. Bare fabric is the part of the picture’s colours that the top stitches leave uncovered, each stitch’s thread 0.4 mm wide, opened with a disc as wide as the thread: a sliver narrower than a thread beside a running stitch closes as the thread spreads. A patch of 1 mm² or more, the smallest area that digitizing sews, fails. A top stitch is astray where it shows on another colour or on bare fabric for 1 mm or more, past the 0.4 mm of pull compensation. The overlap lies under a shape sewn later, which covers it. An underlay or travel stitch is astray wherever it shows, and any stitch astray fails. The moves sewn on from one element to the next (REQ-ASM-002) and the jump threads that the PE800 leaves are counted apart.

Test picture, digitizedLargest bare patchLongest stretch astrayMoves astrayJump threads

The pear leaves 2 jump threads, where it left 4: the narrow ends of its olive and dark olive bands are uneven, and sew as fills with their bands (M13.21). tests/auto_fabric.rs keeps the moves and the jump threads as measured. The traced dogs don’t leave a bare patch. They show 3, 5, 3 and 9 moves and leave 60, 62, 49 and 64 jump threads, as tests/auto_photos.rs records them since M13.48.

The owner’s dog, traced from her 2 photos in 6 and in 9 threads (M13.15, docs/fixtures/README.md › The traced dogs), lays 12 stitches astray since M13.46. tests/auto_photos.rs lists each stitch (ASTRAY), with where it lies and why, and its case fails on any other stitch astray and on any listed one that no longer is. 3 are rows of fills beside a line of fur of another colour that sews on the fill or that the fill reaches under: the line sews as wide as its thread, and the row shows beside it. 4 are stitches of outlines, each along the edge of a thin, ragged fill. 3 are rows that moved when a neighbour’s ragged thin lines merged (M13.18), and one a row moved when the blocks sew in another order. The last is a straight move between 2 lines of one narrow part, which is a stitch because it is shorter than the collapse length. M13.17 looked for a hidden way for it and didn’t find one (runs in place of jumps). Before M13.21 the dogs laid 7 stitches astray. 2 of them were satin stitches 7.89 and 4.72 mm long that fanned across a shape biting into their column, and M13.21, which fills where columns sewed, removed those. The dogs’ moves astray are moves from one element to the next, as the button’s are. Their jump threads, 49 to 64, are those that no later shape covers: each thread sews its shapes one after another, and the PE800 cuts no jump. Each dog takes too long to digitize and measure in the tests’ debug build, so CI’s perf job runs these cases in the release build (conformance). The pieces of the dogs that showed fabric before the rules above, the paw of the dog on turf and the running dog’s ear, are cases of tests/auto_fabric.rs too.

Each thread threaded once

The owner’s machine has one needle, and each change of thread is a rethread by hand. Of the traced dogs she wrote on 2026-11-22 that each colour should be sewn in one go, “this is also because of depth: on a sewing, a shade is all one depth”. In her answers she added “Please expect 1 color is one rethreading, so 9 layers would be 9 rethreads”. So a design of N threads should be threaded N times. All of one shade then sews together, with N − 1 changes. M13.63 measured where the extra threadings come from and what sewing each thread once costs. How thick a design sews is still her first concern (the limit of 4 fills, SC-W0913), and a rule must hold on every design of the varied set.

The extra threadings today

The set has 77 designs: the 18 review drawings, the repository’s 30 SVG fixtures and its traced round patch, the 9 test pictures, the traced pear, the lettered patch, the digitized halves of TS-13A to TS-13E, the 4 traced dogs and 8 traced photos kept locally, 2 of them traced in 9 and 12 threads. The TS-13 sheets as a whole are left out: their drawn halves have settings and sew in paint order. A design is threaded once for each run of colour blocks in its plan. Where it is threaded more often than it has threads, the order’s rules are read as a graph of threads, with an arrow from one thread to another wherever an element of the first must sew before an element of the second. An order threads each thread once exactly when that graph has no loop, and each loop names the shapes that close it.

The other 65 designs thread each thread once. Of the 103 threadings beyond one a thread, 97 come from rule 2, a line after the fill it borders. A dark line of fur borders a lighter fill that lies on a dark fill. The dark fill sews before the light fill and the line after it, and the dark thread is threaded twice. In the traced photos lines of each shade border fills of the others, and rule 2 alone makes loops through up to 6 threads. On the coleus and the magnolia traced in 9 and 12 threads the search for the fewest changes ran out of work after 204,000 and 312,000 rows, and the order fell back to sewing each thread while it lasts: 47 and 48 threadings.

One threading comes from the base layer. It sews in the thread of the largest area, before every automatic element. The flower’s pink head lies over its green stem, so pink can’t sew first: the base layer sews pink, then the stem green, then the head pink again.

5 come from shapes truly drawn under and over another shade, in 4 designs of the 77: the house, and 3 traced photos. The house’s red chimney has a brown outline, and the red roof is drawn over the chimney’s foot and its outline, with a brown outline of its own. Red lies under brown at the chimney and over it at the roof. In the building a dark fill digitized whole lies under a grey part’s line, and a grey fill digitized whole under a dark part’s line elsewhere. None of the dogs, the pear, the test pictures, the fixtures or the other 17 drawings has a shade under and over another.

Each element of the set sews in one thread. The underlay of each element sews in its own thread just before its top stitches, so it doesn’t add a threading. No search ran out on the other 75 designs.

The candidates, measured

Each candidate was prototyped and the set planned with it, in the tests’ build at opt-level 1. The fabric is measured as the dogs’ cases measure it (stitchcraft_testkit::fabric), on the 69 designs that are planned quickly enough. The 2 photos traced in 9 and 12 threads are left out of the totals and named apart. (b) and (c) were measured with a first form of (d), whose base layer took the first thread of the free search.

  • (a) Rule 2 gives way, as rules 4 and 5 do. A line sews after the fill it borders where that costs no threading, and otherwise with its thread. Of the orders with the fewest threadings the search takes the one with the most lines after their fills, as it does for columns. The dogs are threaded once a thread, 5, 8, 5 and 8 changes where they had 8, 12, 8 and 11. The base layer still adds the flower’s threading. Alone, (a) starts every design in the base layer’s thread, and on TS-13B that put the black lines before the panes: the panes’ rows, pull compensation included, covered the lines 0.3 and 0.6 mm wide. The likeness measure doesn’t see that, since the lines are a small share of the black.
  • (d) The base layer sews in the thread the best order starts with. The search is made once from the largest area’s thread and once with no thread sewing. The base layer takes the largest area’s thread unless the free search finds an order with fewer threadings, or as few with more lines after their fills, and then the thread that order starts with. The flower’s base layer sews in green, and TS-13B’s in red: its black words and lines sew last, its lines after the panes as today. 0.066 % of its area is bare, where 0.069 % was, and 0.225 % with (a) alone.
  • (b) A fill sewn after a line ends short of it. A fill is cut back by its pull compensation, 0.4 mm, from a line sewn before it, so that its rows don’t cover the line. The lines show, but fabric opens beside them: the dogs’ bare share grows 1.6 to 3.4 times more, and the set has 2 slivers more. (a) alone sews TS-13B’s lines first, and with (b) as well its bare share grows from 0.07 % to 0.77 %. At 0.2 mm a traced bird sewed 6.6 fills thick. Reaching onto the line instead, half its width, covered the lines and cost the dogs up to 0.06 of their likeness. The proposal leaves both out.
  • (c) The lower shape of a loop is cut away. Where the graph of threads still has a loop, the direction of it whose arrows are all an automatic area under a shape of another thread, with the least overlap, gives way: those areas sew after the shapes on them and are cut away under them, as an abutting trace sews. Every design is threaded once a thread. The house’s red fills end at their outlines and its thickest spot falls from 3.88 to 3.63 fills, but a traced cat’s rises from 3.87 to 4.23. Kept as rule 2 and cut where loops close, (c) doesn’t reach the dogs: there rule 2 alone closes the loops, a dark line beside a grey fill and a grey line beside a dark fill.

The coleus and the magnolia traced in 9 and 12 threads are threaded 10 and 12 times with (a) and (d), where they were 47 and 48, and the search keeps 133 and 1,452 rows. The magnolia’s thickest spot falls from 4.52 to 4.00 fills. With one pass the search mostly takes one batch a thread, and it no longer runs out on these photos.

What is built

The owner asked for one threading per colour in so many words, and M13.63 builds (a) and (d), with a setting and 2 notices. Rule 2 and rule 3 of the sewing order now read:

  1. A line after the fill it borders, where that costs no thread change (REQ-AUTO-184). A line that touches a fill without overlapping it sews after the fill and covers its edge where the row of batches with the fewest changes allows it, as a column does by rule 4. Otherwise it sews in the batch of its thread, before the fill, and the fill’s rows end on it. With auto_thread_once off (REQ-AUTO-186) it always sews after the fill, and the whole order is the one before M13.63: the base layer keeps the largest area’s thread and rule 4 doesn’t hold for a part’s running line.
  2. The fewest thread changes. The base layer sews before the first automatic element, in the thread that the order with the fewest changes starts with (REQ-AUTO-185). The search is made once from the largest area’s thread and once with no thread sewing, and the base layer keeps the largest area’s thread unless the free order changes thread less, or as little and sews more lines and columns after their fills.

Rule 4 now holds for a part’s running line too: a satin column sews after a part’s running line of another shape that it borders, where that costs no thread change. Sewn first, the column reaches its overlap under the line’s part (REQ-AUTO-029). With one pass the running dog in 9 threads sewed a part’s column, then a part’s running line along it, on a fill, and the column’s overlap under the line made a spot of 4.15 fills, where main sewed 3.26 there with the column last. With the column after the line the spot sews 3.96 fills.

Thickness before threading (REQ-AUTO-189). A line sewn before its fill can raise a spot: the fill’s rows end over the line, where the last pass and the cap could take their ends under a line sewn after it. With auto_details_last on, 2 traced dogs sewed 4.07 and 4.18 fills that way, where main sews 3.96 and 3.99. The owner’s first concern is whether her machine sews the design. So a design threaded once that sews a spot above the warning’s limit is planned again with each line after the fills it borders, as with auto_thread_once off, the order before M13.63. When that plan sews at the limit or under it, it comes back, and SC-W0927 says how many more times the threads are threaded. Both dogs then sew as main sews them, each threaded 3 times more. A plan above the limit pays for the second plan only when the strict rules wouldn’t allow its order: where a line sews before a fill it borders or a column is held after a part’s line, and where the base layer has another thread than the largest area’s. An order they allow is itself a strict order with as few thread changes, so there is nothing to weigh. Main’s search may break a tie between such orders another way. That such a design sews as main does is measured on the set, not proved. The guard reads each plan’s thickest spot from the cap’s measure, so no plan is measured twice: measured again, the dog on turf in 6 threads took 7 % more instructions.

SC-W0925 (REQ-AUTO-187) names the shapes where a shade is drawn under and over another, and SC-I0926 (REQ-AUTO-188) counts the lines that sew before a fill of another thread beside them and names the 3 with such fills on the most sides, the smallest first. Both are found from the order and the shapes, with no raster. The notice SC-I0907 now says how many times the machine is threaded beside the thread changes: “threaded 6 times, with 5 thread changes, where paint order is threaded 9 times”.

Measured with the build

The whole set, 78 designs with the sew-out piece, planned with the build in the tests’ build at opt-level 1, and measured as above, on main with M13.62. The build sews as the prototype of (a) and (d) did on every design, but where rule 4 now moves a column after a part’s line. 9 designs sew above 4 fills at the default settings. The thickness guard plans 2 of them again, the goose and the magnolia, whose orders the strict rules wouldn’t allow. Sewn strictly they are above 4 too, at 4.37 and 4.52. Each keeps its first plan. With auto_details_last on, the dog on turf and the running dog in 6 threads fall back, each threaded 3 times more, and sew as main sews them.

What gets worse

  • What shows of a thin line. A line sewn before a fill beside it loses up to the fill’s pull compensation, 0.4 mm, under the fill’s rows. Measured on each running line of the set, as the share of its top thread that no later stitch covers. On the 4 traced dogs 37 fur lines newly show less than half their thread, the worst 2, 13 and 13 %. 18 of the 37 that main hides as much now show more, so 56 lines show less than half where main has 37. On the traced magnolia and the traced coleus leaf, measured whole only for the build, 80 and 81 lines show less than half where main’s 19 and 30 do. No line of the drawings, the fixtures, the test pictures or the TS-13 halves does, and the likeness of every design measured is as it was. Round a fill on the sew-out piece’s dark fill, a line 0.3, 0.6 and 0.8 mm wide keeps 0.49, 0.82 and 0.92 of its thread, where with the setting off it keeps all of it (REQ-AUTO-188). Holding the fill’s pull compensation back to the line’s near edge gives the stitches of (b), which left more fabric bare. Held back to the line’s thread, our measure wouldn’t show a gap, but it doesn’t model the pull: on the machine the rows would pull in about 0.4 mm short of the line. So nothing is held back, SC-I0926 names where to look, and the sew-out piece decides.
  • Fabric beside the lines. An area reaches its overlap only under the shapes sewn after it (auto::neighbours::under, beside with near_later). Where a part’s running line sews before the areas beside it, none of them reaches under the line’s part, so the line’s whole part is left to the line, whose running stitch covers only its middle. Sewn after them, the line had the first area’s overlap under all of its part. On the dogs the bare share rises up to 2.8 times, with 1 sliver more, all under the limits. On the traced magnolia the cover’s check finds 3 new gaps of 0.13 to 0.61 mm² where main finds none. On the traced coleus leaf it finds 2 new gaps of 0.30 and 0.63 mm², and 3 that grow from 0.12 to 1.07, 0.74 to 1.20 and 0.19 to 0.65 mm². No design of the repository’s own set changes its count of gaps. Keeping the line’s part to the area, and keeping the line seen, is the next step’s.
  • The area above the cap. It rises on the dog on turf in 6 threads from 14.0 to 23.0 mm², in 9 threads from 8.0 to 10.0 mm², on the running dog in 6 threads from 11.0 to 13.25 mm², on the coleus from 6.5 to 13.5 mm² and on the magnolia from 16.5 to 26.25 mm², and falls on 4 designs. No design has an area at 4 fills or more, and none rises above 4 that was at or under it.
  • The base layer’s thread. On 5 designs the base layer sews in another thread: the flower in green, TS-13B in red, 2 dogs in white and one in tan. Under a slit it shows that thread, where it showed the largest area’s.
  • Jump threads. The machine cuts at a change of thread and not at a jump. Fewer changes leave fewer jump threads, but a thread that sews once crosses farther: the running dog’s brown jumps across its muzzle.

The lettered patch, the digitized halves of TS-13A and TS-13C to TS-13E, and the fish whose eye M13.61 keeps on top sew as they did: only the designs named above change.

Open points

  • A shade at 2 depths. 5 threadings are left in 4 designs. The house has 2, and a traced building, a traced cat and the coleus have one each. SC-W0925 names the shapes so that a person can change the drawing, and the house with its hidden outlines cut away sews each thread once. A later step could sew them once too. Candidate (c) cut away the lower shape where a loop of threads closes, and every design of the set was threaded once a thread. Its rule chose the shape that loses its depth by the least overlap, and the traced cat then sewed 4.23 fills thick where it sewed 3.87. The step needs a rule for the depth under which every design stays at or under the cap, measured on the set.
  • The lines a fill sews over. A remedy would end the fill’s rows short of the line’s thread without letting the fabric show where they pull in. The sew-out piece shows whether the lines read and whether fabric shows.
  • The fallback is for the whole design. Sewing only some threads’ lines after their fills could keep a design under the limit at fewer threadings.
  • Fill tips and line ends at one point. Where the tips of several fills meet the ends of running lines and their locks, the cap can’t take the thread today: it moves a row’s end or joins 2 stitches, and these are neither. A fill or a line sewn earlier raises what later fills lay there. Taking the row ends over a line sewn before back to the line’s edge, tried for this step, didn’t lower such a spot. The cap’s step owns it.

The sew-out piece. A navy fill 56 mm by 40 mm, broad enough to be digitized whole, a cream fill 28 mm by 20 mm drawn on it, a navy line 0.8 mm wide round the cream fill, abutting it as a traced line abuts its fill, and a gold square beside them (docs/src/plan/machine-testing.md › MC-8: the kit). On main navy is threaded twice, since the navy fill sews before the cream fill and the line after it. With one pass the line sews with the navy fill, and the cream fill’s rows end on it. Sewn both ways side by side, it shows what the trade costs on fabric: whether the line still reads as a line and whether fabric shows beside it.

Details last

(REQ-AUTO-096 to REQ-AUTO-098, M13.40.) A digitizer sews an eye, a nose or a mark on a patch after the fills round it, so that no fill’s rows lie over it. StitchCraft can’t tell an eye from a speck of noise, and no rule may know what a picture shows. A detail is known by geometry and order alone. It is an automatic area that meets a larger automatic area without overlapping it, and that the shapes round it close in on. The strip one tolerance wide round its region lies under what the other shapes paint, grown by twice the shapes’ tolerance, for 3 quarters of its area or more (order::Order::closed_in). An eye or a mark has neighbours all round it. A patch at the picture’s edge is open along it, and a patch of fur is larger than the shapes inside it.

What the layers’ rule missed. Neighbours already digitizes an area wider than a satin column whole under the details in its holes, and moves it back before them. That rule missed 4 cases, each shown on main before M13.37 by a case of tests/auto_details.rs:

  1. A detail where 2 or more patches meet lies in the hole of none of them, and the rule left it where it was drawn. The cases draw a mark 12 mm square where 2 patches meet.
  2. An area no wider than a satin column keeps its holes. Where it sews as a fill, as a ring 3 mm wide round a square did, the square in its hole sewed first. Since M13.37 such a ring sews as satin columns, which sew after the square as an outline does, and the square reaches under them: no miss. A ring that asks to sew as a fill sews before the square with the setting off too, and the case stays to keep it so.
  3. An area that can’t move back past a shape with settings near it opens its hole again, and the detail sewed first (REQ-AUTO-097). A stop keeps every element on its side of it, so a detail drawn before a stop still sews before an area drawn after it.
  4. A fill sewn after a detail eats its edge. On the marked patch, sewn on main, the black dot measures 5.5 mm where it is drawn 6 mm, and the diamond 11.7 mm where it is drawn 12 mm: the later fill’s rows and their pull compensation reach over them.

Wishes to the search. Each detail sews after the larger areas it meets where that costs no thread change, as a satin column does after its fill (rule 4 above). Of the rows of batches with the fewest changes, the search takes the one that sews fewer details early. A detail then waits for a later batch of its thread. The owner’s machine has one needle, and each thread change is a rethread by hand. As hard rules, details last took the traced photos in 6 threads from 8 thread changes to 13 and 14. In 9 threads the search ran out of its budget, and they changed thread 35 and 38 times.

Off by default (REQ-AUTO-098, M13.48). The rule runs only when the design’s lowest automatic shape has auto_details_last on. Off, the order skips the search for details, and a design sews as it did before M13.40. The rule landed off, and waited for one cap on thickness at the end of planning (M13.35). Reordering fills can raise the thread at a spot where no detail lies. A fill that moves for a detail starts and ends elsewhere, and its own travel runs elsewhere under its rows. It also reaches under other neighbours. On one traced photo the thread over one spot rose from 4.69 fills to 5.49 by one wish: the fill that moved crossed the spot 3 times more with its own travel, 9.50 mm of its thread in a box 1 mm across where it had 3.76 mm, with the same stitches. On another the wishes changed which of the rows with equally few changes the search took, and a fill sewn later retraced a row. Nothing in the shapes’ geometry told the wishes that do this from those that help. Leaving out the wishes of details whose rim the larger area covers less than a share, for every share tried from a quarter to all of it, kept one photo at its old thickest spot and raised another. A check that plans twice and keeps the better plan, or a cut-off by the number of areas, would only hide that. An area count can’t even tell a photo from a logo: a traced photo can have fewer areas than a badge. Once the cap limits every spot, a reorder can’t pile thread anywhere, and so M13.45 turned the setting on by default. M13.48 turned it off again, for its jump threads (off by default again, below).

A detail inside a detail (REQ-AUTO-108, M13.45). A detail can have details of its own, as a pupil on an iris or a window in a door does. With both settings on, a traced dog’s grey area that the darker fur closes in on moved to sew after the fur, while the tan patch on it, which would cost a thread change to move, stayed where it was. The grey’s outline and rows then lay over the tan’s edge. So a detail moves only together with what sews on top of it. A wish moves its second element later. Its nest is that wish, each wish that something sews after that element, a detail of it or a satin column bordering it, and the nests of those. The search grants a nest whole or not at all. It also keeps the row itself from doing the same. Of the rows with the fewest changes it takes the one that misses the fewest inner wishes, those whose first element another wish moves, and only then the one that misses the fewest wishes. So wherever a row with as few changes keeps a pupil on top, the search doesn’t take one that sews the iris after the fur and leaves its pupil under it. A nest goes by the shapes’ sizes and where they meet. It doesn’t need a second plan or a measure of what shows. The cases in tests/auto_details.rs and order/fewest/tests.rs draw a pupil on an iris where 3 patches meet.

Making every smaller area that a moving detail meets one of its followers, as the nest’s own wishes are, was measured and not taken. With more inner pairs to weigh in the choice of the row, the search took a row that left the dog’s tan patch under the grey’s edge again. The small areas of the dogs that show less, by each area’s own pixels that show its thread (fabric::shown), were as many as before.

Cost. Counted in instructions with callgrind (stitch export --auto, a build without link-time optimization, 2026-11-18). Off, every picture of cargo xtask perf writes a machine file byte for byte the same as before M13.40, and its count moves by 0.24 % or less, within what a build’s layout moves it. On, the pear takes 8.3 % more, the dog on turf in 6 threads 13.6 % more and the running dog in 9 threads 9.8 % more.

Open question. Where a detail sews before a fill, the fill’s rows and pull compensation reach about 0.25 mm over its edge, and a later step could end them at the detail’s edge.

Both on by default (M13.45, REQ-AUTO-103). With the cap merged, the owner turned on details last and merging by likeness, at 0.85 (merging by likeness), on 2026-11-18. Each design of the repository sews as before, stitch for stitch, but the traced dogs and the marked patch drawn abutting, which now sews as the stacked one does. The machine file holds as many stitches as the plan. The dogs keep their thickest spots within the cap, and all 4 sew less area at 3 fills or more. The lines kept and the details moved cost the dogs 4 to 8 jump threads and the photos 60 to 274. More small areas show more than show less: the details now sew on top. What got worse:

  • Each local photo changes thread once or twice more. A line kept sews after the fills it borders, which can bring its thread back. Which lines did it was not traced.
  • The coleus keeps a spot at 4.01 fills, where it kept 4.22, and SC-W0913 names it. It also leaves 2 slivers, and its bare share rises from 0.006 % to 0.053 %, within the cover’s limit of 0.5 %.
  • 2 to 14 % of the small areas show less. On the running dog in 6 threads a patch of 16 mm² shows 2.2 mm² less: an area of 108 mm² beside it now sews after it. The search took another row of threads with as few changes, to grant wishes elsewhere, and that area’s thread now sews later. A nest binds only what a wish moves, not a whole thread’s batch that a change of row moves. Traced by the order of the 2 areas alone.

Planning takes longer where lines are kept, since each line is measured as a part: by the clock on a shared machine, the local photos but the magnolia took 1.8 to 2.2 times as long. Counted in instructions by CI, the dogs’ exports took 10.6 to 13.3 % more and the pear’s 9.4 % more before nests, and the test sheets as much as before (conformance/perf.toml).

These leave every limit as it was, since a limit comes from the whole set.

Off by default again (M13.48, REQ-AUTO-098). Shown the cost of each setting alone on the traced dogs, the owner kept merging by likeness on and turned details last off by default on 2026-11-19. Measured at opt-level 2 with M13.45’s nests, each dog with merging by likeness on: the jump threads with details last off, then on. The last column counts the areas under 20 mm² that details last makes show 0.1 mm² or more more, and less, with the sum of each, as the table above does:

Merging by likeness alone costs the dogs 3, 4, 0 and 0 jump threads, and raises the likeness of the 2 dogs where it costs. Details last adds 1, 4, 7 and 7 more and leaves the likeness as it is. The owner’s machine trims no jump thread within a colour, so each is cut by hand. Most of the cost is the row of threads: to grant a wish, the search takes another row with as few thread changes, and a thread’s whole batch then sews later. That is the cause M13.45 traced on the running dog in 6 threads. The setting stays, for a design where an eye or a mark must keep its edge clear. It comes back on by default once the search’s tie-break no longer moves a whole batch of a thread later for a wish, and the dogs show a clear gain for it.

A small detail on top

(REQ-AUTO-182, M13.61.) The owner reviewed 18 drawings sewn by automatic digitizing on 2026-11-21. Of the fish she wrote: “biggest issues is the black part of the eye”. The eye is a white disc 4.4 mm across on the body, and the pupil a black disc 1.8 mm across on the eye. It sewed as a wheel of white stitches that all ran in to the pupil, and the black showed only between their ends. The rule she was shown was: a small shape drawn over another sews last, whole, and can be seen.

What happens today, measured. Every filled shape of 50 mm² or less that lies at least a tenth over a shape painted before it in another colour was measured, on main 1952f48c, in 63 designs: the 18 drawings, the fixtures’ SVG files and documents with the 4 traced dogs and the pear among them, 6 traced photos and the TS-13A to E sheets. A shape is seen when its own thread is on top on at least half of what shows of it, each stitch drawn 0.4 mm wide in sewing order. Its stitches run in rows when at least 0.4 of its top stitches longer than 0.5 mm run within 20° of their commonest way. A wheel’s run every way. A probe kept out of the repository measured both from each plan, and tests/auto_small_details.rs holds them as cases.

CauseDrawings, fixtures, sheets (39)Dogs and pear (111)Photos (820)
A ring round a detail sewn as one satin column, a wheel1, the eye: the pupil seen on 0.40nonenone
Sewn before the shape under it2: the pupil, a snowcap (seen, 0.83)9, all seen13, 2 not seen
No wider than 1 mm, so one running line1, the camp’s pole: seen on 0.354, all seen56, 5 not seen
Merged: a speck under 1 mm², or a thin line or a like colour into a neighbournone93, 21 not seen720, 529 not seen
Other: whole, after the shape under it35, 1 not seen (a line on TS-13B, 0.47)5, all seen30, all seen

The merged shapes are the tracer’s noise, merged on purpose (SC-I0905, SC-I0915). The stars, dots and rays on top all sew whole and in rows, after what they lie on. The night sky’s dots of 1.2 to 3.2 mm² are small fills of 21 to 33 needle points, and the flower’s middle and TS-13E’s dots are fills too. The wheel is the eye’s alone among the small shapes. The same cause sews 2 larger shapes badly: TS-13D’s white disc round its red star sews over the star’s points, and TS-13A’s blue badge, a square round a gold disc, fans into its corners and shows fabric between its stitches.

Why the eye sews as a wheel. The layers are flattened first (REQ-AUTO-023). The eye sews only what shows of it: a ring round the pupil, 0.9 to 1.7 mm wide. Areas that the rules fill are digitized whole under their details, with their holes closed, but a narrow area keeps its holes, as an outline round a letter does. The ring is narrow and sews as one satin column round the pupil. Its inner rail is the pupil’s edge, 0.5 to 1.3 mm from the eye’s centre. Pull compensation takes the inner ends 0.4 mm further in, to 0.13 to 0.9 mm from the centre: 40 stitch ends on a circle 3 mm round. The column would sew 2.4 mm of its outer rail to 1 mm of its inner one. A column that crowds more than 2 to 1 is filled (REQ-AUTO-068), but a part with a hole isn’t measured, because a fill in its place would sew in pieces round what fills the hole. Then the pupil, a compact fill, sews before the eye. Only a fill moves back before its details, and a column round a detail goes by the order’s rules 2 and 4 (see sewing order). So the white’s ends cover 60 % of the black, and the eye’s middle is the fish’s thickest spot, 3.55 fills.

The cap takes no small shape. Planned with the cap lifted, the 960 small shapes that sew gain 368 of 9,492 needle points (3.9 %). Most are lock stitches and travel. What shows of each changes by 0.09 of its area at most, either way, on lines of the photos. The pupil keeps 25 of its 32 points and is seen on 0.40 either way. The cap and the last pass don’t hide a small shape: the order and the wheel do.

Candidates, measured on all 63 designs with a prototype that was never committed. Changes are against main, and the second column counts the small shapes seen.

a. The crowding bound for a ring round a detail. The eye’s ring then crowds, its holes close under the pupil, and the eye, a disc 4.4 mm across, is compact and filled whole. It sews in rows (0.26 → 0.73 of its stitches one way), before the pupil, and is cut away under it as any area digitized whole is. The pupil shows whole, sewn after it, and the eye’s thickest spot falls to 3.23 fills. Of the 63 designs only 4 change. TS-13D’s white disc becomes a fill under its star, whose points then show, and TS-13A’s badge a fill under its disc, with no fabric between its stitches. On the goose photo one part of 0.92 mm², merged as a speck before, sews 28 stitches of its own. The photo’s area above the cap falls by 1 mm². Held to rings whose holes other areas fill, the rule keeps a ring on bare fabric a column, as a case shows. A fill in that ring’s place would sew in pieces round the hole, and that was the reason rings weren’t measured. Prototyped first for every ring, the bound changed the fish and the 2 sheets alone.

b. Details last on by default. It moves 1 more small shape after the shape under it, the alpine snowcap. The snowcap lies in a notch of its ridge, and the layers’ rule looks only at holes. It costs 25 jump threads, most on the dogs, and raises their area above the cap. That is the cost M13.48 measured when it turned the setting off (see Off by default again above). It stays a setting.

c and d. Whether a small shape is seen depends on its stitch. A fill or a column shows whole down to the night sky’s smallest dot, 1.2 mm² and 21 needle points. A speck under 1 mm² merges into its neighbour on purpose. A shape no wider than the narrowest satin stroke sews as one running line, which covers about a third of a shape 1 mm wide. Sewing a line as wide as it is drawn is the queued step “line weight by drawn width”, for lines and narrow shapes alike, not this one.

M13.58 and the eye. M13.58 keeps a column’s stitches full length and spaces them on the inside of a bend. The eye’s ring is all bend, 5 to 1 between its rails once compensated. Its inner ends crowd more: measured with M13.58’s prototype, the pupil is seen on 0.23 and the eye’s middle sews 3.99 fills. M13.58’s rule alone makes the eye worse. With rule a there is no ring left to bend, and the 2 rules don’t meet.

Phase 1 proposed rule a, a ring whose holes other automatic areas fill held to the crowding bound, as it measured it on 63 designs. The owner said yes on 2026-11-22, with no setting.

The build measures the bound from end to end of the rails. It ran the engine’s own test pictures too, and one of them showed what the 63 designs didn’t. The lettered patch is a red plate with a cream field stacked on it, and shown, the plate is a border 3 mm wide round the field. Measured by a stretch, as REQ-AUTO-068 measures a part without holes, the border crowds at each of its cut corners, more than 2 mm of its outer edge to 1 mm of its inner one. Rule a filled it whole under the field: the patch lost its satin border, sewed 108 more stitches, and its thickest spot rose from 3.98 to 4.00 fills. So the ring is measured from end to end of its rails. It closes its holes only when one of its rails, the whole way round, is more than 2 times as long as the other, a wheel.

Open points, measured and left to later steps. TS-13D’s white is a ring whose hole is the star, so its width varies between the star’s points and its notches. Its column sews after the star it borders, and its inner ends are pulled over the star’s points. That is not this step’s test: a wheel is. Measured with M13.59’s prototype (a part whose width varies by more than 0.3 filled whole), the ring, 2.87 mm wide on average and varying by 0.39, is filled whole under the star. The round fills’ outlines are polygons of chords up to 1.1 mm, which lie 0.08 to 0.09 mm inside the circle, under a thread’s width.

Runs in place of jumps

Between 2 shapes of one thread the needle jumps, and a machine with one needle cuts no thread at a jump: each is a thread to cut by hand. Where the stitches sewn later cover a way from one shape to the next, plan assembly sews a run along it instead (REQ-AUTO-053, M13.17, stitchcraft_engine::moves). The needle neither ties off nor jumps. The run is a row of travel stitches of the element it leads to, 2.5 mm long at most, and the shapes sewn after it hide it. Assembly decides it, because only there is it known where the needle is and whether a move is sewn on or jumped (REQ-ASM-002). Only moves between elements that automatic digitizing made are looked at. Elements with settings keep their jumps, as Ink/Stitch sews them.

What covers is read from the stitches as generated, and not from the shapes: a fill with rows far apart covers nothing, whatever its outline says. Every stitch of the top layers is painted 0.4 mm wide on 2 grids of 0.2 mm. One records, for each cell, the last fill or satin column whose thread covers it. The other records the last stitches of any kind, whose colour is what shows there. Underlays and the base layer are not painted. A cell is hidden from a move when a fill or satin column sewn after the move covers it and every cell within 0.4 mm of it. The run then lies as far inside what covers it as an underlay lies inside its fill, clear of the ends of the rows, where a fill covers least. A cell is also hidden where the stitches that show are of the run’s own colour and sewn after the move. The outline that follows a fill is such: the run lies under it as the first pass of a double line.

The ends. An element ends on its own stitches and starts at its own edge, so a run can’t be hidden from its first millimetre to its last. Within 0.8 mm of each end, less than the 1 mm at which a stretch of thread counts as showing (REQ-AUTO-026), it may cross cells of its own colour sewn before, and cells covered later without the 0.4 mm to spare.

The way is the shortest over the cells a run may cross, found by a search that goes on from the way that is cheapest so far with what is still ahead. It steps to the 8 cells round a cell and counts in whole tenths of a cell. Equal ways are taken in a fixed order, which gives a design the same runs on every machine. A run goes round what would show it, up to twice the move and 10 mm in length. The search stays within 10 mm of the box of the move’s ends. The way is then pulled straight wherever the straight line keeps to those cells, and each straight piece is sewn in equal stitches, with a needle point at every corner, so that no stitch leaves the way.

A move that is sewn on, within the collapse length, stays a stitch unless it would show for 1 mm or more on another colour or on bare fabric. Then it becomes a run where a way is hidden. Where none is, it stays a stitch. The roadmap’s row had it become a jump there. Measured, that left each traced dog 5 to 15 jump threads more, each no longer than the stitch it replaced and harder to cut than the stitch is to live with, so the stitch stays and tests/auto_photos.rs goes on counting it.

Work. Painting and the searches share the work budget once for the whole design. Each cell of the grids costs 100 units. The default budget then allows a design of 450 mm by 450 mm, and a larger design gets the same memory at most. A stitch costs a unit for each cell of its box, and a search costs a unit for each cell of its box and for each cell it looks at. When the work runs out, the moves from there on are sewn on or jumped as they were, and SC-I0914 names the element where it ran out.

Measured before it was built (2026-11-15, main 399f82b7). The traced dogs left 76, 84, 73 and 96 jump threads, each a jump between 2 groups of one thread. Of those, 8, 9, 17 and 22 had a way hidden but for its ends, and 14 to 23 more had a way that shows only on its own colour. For the others every way crosses another colour that nothing later covers. No order of the shapes could leave fewer than 53, 58, 43 and 50, counted with every shape of a thread’s run still to sew and free to start and end anywhere. The lines that sew after the fills they border, and the last thread, have nothing sewn after them. The traced pear’s 2 jumps have no hidden way, and of the 6 of the badge web/templates/alpine-night.svg one has.

Measured as built.
