The dogs sew 18 to 52 stitches fewer than before, because the needle sews on into a run and out of it without a lock. Their thread changes, their slivers and their stitches astray are as they were. TS-13A, TS-13C and TS-13D sew as they did: their shapes of one thread lie apart on bare fabric.

Alternatives measured. A run on top of stitches of its own colour would replace 14 to 23 more jumps on each dog. It would show as a line across the fill, and a travel stitch that shows is astray (REQ-AUTO-026). With 0.2 mm to spare in place of 0.4 mm the dogs left 0 to 5 jump threads fewer, with runs under the very ends of a fill’s rows. With a limit of 4 times the move and 20 mm, one dog of the 4 lost 2 more jumps, for 90 mm more thread. An order that sews next the shape with a hidden way to it could come nearer the 43 to 58 jumps that no order betters. The order is chosen before a stitch is generated, and what covers is read from the stitches, so that is a step of its own.

Settings

The rules’ values are StitchCraft’s own parameters, registered as any other (parameter registry, reference), since M13.7 (REQ-AUTO-020). Each rule goes by the settings of the shape it digitizes (stitchcraft_engine::auto::AutoParams):

The settings come in 3 tiers. The Auto effect’s dialog shows the first 2, and the third is the rules’ bounds, which stitch plan --set KEY=VALUE sets on every shape digitized automatically, as a document can on one. The rules’ reference lists every setting with its default and its tier, generated from the registry. The table below gives the dialog’s settings and the rules each one changes. The advanced settings are the rules’ bounds and the limits of the measures, and each is described with its rule on this page.

TierSettingKeyRules
A shapeStitchauto_stitchthe stitch the shape sews as, where its parts can: left at auto, the rules choose
A designSatin or fillauto_preferleans the least slenderness, the most a width varies and the narrowest piece of a fill together, by half
A designFill uneven partsauto_fill_unevenwhether a part of several lines whose width varies is filled
A designFill angleauto_fill_anglethe angle of every fill made of the shape
A designOutline round fillsauto_fill_outlinewhether each fill sews an outline, read of the lowest shape
A designOverlapauto_overlap_mman area reaching under its neighbours and cut away under them, a satin column past a rail that a shape sewn after it runs along
A designPull compensationauto_pull_compensation_mma satin column past a rail with no shape sewn after it beside it, and its end under another column
A designLeave out the backgroundauto_ignore_backgroundthe background, read of the lowest shape
A designBase layerauto_base_layerwhether the design sews one, read of the lowest shape whose settings read, as its other settings are
A designBase layer’s stitch lengthauto_base_stitch_length_mmits rows’ stitches and their spacing
A designBase layer keeps holesauto_base_keep_holeswhether the outer shape keeps its holes

A design’s settings are a shape’s too: VectorCraft’s Auto effect on a group or a layer sets them on every path inside it. The Auto effect’s dialog held 13 rows after M13.16 and was 903 px tall in a window of 900. With the bounds and the 2 limits of the measure left out it holds 12. M13.28 gave the outline’s switch a row, and the base layer’s offset became an advanced setting to make room. No picture so far has needed that fine adjustment changed, which is matched to the pull compensation, where the base layer’s switch, its stitch length and its holes each change what one sees.

The defaults are the values on this page, and MC-8 confirms or changes them. A shape that sets none of the settings takes the defaults, and an automatic shape with a setting that doesn’t read sews nothing (SC-E0101). Each area reaches its own overlap under the shapes sewn after it, and its specks are its own parts smaller than its smallest area, wherever they go. The narrowest satin column is the design’s min_satin_stroke_width_mm, one value for the whole design, and the underlay of a column’s width (above) is no setting.

An automatic element reads the settings before it is sewn as any stitch type, and the ordinary elements it becomes don’t (parameter registry). They are in no stitch family’s dialog, and a shape that sets them is still automatic. VectorCraft’s Auto effect (M13.7) sets them from its dialog for the paths it is on and those inside the group it is on, and with --auto every other shape takes the defaults.

Previews in VectorCraft

The Auto effect is a live effect like the plug-ins of each stitch family (plug-ins). VectorCraft runs a group’s effects on each path inside it (REQ-VC-006), so the effect on a traced group previews every path’s stitches, each sewn as its width says. A live effect is given its path and its paints alone. Like stitch export, it reads the path as an area when a fill paints it, and as a line along its topmost stroke. Its preview shows each shape’s stitches without the overlaps, the merged specks or the order, which stitch export adds. VectorCraft draws what a live effect returns with every paint of its object. The stitches come back as gaps in the area that the fill fills when a fill paints the object, and as lines that its stroke draws otherwise.

Digitizing is most of a preview’s work, from about 1,450 instructions a unit to 2,260 in VectorCraft 0.18.0’s host, and a live effect has 50 million (live-effect previews). A square’s takes 12,900 units, an L’s 21,000 and a ring’s 31,800. The auto level digitizes within eight tenths of the budget, then shows each element made as auto shows its family’s: a fill’s top rows, and a satin column’s and a running stitch’s every stitch. When that costs too much it shows the outline, as a larger square, an L, a ring and most traced letters do. Exact shows every stitch, and outline the path, a fill’s shape whole. A preview that costs more than the level allows shows less, with SC-I0810, as the other effects’ previews do. The command line’s preview always shows every stitch.

Tracing again replaces the group with a new one, without the effect. Apply the effect after the last trace, or use --auto, which works without it.

With VectorCraft’s newer plug-in features

  • Exporters (0.14, P1). File › Export › Export As › PES plans the document inside VectorCraft, and its auto option digitizes the objects without effects (M6.9). The flow is then trace and export, both in VectorCraft.
  • Effects in filter objects (0.15, P8). A filter could set on each path the effect of the family its width chose. The user then adjusts it in that effect’s dialog.
  • Budget classes (P4, still proposed for live effects). The Auto effect’s previews of large shapes would get the heavy budget.

Text in pictures

Text traced from a picture is shapes, and the rules for shapes sew its letters: strokes as satin columns, small parts as running stitches. Small letters come out ragged, and neighbours that the tracing joined sew as one blob. An embroidery font is drawn for the needle, and lettering sets text in one (fonts). M14.5 puts lettering where a picture’s text is. A person marks the text and says what it reads, since nothing here reads a picture. Spike M14.6 looks for a way to find and read the text without a marker. A drawing’s text that is still a text object sews without a marker: from M14.7 automatic digitizing letters it (text objects in automatic digitizing).

The pass is stitchcraft_svg::lettering::pictures. It works on a design once a reader has made it and before anything is digitized (REQ-AUTO-063 to REQ-AUTO-066). What is left of the traced shapes is then digitized as if the letters had never been traced, with its overlaps and its base layer.

The marker

A marker is a shape named stitch:text and the text, such as stitch:text SAMPLE PATCH. The name is an object’s name in VectorCraft’s Layers panel, and an object’s label in an SVG file, Inkscape’s inkscape:label. Both readers already give a shape’s name to its element, so one pass serves both. The marker needs a fill or a stroke, since a reader is given only objects that paint. It is never sewn.

The marker isIts areaThe text
a closed shape, such as a rectanglewhat its outline encloseslies level across it
an open line with a strokewhat lies within half the stroke’s width of the line, between its endsfollows the line, the letters’ tops to the left of the way the line is drawn

A line is for text on an arc, as round a badge. It is drawn through the middle of the letters in the direction the text reads, with a stroke wide enough to cover them. The line says which way the letters stand, which the letters’ shapes alone don’t say. A straight line drawn at a slant sets slanted text the same way. Finding the arc from the letters without a line was left out: it is a guess where a drawn line is not, and M14.6 decides whether text is found without help at all.

A font other than the default, and lettering’s trims and sorted colours, follow the text in double quotes: stitch:text "SAMPLE PATCH" font=geneva_simple trim=word. The font is named by its folder, as a Lettering effect names one. It is found where the reader’s fonts are (lettering in VectorCraft). The default is the font built into the plug-ins. A setting that doesn’t read is named with SC-W1113, and the marker is still used. The scale and the spacing are found from the traced letters, so a marker doesn’t set them.

From the command line, --text LEFT,TOP,WIDTH,HEIGHT:TEXT marks a rectangle (REQ-CLI-020). The 4 numbers are percent of the box round the design’s shapes. A traced picture’s background is traced too, so that box is the picture, and a share of it can be read off the picture whatever size it sews at:

stitch plan patch.png -o patch.pes --text '20,35,60,30:MATCH'

The text after the colon is written as a marker’s name writes it. An option marks level text only. For text on an arc, --traced writes the traced document, a line is drawn on it in VectorCraft, and the document is planned. A picture with a transparent margin measures from what was traced, not from the picture’s own edge.

Which shapes are the letters

Every subpath that lies wholly inside the marker is taken out, of every element. A subpath is one closed outline. Image Trace keeps all the areas of one colour in one compound path, so a letter is a subpath of its colour’s path, not an object, and the hole it leaves in the colour round it is a subpath of that colour’s path. Taking both out removes the letter and closes the hole. An island of the background’s colour inside a letter, its counter, goes the same way. What is left of the background is its own outline, so it sews whole under the lettering. A picture drawn in layers has no holes to close, and its letters are taken out as whole objects.

So the background is told from the letters by reaching outside the marker, and by nothing else. Colours are not compared, since a traced edge has specks of other colours along it.

A subpath that crosses the marker’s edge stays as it was traced. When more than half of its points lie inside, it is probably a letter partly outside the marker, or a letter the tracing joined to something outside, and SC-W1114 counts those. Points are those of the outline flattened within 0.05 mm (stitchcraft_engine::design::mark::FLAT): a piece a tenth as tall as the shortest letter an embroidery font sews still measures within 2 %.

Size and place

What was taken out is measured along and across the line the text follows. A closed marker’s line is level, so along is across the page and across is up. The text is set once at the font’s own size and measured the same way. Then:

  • Height. The scale, to a tenth of a percent, makes the box of what the text sews as tall as the box of what was taken out. The box holds parts that reach below the baseline, so a p in the text is measured with its tail.
  • Baseline. The text’s box is centred on the traced letters’ box across the line. The baseline lies as far above the box’s bottom as the text’s lowest point lies below the baseline.
  • Width. Every gap between 2 glyphs, a gap between words too, is widened or narrowed by one amount until the text is as wide as the traced letters. The amount is at most half the letters’ height wider and a tenth of it narrower. Past half a height a word reads as separate letters. Closer than a tenth, the strokes of neighbours sew over each other. The text is then centred on the traced letters along the line.
  • Along an arc. The baseline is the marker’s line moved sideways to the baseline’s distance, each corner of it pushed out along the line that halves the corner. Lettering along a path then places each glyph on it and turns it whole (along a path).

The scale stays within the heights the font sews at. Letters shorter or taller than that are set at the nearest height the font sews, centred on the traced letters, and 2 warnings say so: lettering’s own SC-W1106, with the font’s range, and SC-W1115, with both heights and both widths in millimetres. SC-W1115 is also said when the text ends up more than a tenth of the letters’ height from their width. The traced letters are not kept in such a case. Text at a height the font sews well reads better than a blob, and the picture shows the difference.

A font that sews in one thread takes the thread that painted the most of what was taken out. A hole counts against its thread, so the background, whose holes are as large as the letters, never wins. A font of several colours keeps its own. The lettering’s elements sew after the last element a subpath was taken from, which puts them on the background. SC-I1112 names each marker with the count of outlines taken out, both heights, the text and the font.

Markers that can’t be used

Nothing is taken out before the lettering is set. A marker without text, one that encloses no area, one with nothing wholly inside it, and one whose font isn’t found, doesn’t read or draws none of the text’s characters are each named by SC-W1113 with the reason. The traced shapes then sew as they were, so a design is never left with a hole and no text.

What it sews

Measured as stitch plan --auto plans each picture, in Ink/Stitch Small Font, which sews from 3.6 to 15.2 mm. The thickness numbers are M13.29’s (how thick a design sews):

The thickness doesn’t get worse. Lettering lies on a whole background where traced letters were stacked columns that overlap at their joints. The background has no overlap to reach under a letter any more.

Small Font’s strokes are thinner than the bold block letters of TS-13B. At 15 and 10 mm the traced block letters sew well as they are, and the lettering looks lighter than the picture. At 6 mm and below, and for the emblem’s letters, the lettering is the one that reads. From M14.8 a second, bolder font is built in, and the font is chosen by the letters’ weight.

The font chosen by the letters’ weight

The owner asked on 2026-11-16 why lettering that replaces big letters looks lighter than the picture. The stitches were right. The one font built in was a light one. M14.8 measures how bold letters are and builds in a bold font to choose by that measure (REQ-AUTO-080, REQ-AUTO-081).

The measure is the width of a stroke over the height of a letter, found the same way on a traced outline and on a font’s satin columns (pictures::weight). Each closed outline is taken at 48 places evenly spaced along it. From each a ray goes straight into the shape, as far as the next edge. The side that is in is the one where the ray meets the outlines an odd number of times. The stroke’s width is the median of those distances. The long sides of a stroke outweigh its ends and its joints, whose rays run along the stroke and find its length. So an I and an O of one pen measure alike, which the area over the outline’s length doesn’t give.

  • Traced letters are the subpaths taken out of the thread that painted the most of them. The holes the letters leave in the colour round them belong to another thread and aren’t measured. A letter drawn as a line is as wide as the line. The height is the one the lettering is fitted to.
  • A font is measured on its capital letters A to Z. Each satin column’s 2 rails are closed into an outline. The height is the median height of what each letter draws. Both are as drawn: a column’s pull compensation and the thread’s own width add to what sews, for traced letters as for a font’s.

Measured so, at the fonts’ own sizes, for the fonts of Ink/Stitch’s library at c7e3a05 that came into question:

The block letters are drawn with strokes 18 percent of their height wide (testsheets::pictures::word), and the measure finds that. Small Font is a third lighter than they are. Barstitch Bold is within a twentieth of them.

Why Barstitch Bold. It is the nearest in weight to the block letters among the clean faces. It has capital and small letters and 157 glyphs. Its licence file has a copyright line and no reserved name (what was checked). 2 fonts as bold sew smaller, and neither was taken:

  • TT Directors sews from 9.3 mm, which would hold the patch’s 10 mm letters. It has capital letters only. Its licence file names a designer and no copyright holder, and says the font comes from a demo of a typeface that is sold. The OFL’s notice needs a copyright line to carry.
  • Кирилиця, drawn from Roboto, sews from 7.9 mm and has a copyright line. Its files are 3.4 MB, more than twice Barstitch Bold’s, and an export that letters in it would read a glyph file of 2.5 MB.

TT Masters, from the same designer, sews from 9.0 mm, and its licence file is the same as TT Directors’: a demo of a typeface that is sold, and no copyright line.

Manga Impact and Masego are display faces, and bolder than the block letters by as much as Small Font is lighter.

The choice (pictures::chosen). A marker that names a font is set in it. Without a font name, it is given one of the fonts the reader’s host offers, which are the fonts built in that the host has (in VectorCraft):

  1. The fonts whose heights hold the letters’ height come first. When none holds it, the fonts that sew nearest to it take their place.
  2. Of those, the font nearest in weight to the letters is chosen, and the earlier in the list when 2 are as near. Letters without a width to measure take the earliest.

The height comes before the weight because a font sews only at the heights it was drawn for. Bold letters set taller than the picture’s would run into their neighbours and past the marker. A font’s heights are those of its capital letters: how tall it draws them, times its least and its greatest scale.

SC-I1118 names the marker, the letters’ height and weight, and the font with its weight, and says which of 3 things happened. It gives weights to 2 places, as the fonts’ are written down: 0.13 and 0.19.

The lettersSet inThe note says
15 mm, strokes 0.18Barstitch Boldit is the nearest in weight of the fonts that sew at that height
15 mm, strokes 0.14Small Fontthe same
10 mm, strokes 0.18Small FontBarstitch Bold is the nearest in weight and sews from 12.5 mm, and leaving the text unmarked sews the traced letters
40 mm, anyBarstitch Boldno font here sews at that height, and this one sews nearest to it

The fonts’ numbers are written in stitchcraft_vectorcraft::lettering::HELD, since a plug-in chooses a font before it reads one. A build that has the fonts measures them again (REQ-FONT-022). With fewer than 2 fonts offered there is nothing to choose, and the marker is set in the default font without a note.

Bold letters from 6 to 12.5 mm are the gap. Barstitch Bold doesn’t sew that small, so Small Font sets them at their own height. They look lighter than the picture, as before this step. The note says from what height the bold font sews. Traced block letters sew well as shapes from about 10 mm, so from 10 to 12.5 mm the person can leave the text unmarked. Below 10 mm the lettering is the one that reads, light as it is.

A text object has no traced letters. Its Lettering effect sews in the font it names, or in the default font. Since M14.8 a bold text object sews in the bold font where that font sews its height (text objects in automatic digitizing).

Left for later. M14.9 built widen_percent for bold letters under 12.5 mm (below), and its automatic choice waits for the owner’s sew-out. The effect’s dialog has no list of fonts.

Bold letters under the bold font’s heights

Barstitch Bold sews capital letters from 12.5 mm, the least scale its font.json gives, 0.5, times the 25.0 mm it draws them at. Lettering keeps a scale within the font’s min_scale and max_scale with SC-W1106. Lettering on patches is often 6 to 12 mm tall and bold, and there a marker or a bold text object gets Small Font’s strokes whatever the drawing’s weight. M14.9 measured the gap on the 62 letters and digits of both fonts, and 4 ways to close it, on 2026-11-20. The probes are kept outside the repository, beside the hand-off of the step.

The measures, of each letter set alone and on a field 3 mm round it, with M13.50’s cap:

  • the stroke as sewn, the median length of the satin columns’ top stitches with their pull compensation, given in millimetres and in heights of the capital letters;
  • the narrowest column: the least of a letter’s columns’ median widths;
  • counters: the holes in the thread’s cover, 0.5 fills or more on a grid of 0.1 mm, against those the font sews at a large height. A counter is open to read when a circle 1 mm wide fits it, the narrowest satin column of digitizing;
  • letters that run together: the parts of the cover of a sheet of 9 lines against the letters on them;
  • the thickest spot, and the area at 4 fills or more on the field.

Today. Small Font sews every letter at every height from 5 to 14 mm. No stitch is under 0.3 mm, and no letter’s columns run into the next letter’s. Its pull compensation, 0.2 mm on each side, is the same at every height, so it sews heavier for its height the smaller it is set. Of the 22 counters of its letters:

Barstitch Bold at 13 mm sews strokes 3.06 mm wide, 0.24 of the height, with 19 of its 21 counters open to read, 5.78 fills at its thickest on a field and 64 mm² at 4 fills or more. 18 of the 64 letters of the sheet touch the next one there, 8 at 16 mm. The font’s spacing is drawn tight, and its 0.3 mm of pull compensation on each side closes it at its least height.

So the gap is narrower than the heights say. At 6 mm Small Font sews strokes 0.19 of the height wide, as heavy for their height as the traced block letters are drawn (0.18). The difference grows with the height, to 0.15 against Barstitch Bold’s 0.24 at 12 mm.

The candidates. Each was planned at every height from 5 to 14 mm. At 6, 8, 10 and 12 mm, stroke in heights, counters open to read (of 21 for Barstitch Bold, 22 for Small Font), counters closed, the area at 4 fills or more on a field, and the letters of 64 that touch the next:

A column widened by a percent gains that part of its width on each side, the effect of Ink/Stitch’s pull_compensation_percent added to the glyph’s own. Every candidate sews its columns 1 mm wide or more from 6 mm up and its stitches 0.3 mm long or more, and every letter plans. The thickest spot on a field stays between 5.0 and 5.9 fills in all of them. Barstitch Bold sews 5.78 at 13 mm today.

Where each fails:

  • A and A2 run the letters together. Barstitch Bold’s letters touch at 13 mm already, and below it more of them do: 35 of 64 at 10 mm with A2, 40 at 8 mm. The counters of a and e close under 9 mm in A, and in A2 every counter is still a hole down to 7 mm but under 1 mm wide, too small to read once sewn. A wider letter spacing would part the letters and make the word longer than the traced one it replaces.
  • B closes the 4’s counter at 8 and 9 mm, and leaves 14 counters of 22 open to read at 8 mm against 16 today. It doubles the area at 4 fills or more against B2.
  • B2 keeps today’s counters from 8 mm up, 16 of 22 at 8 mm and 21 at 12 mm, and keeps the letters apart. Under 8 mm it closes the A and the 4 and leaves 3 of 22 open to read at 6 mm, against 8 today. It is a little lighter than Barstitch Bold, 0.20 to 0.22 of the height against 0.24.

A third font. Ink/Stitch’s library, read at c7e3a05, has no bold font for 6 to 12 mm. Its small fonts end at 8.4 to 9.0 mm by their own ranges, and its medium ones start at 9 to 12. Glacial Tiny 60 AGS (OFL) is the boldest small one, strokes sewn 0.22 of the height at its greatest size, 8.4 mm by its range, and sews 5.4 fills at its thickest alone and 6.8 on a field, with 559 mm² at 4 fills or more on a field at that size. Excalibur Small says it is in the public domain, and its narrowest columns sew 0.53 mm wide. None of them was measured for weight by M14.8’s measure, and none is a better start than the 2 fonts built in.

What the trade does. Digitizers put the narrowest satin column at about 1 mm, the needle’s width, and 1.5 mm in a font made for embroidery (ASI, ZSK, Embrilliance). Columns of small letters are widened, the counters opened and the underlay left out or kept to a centre walk (Impressions). A quarter inch, 6 mm, is the usual least height of text in 40-weight thread, and finer thread or a run-stitch font goes below it, a continuous run at about 3 mm (Ignition Drawing). A font’s least size, such as 6 mm for a serif text font, is given with the font (Hatch). No source gives a least size for counters. The 1 mm used here is the narrowest column’s.

The design (REQ-FONT-024, REQ-AUTO-140, REQ-AUTO-141). The setting is built, and off by default. widen_percent is a lettering setting of StitchCraft’s own beside Ink/Stitch’s, from 0 to 50 and 0 by default. With 20, a satin column 1 mm wide sews 0.2 mm wider on each side: lettering adds the value to the pull_compensation_percent of every satin column it places. Running stitches and fills don’t change, and a value outside 0 to 50 is set at the nearest with SC-W0102, as the parameters’ registry holds a value in its range. A glyph’s own value that doesn’t read stays, for the planner to refuse. At 0 a text sews as it did before. stitch lettering --widen-percent, the Lettering effect’s dialog and its record take it by that name.

The 2 automatic choices (REQ-AUTO-140, REQ-AUTO-141, planned) wait for the owner’s sew-out to confirm their limits:

  • A marker with letters bolder than their font and too small for a bolder one has its text widened. The percent is 50 × (the letters’ weight over the font’s, less 1), rounded and at most 20. A widening under 5 % is left out. Letters under 8 mm are set as today, since a widened Small Font closes its counters there. SC-I1118 gives the percent, or says why the letters aren’t widened.
  • A bold text object that the bold font can’t sew at its height sews in the default font widened as far toward the bold font’s weight, with the same limits and the same note.

B2 opens as many counters as today’s Small Font from 8 mm up, measured on all 62 letters and digits, and that sets the 2 limits, 20 % and 8 mm. A sew-out confirms them or moves them, as it does a profile’s values.

The build’s numbers. Measured on 2026-11-20 with the setting itself, on Small Font’s 62 letters and digits. Each cell gives the stroke in heights, the narrowest column in mm, counters open to read of 22, the thickest spot alone and on a field in fills, and the area at 4 fills or more on a field in mm²:

They match prototype B2. Small Font’s glyphs don’t set a pull compensation in percent, so the prototype’s value equals the setting’s. All 22 counters of the set are holes in the thread, though the A, the B, the a, the e and the 4 keep theirs under 1 mm, and at 8 mm and 20 % the R joins them. The 64 letters stand apart, and the narrowest gap between 2 letters falls from 1.5 to 1.0 mm at 8 mm. Every stitch is 0.3 mm or longer.

A finding for the sew-out. Barstitch Bold’s letters touch at its least heights today, as measured above. This step leaves its spacing as it is, and MC-8 shows whether the touching shows in thread.

Decisions for the owner

Each has a recommendation, which the step took.

  • A name marks the text. The marker is a shape or a line with a name, as every other marker in a VectorCraft document is. A dialog of its own would need a filter, and a filter can’t keep a shape’s settings before VectorCraft 0.15. Recommended: the name.
  • A line through the letters for an arc. The other choice is to find the arc from the letters, which can’t tell which way they stand. Recommended: the drawn line.
  • Letters no font sews as tall are set at the nearest height the font sews, with 2 warnings. The other choice leaves them traced. Recommended: lettering, since small traced letters are the ones that sew worst.
  • The limits of the spacing, half a height wider and a tenth narrower, are starting values that no sew-out has confirmed. The lettered patch that machine testing sews both ways from M14.5 can show them.
  • --text in percent of the design’s box. Pixels of the picture are the other choice, and need the picture’s size, which stitch didn’t read when this was decided. Recommended, and taken: percent.
  • A bolder font built in. Decided on 2026-11-16: the owner asked for one, and M14.8 built in Barstitch Bold.
  • Bold letters from 6 to 12.5 mm, which the bold font doesn’t sew, are set in Small Font at their own height, with a note. The other choices are to leave them traced, which sews badly under 10 mm, and to set them in the bold font at 12.5 mm, taller than the picture has them. Recommended, and taken: the light font and the note.
  • Bold letters from 8 to 12.5 mm (M14.9). The recommendation is Small Font with its columns widened (bold letters under the bold font’s heights), by at most 20 % and from 8 mm. Its counters open as today’s do, and its letters stand apart. Barstitch Bold set below its least height is bolder and runs its letters together. A third font from Ink/Stitch’s library is the other choice, and none of them sews at 9 to 12 mm by its own range. MC-8 sews the candidates side by side.
  • A text object’s bold weight. Recommended in M14.5: it doesn’t choose the bold font, so documents sew as they did. M14.8 changed that, and a bold text object sews in the bold font where it can (text objects in automatic digitizing).

Marks that keep parts

Automatic digitizing decides by geometry and measures alone which small shapes are specks to merge (step 4 of Neighbours) and which thin lines merge into their neighbours (step 3, and merging by likeness). Details last decides by geometry too which parts sew last. Geometry can’t know which small part a person cares about. The same speck is noise in one picture and the point of another, such as a bird’s eye or the dot over a letter, and in other pictures a flower’s centre or a window. M13.39 lets the person say so with a mark, which works on any subject and names none.

The rule

A mark is an area of the design. Automatic digitizing sews the parts that lie wholly inside it by their width, where it would merge them, and changes nothing else (REQ-AUTO-104 to REQ-AUTO-107).

  • Which parts. The parts of the automatic areas as the layers leave them once flattened (step 2), each one connected piece of one area with its holes. The speck and line steps look at these pieces. A part is inside a mark when every point of its outline is, flattened within 0.05 mm as a text marker’s subpaths are.
  • Specks. Automatic digitizing doesn’t merge a marked speck into the area beside it, or leave it out. It sews by its width, as a larger part does. Narrower than the narrowest satin stroke everywhere, it is a running stitch along its middle, and wider, satin columns or a fill. A speck of 0.8 mm², 1.6 mm by 0.5 mm, sews a running stitch 1.6 mm long.
  • Thin lines. A marked thin line sews as a line in its own thread, whatever the likeness bound (auto_min_likeness).
  • Colour. Merged, a part sews in its neighbour’s thread, and marked, in its own. The threads of a picture are the trace’s, and a mark doesn’t change them.
  • Partly inside. Automatic digitizing decides a part that crosses the mark’s edge as if there were no mark, since a part cut at the mark’s edge would sew an edge the picture doesn’t have. A person who wants it drawn draws the mark larger.
  • Left alone. The rules of the background, the flattened layers, the widths, the order, the overlaps, the base layer and the cap on thickness go as without a mark. The order they find can differ, since every part sewn is a shape more (what marks sew). A marked part reaches under its neighbours, and they reach under it, as for any part. A speck or a line outside the mark may still merge into it. Details last stays its own setting: a marked detail sews last when auto_details_last is on, as it would unmarked. The mark doesn’t name a stitch. For another stitch, a person gives the part its own effect, as for any shape.

One mark covers what the row of M13.39 asked: a marked part’s lines and specks don’t merge, and it sews by the rule of its width. A second mark, to merge what the rules sew, isn’t proposed, since the pictures measured didn’t need one.

Drawing a mark

A mark is a shape named stitch:keep, or stitch:keep and a note of the person’s own, such as stitch:keep left eye. The name is read as every marker’s: in any case, after any white space. Its area is read as a text marker’s is, by the same code. A closed shape’s area is what its outline encloses. For an open line with a stroke, it is the band within half the stroke’s width of the line, between its ends. A line suits a whisker or a twig. The mark needs a fill or a stroke, since a reader is given only objects that paint, and a rectangle or an ellipse drawn with a stroke and no fill shows the picture under it. It is never sewn.

  • In VectorCraft, the person draws the mark with VectorCraft’s own tools over the traced picture and names it in the Layers panel. VectorCraft’s plug-in ABI v1 has no canvas tools. A group or a layer so named is not a mark, and SC-W0807 says so (REQ-VC-031).
  • In an SVG file, the mark is an element whose inkscape:label is the name.
  • From the command line, --keep LEFT,TOP,WIDTH,HEIGHT marks a rectangle in percent of the box round the design’s shapes, as --text does, read by the same code (REQ-CLI-021). The marks are named keep-1, keep-2 and so on, and a bug-report bundle keeps them with the rules’ settings.
stitch plan bird.png -o bird.pes --keep '62,18,6,5'

Read when the design is planned. The roadmap’s row planned an Apply Marks command. Instead a reader takes the marks out of the design’s elements and gives them to the design, as it reads text markers, so stitch, File › Export and the bug report each read them on every plan. A command would copy what the names already say, and its result would go stale when the picture is traced again. The Auto effect’s preview shows each shape alone and doesn’t merge, so it looks the same with a mark or without one (previews).

Where it runs

The readers take each mark out of the design’s elements and give it to the design, with its id (stitchcraft_engine::design::mark::Mark, mark::taken_out). An object painted with a fill and a stroke is read as 2 elements of one path, and is one mark. The reading of a marker’s area lies in the engine (design::mark::Area), and the text markers of stitchcraft_svg::lettering::pictures use it from there. Planning reads each mark’s area once (auto::marks), with the budget’s work to itself. Automatic digitizing tests a part against the marks where a step would take the part away: in the width rules, which leave a speck in its area’s fill (auto::narrow), and in the steps of thin lines (auto::neighbours::lines) and of specks (auto::neighbours::specks). The step of thin lines tests every part of every automatic area, so that each mark knows what it holds. Testing a part costs a unit for each mark, and where the box round the part lies in the box round the mark, a unit for each of the part’s points and each segment of the mark, charged to the budget that step has for the part. A part that needs more is decided as if there were no mark, and SC-I0916 says so. Without marks, nothing is tested and nothing is charged.

SC-I0916 names each mark with the parts that lie wholly inside it, and how many of them sew that would have merged, as specks and as thin lines. SC-W0917 names a mark that encloses no area, a mark with no part wholly inside it and a mark in a design without an automatic shape, which then sews as without it.

What marks sew

Measured on 2026-11-19 with the build, on main with M13.35’s cap and before M13.45, so with both auto_min_likeness and auto_details_last off (stitch plan --auto’s numbers, as M13.41’s measure and M13.29’s count them, and the stitches of the PES file read back). No picture is a goal: one procedure marked each, never an eye. Each picture was planned without marks. Its 3 largest parts that the plan loses (likeness) were then each marked by the box round them, grown by 0.5 mm, as a person draws a rectangle round an eye. A picture that loses none was marked round its 3 smallest parts, and TS-13A round 3 of the specks it merges.

Castlenone0.43481 of 1914.00221.7554826,20126,2011,334116

  • The marked parts sew. Of the 48 parts the likeness measure finds inside the marks of the dogs and the photos, 47 sew. The other, a part of 5 mm² in the goose, sews as a line that shows less than half its area. Each photo’s likeness rises, by 0.007 for the coleus to 0.044 for the running dog in 6 threads.
  • The pictures whose marks merge nothing sew as without them, stitch for stitch: the pear, the lettered patch, TS-13B to TS-13E, the badges and emblems traced either way, the button and the marked patch. No part lies wholly inside a mark of the lettered patch or inside any of TS-13C’s 3, since the part the likeness measure sees is a piece of a larger area, and SC-W0917 says so. TS-13A sews all 7 of its marked specks as short running stitches, with 5 more jump threads, 1 more thread change and 50 more stitches. 96 of its stitches far from the marks start or end elsewhere.
  • Outside the marks, a traced photo sews in another order. Each marked part is an element of its own, and the order of the whole design (sewing order) is found again. Of the stitches more than 2 mm from every mark, 51 to 1,394 of a dog and 1,629 to 7,468 of a photo start or end elsewhere. So the area at 3 fills or more moves both ways, from 6.75 mm² less on the dog on turf in 6 threads to 48.5 mm² more on the magnolia.
  • The cap holds but for one spot. Every picture sews at most 4 fills where it did, but the goose: a spot about 19 mm from its marks sews 5.09 fills, where one shape of grass lays 2.6 fills by itself, and SC-W0913 names it “above the cap”. The coleus sews 4.22 fills with marks and without them.
  • Marked specks cost jump threads. The boxes round the parts of a traced photo take in specks of the trace’s noise too: 32 to 84 of them in a photo’s 3 marks, and 4 to 13 thin lines. They cost the photos 15 to 45 jump threads. The dogs’ marks took in no speck, and cost 2 to 4.
  • What it costs to plan. Without marks nothing is tested, and every picture sews as on main. With the pear’s 3 marks, stitch export --keep took 1,074,680 instructions more of 3.18 billion (0.03 %), testing every part of every area against the marks, and wrote the same file. With the marks of the running dog in 6 threads it took 3.4 % more, 29.38 billion, for the parts that now sew and the new order (callgrind, a build at opt-level 1).

Decisions

The owner’s choices of 2026-11-18, each the one recommended. The owner confirmed the 4th, that marks keep specks too, on 2026-11-18.

  • One mark, stitch:keep. The row asked that a marked part’s lines and specks don’t merge, and that it sews by its own rule. That is one rule, so one mark does both. Other names were stitch:detail, which suggests the order of details last that a mark doesn’t change, and stitch:favour, which doesn’t say what it does. Chosen: stitch:keep.
  • Read when planned, with no Apply Marks command, as text markers are. Chosen: no command.
  • Wholly inside. Automatic digitizing decides a part crossing the mark’s edge as if it were unmarked. One other choice sews a part when most of it is inside, which is a guess. Another cuts a part at the edge, which sews an edge the picture doesn’t have. Chosen: wholly inside, as text markers take their letters.
  • Marks sew specks too. The row asked that a marked part’s specks don’t merge, and a dot under 1 mm², such as the light in an eye, is a speck. A mark also takes in the specks of a trace’s noise, which cost the photos 7 to 39 jump threads and no likeness. The other choice sews marked thin lines and merges specks. Chosen: sew specks, since a mark is the person’s word. SC-I0916 counts the specks, so a person can draw a tighter mark.
  • A marked speck sews by its width, usually a short running stitch. The other choice is a fill, which sews a knot under 1 mm². Chosen: by its width.
  • The rest of a photo may sew in another order. Each marked part is a shape more to order. Holding the rest of the order fixed would need a second order for marked designs. Chosen: accept it, and let M13.35’s cap bound the thickness, as for details last and likeness.
  • Details last stays its own setting. A mark could also sew its parts last. That moves fills, which can thicken a spot elsewhere, as M13.40 measured. Chosen: marks don’t change the order. Details last is its own setting, off by default since M13.48.

What isn’t built

  • The Auto effect’s preview in VectorCraft shows each shape alone, so it can’t show what a mark sews. The exported file and its preview picture do.
  • A mark that merges what the rules sew, or that names a stitch.

A guide on one shape

Automatic digitizing chooses the rows of each fill by geometry: every fill has the rows of auto_fill_angle. No rule can know the form that the rows of fur or a ribbon follow. The person draws it. Before M13.24 a guided fill needed the shape taken out of automatic digitizing by hand, which lost its overlaps with its neighbours, the base layer under it and its place in the sewing order. A guide keeps all of them, and works on any subject without naming one.

The rule

A guide is a line the person draws over the picture. Each filled part of an automatic area that the line crosses sews as a guided fill whose rows follow it. The other parts and the steps after them sew as without the guide (REQ-AUTO-109 to REQ-AUTO-111).

  • Which parts. The parts the rules by width leave filled (stitch types by width): the broad parts of an area, its compact and uneven parts, and the broad pieces of a part split by width. A part follows a guide when a stretch of the guide’s line lies inside it where the area shows. An area digitized whole under the details that fill its holes (neighbours, REQ-AUTO-023) shows only round them, so a guide drawn over a detail steers the detail and leaves the area under it alone. Specks, under the smallest area auto_min_area_mm2, stay in the area’s fill and merge as before.
  • The parts it crosses. Unlike a mark, which keeps the parts lying wholly inside it, the guide steers the parts it crosses. The guided fill lengthens the guide straight on at both ends until its rows cover the whole part (guided fill). A guide that crosses only a corner of a large part steers all of it. The person sees that in the exported file’s preview and draws the guide again.
  • Parts sewn along their middles keep their stitches. A satin column already lays its stitches across its width, which follows the part’s form, and a running stitch follows its centre line. Such a part sews the same stitches when a guide crosses it, and SC-I0918 counts it.
  • 2 guides on one part. The part follows the guide drawn last, the one a person sees on top. Blending 2 guides would guess what the person meant.
  • One element for each area and guide. The parts of one area that follow one guide are one element, since a guided fill sews several parts nearest the needle first. These elements come after the area’s other elements, its fill and its parts sewn along their middles, in the order the guides are drawn, so that the other elements keep their names. The first element of an area keeps its name and the others are numbered, so a guided fill is the area’s own element when nothing else of the area sews. The area keeps its thread.
  • Left alone. The background, the flattened layers, the specks and the thin lines go as without a guide, and so do the rules by width, the overlaps, the base layer and the cap on thickness. The guided fill takes its own defaults and the area’s other settings. The base layer keeps its rows across auto_fill_angle, so under a guided fill it lies at an angle that changes along the rows. Each element a guide makes is a shape more to order, so the order can change (what guides sew).

The guide’s line

The guide’s line is its first subpath, as a guided fill reads its guide with the copy strategy, its default. Its stroke’s width is ignored. A closed guide is opened where it closes, as a guided fill opens it. Other subpaths are ignored.

Drawing a guide

A guide is a shape named stitch:guide, or stitch:guide and a note of the person’s own, such as stitch:guide left petal. The name is read as a mark’s, by the same code (design::mark::note), and the readers take guides out of the elements beside the marks (mark::taken_out). It is never sewn.

  • In VectorCraft, the person draws the guide with the Pen or Pencil tool over the traced picture and names it in the Layers panel. VectorCraft’s plug-in ABI v1 has no canvas tools. stitch:guide is the name VectorCraft documents already give a guide grouped with the shapes it steers (the markers table). A path so named keeps that meaning for shapes with settings, and is also a guide of the design for every automatic area it crosses, wherever it lies in the layers. A traced picture lies in one layer, and a person draws all its guides in that layer. A guide its group doesn’t use is no longer said to be unused (SC-W0807) when the design has an automatic shape, and an automatic shape grouped with a guide alone is no longer told that its markers are unused. SC-W0919 names a guide that steers nothing (REQ-VC-032).
  • In an SVG file, the guide is a path whose inkscape:label is the name. Ink/Stitch’s guide line, a path with its marker grouped with a shape, keeps its Ink/Stitch meaning and guides only that group.
  • From the command line, --guide X,Y,X,Y… draws a guide through 2 points or more, in percent of the box round the design’s shapes as --keep reads its numbers, joined by straight lines. A few points serve, since the guided fill smooths its rows. The guides are named guide-1, guide-2 and so on, and a bug-report bundle keeps them with the rules’ settings (REQ-CLI-022).
stitch plan leaf.png -o leaf.pes --guide '20,80,45,50,60,20'

What the notices say. SC-I0918 names each guide with the areas and the number of parts that follow it, and the parts it crosses that keep their stitches. SC-W0919 names a guide that crosses no filled part of an automatic area, whose line has no length, or that is in a design with no automatic shape. The design then sews as without it. The Auto effect’s preview in VectorCraft draws straight rows until a live effect is given its document’s other objects (the RFC’s P6). The exported file and its preview picture follow the guide.

Where it runs

Planning reads each guide’s line once, beside the marks (auto::marks). In the rules by width (auto::guides), each filled part that is no speck is tested against the guides’ lines, as a running stitch is cut to its clips (normalize::region::cut::inside). Each pair of a segment of a line and a side of the part costs a unit of work of the area’s element. A design without guides tests nothing. The guided fill then sews as it does for a shape set by hand.

What guides sew

Measured on 2026-11-19 with the build on main at 7486520b, built at opt-level 1, by the numbers of M13.41’s measure and M13.29’s count, and the stitches of the PES file read back. No picture is a goal: one procedure drew every guide, never a form, and phase 1’s prototype measured the same guides. Each picture was planned without guides. Its 3 largest filled parts were then each given a guide along their longest axis, the line between the 2 points of the part’s outline farthest apart, bent so that its middle lies a fifth of its length to the left, through 25 points. A guide drawn so crosses other parts too, as a person’s would. The last column counts, among the elements of the areas that the guides miss, those that sew the same stitches as without guides.

  • The parts follow their guides. Each guide steered up to 16 filled parts, the parts it was drawn along and those it crossed on the way. The rows bend with the guide in the preview pictures, and the export sews what the plan says, stitch for stitch.
  • The repository’s drawn designs sew the rest as before. TS-13A, TS-13B, TS-13E, the badges, the emblems and the button sew the same stitches in every element of the areas the guides miss. Elsewhere the order changes. Each element a guide makes is a shape more to order, so the rest of a traced photo or dog starts and ends elsewhere. Thread changes rise by up to 3, on the running dog in 6 threads.
  • The cap holds. No guided picture sews above 4 fills where main doesn’t, and the coleus, which sews 4.12 fills on main, sews 4.01. The prototype sewed the goose at 4.02 and the castle at 4.28, where parts of one area that rounding left a hair apart each reached under the other once a guide made them 2 elements. Such fills stay one area for the overlaps (REQ-AUTO-110). The area at 3 fills or more grows on most pictures, by up to 82 mm² of the magnolia’s 521 and 50 mm² on TS-13D, since a guided fill sews more stitches than the tatami fill it replaces: its copies lie closer where the guide bends.
  • The fabric is covered as before. The prototype left up to 0.23 % of a design bare, at the edges where the guided rows ended square on: automatic digitizing keeps a fill’s turns as stitches (turn_stitches), and the guided fill didn’t read it, so every second row started a needle point inside the edge. It reads it now (guided fill). What is left is at most 0.018 % more of a design, on TS-13E, and 0.009 % on a dog, where a few rows meet the outline at a slant and end short of it, far under the limit of 0.15 %.
  • A guide over a detail leaves the area under it alone. The badge’s preview showed a guide drawn over its disc steering the field round it too, since the field is digitized whole under the disc. The guide’s line is now tested where the area shows.
  • The likeness doesn’t move, by at most 0.001, since a guide doesn’t change a part’s colour or place.
  • What it costs to plan. The guided fills cost more or less than tatami fills by their stitches, and the test against the guides is a small share of it. With the prototype, planned and measured with its 3 guides, the pear took 1.2 % more instructions than without them, and the running dog in 6 threads 4.1 % less (callgrind, opt-level 1).

Decisions for the owner

Each with the recommendation, which the step took on 2026-11-19. The roadmap’s row no longer says the area is grouped with the guide.

  • Which parts follow: those the guide crosses. The roadmap’s row planned an area grouped with the guide. A traced picture is hundreds of shapes in one layer, and a person can’t easily group one of them, and in VectorCraft a guide in that layer is already grouped with all of them. The other choices are the parts wholly inside the guide’s stroke, read as a mark’s band, which leaves out the large parts whose rows show most, and the parts of the shapes grouped with it. Recommended: the parts it crosses, drawn over the picture as a mark is.
  • One name, stitch:guide. VectorCraft documents already name a guide so. The other choice is a name of its own, such as stitch:rows, which would keep the 2 readings apart but gives a person 2 names for one idea. Recommended: one name, with both readings.
  • The guide drawn last steers a part that 2 cross. The other choices, the guide nearest the part’s middle or the one with the longest stretch inside it, are guesses a person can’t see. Recommended: the last drawn.
  • Columns and running stitches keep their stitches. The other choice sews a crossed part no wider than a satin column as a guided fill too. A column’s stitches already follow its form. Recommended: keep them.
  • No setting, the guided fill’s defaults. The guide is enough, as the row says. A person who wants other rows gives the shape its own effect, as for any shape. Recommended: no setting.
  • The rest of a photo may sew in another order, as for marks. Each element a guide makes is a shape more to order. Recommended: accept it, and let M13.35’s cap bound the thickness.

What isn’t built

  • A guide that steers satin columns. Their stitches already lie across the part’s width.
  • A guide with an area, which would steer only the parts inside it, or a guide’s stroke read as a band. The prototype’s guides did their work without them.
  • Turning the base layer to follow a guided fill.

Names of shapes

A traced photo has hundreds of shapes, and every manual control starts with the same question: which shape is this? A mark (marks that keep parts), a guide (M13.24) and a shape’s own effect all need the person to find the shape in VectorCraft. Before M13.25 a notice named a shape by its element id alone, vc:63:fill, which is the number VectorCraft keeps in the file. The Layers panel never shows that number. Roadmap step M13.25 gave each shape of a traced design a name that the Layers panel, the notices and the report all show (REQ-AUTO-125 to REQ-AUTO-127, REQ-VC-033, REQ-CLI-023), and said when a path’s own Auto effect replaces its group’s (REQ-VC-034, SC-W0920). The engine’s names module builds the names.

What VectorCraft shows

Read in VectorCraft’s source at 3f8d7a0, its .vectorcraft format and the Layers panel’s code. The app’s window wasn’t run: VectorCraft isn’t built here.

  • A row shows the object’s name. A node of a .vectorcraft document has an optional name. The Layers panel shows Node::display_name() (crates/doc): the name when there is one, else the kind in angle brackets, <Compound Path>, <Path> or <Group>. In a traced document no node has a name. It is a layer with one group in it, and in the group a compound path or a path for each shape. So a traced photo shows hundreds of rows that read <Compound Path>.
  • Search All finds by name. The panel’s search keeps the rows whose shown name, in lower case, contains what the person typed (list_rows in crates/ui-egui/src/panels/layers.rs). A name written into the file is what a person can search for. A query matches inside longer names, so fill 1 also finds Fill 12, Red.
  • A long name is cut at the row’s end. The panel paints the name clipped to the row, which loses its right end on a narrow panel. VectorCraft keeps at most 255 characters of a name.
  • A name stays a name. StitchCraft’s reader gives an element its node’s name already (Element::name), and a name that begins with stitch: is a marker. A name StitchCraft writes must never begin with it.

Seen on 2026-11-20 in VectorCraft’s own window, by the pinned release on CI (the shot vectorcraft-layers-names): at the panel’s default width, a row of a shape in the traced group shows about 20 characters, and cuts the rest with no …. With the thread first, Dark Olive column (1), of 21, lost its closing bracket, and Russet Brown column (1), of 23, showed only its first 18, without its number. So the kind and the number come first (the parts of a name). In the shot of the same pear with the kind first, 5 of the 6 names show whole, and Column 1, Russet Brown shows its first 20 characters, Column 1, Russet Bro. The accessibility tree doesn’t give the rows’ text. The probe says so and keeps the picture. Not checked: whether a person can read the names at the panel’s smallest row size.

The parts of a name

A name tells a person how the shape sews, which one of them it is and its thread, in that order: Fill 3, Red, Column 1, Deep Gold, Line 12, Black. The Layers panel cuts off a long name’s end. The parts that tell 2 shapes of one thread apart come first, in the 20 characters a row shows. The row’s picture shows the thread’s colour, and the thread’s name is the part a person can lose.

  • The thread is the thread’s name. A traced shape’s colour is one of the machine’s threads exactly, since the trace uses the library’s colours as they are, so its name is the palette’s name of that colour. A colour that no thread of the palette has is written as its hex code, #7d6f00.
  • How it sews is the way the shape’s main part sews: the part that keeps the id of its lowest element, which is an area’s fill, or its first narrow part when nothing of it is filled, or a stroke’s first column. A shape none of whose parts keeps that id goes by its first part in the sewing order. A fill of any method is Fill, a satin column is Column, and a running stitch or another stroke is Line. A shape that sews nothing is Left out, as the background the design leaves out is (SC-W0906), and so is one merged whole into its neighbours.
  • The number counts the shapes of that thread and that way of sewing in the Layers panel’s order, from its top row, 1 first. The top row is the object drawn last. A comma follows the number, so that a search for fill 1, doesn’t also find Fill 12, Red.
  • A shape is what the elements of one object make. Their ids differ only in their last part (vc:6:fill and vc:6:stroke are of vc:6), and the parts digitizing makes of them add # or ~ and more to an element’s id (vc:6:fill#2). A part has its shape’s name.

Measured on 2026-11-20 by stitch plan --auto --report on the repository’s 6 traced documents, the badge, the pear and the 4 dogs: the longest name has 22 characters (Column 1, Russet Brown, Left out 1, Dark Brown), and the kind, the number and the comma take at most 12 (Left out 10,). By the rule, the longest kind is Left out, of 8, and Expand’s Base layer, of 10. The longest name of a thread a trace can match has 15 (Wisteria Violet), and an applique entry of the Brother palette, which a trace never matches, 17 (Applique Position). The longest name is then Base layer 1, Applique Position, of 31. The kind, the number and the comma fit in the panel’s 20 characters for any number of up to 8 digits.

Where it sews. A name doesn’t say where the shape is in the sewing order. The order is chosen again at every plan, and one small edit moves many shapes in it, as the measures below show. So the report lists the shapes in their sewing order at each plan, each with its place, which the name doesn’t give.

How stable names are

Measured on 2026-11-19 with a probe test (not committed), on the repository’s traced badge, pear and 2 dogs and on 2 of the 4 local photos, a magnolia and a coleus. Each design was read with --auto, digitized, and for the first 4 also planned on the reference machine. Then each of 9 shapes, spread through it, was deleted, or painted in another of the design’s colours, and the names were made again. A count is of the other shapes whose names changed.

Planned twice unchanged, every design gave the same names: digitizing is deterministic.

Number countedBadge (3)Pear (5)Dog on turf (78)Dog running (92)
In the whole sewing order0 to 20 to 330 to 6018 to 70
By thread in the sewing order0 to 20 to 27 to 541 to 32
By thread and kind in the sewing order003 to 251 to 29
By thread and kind in the Layers panel’s order005 on average4 on average

The first 3 rows are for the deletion of 4 shapes. Deleting one shape reorders the sewing of shapes far from it, and a name with its place in the sewing order changes for up to 3 in 4 of the others. A number in the Layers panel’s order changes only for the shapes of the same thread and kind below the one deleted.

On the local photos, numbered by thread and kind in the Layers panel’s order, a deletion renamed 23 of the magnolia’s 424 other shapes on average. With the number counted by thread alone, without the kind, it renamed 45. The magnolia and the coleus, of 704 shapes, gave the same names when digitized twice.

The names as built were measured on 2026-11-19 with a probe test (not committed) that planned each design on the reference machine, at opt-level 2, and then planned it again with each of 4 shapes deleted, spread through it. Here every shape counts, those merged whole into their neighbours too, which are Left out. The longest names were measured with the thread first, Russet Brown column (1). Every name in the order of 2026-11-20 is one character shorter, a comma and a space in place of a space and 2 brackets.

DesignShapesLongest nameOthers renamed by a deletionPlan
Badge4180under 0.1 s
Pear62300.2 s
Dog on turf, 6 threads97220 to 102 s
Dog running, 9 threads107220 to 22 to 3 s
Magnolia2,0142684 to 43043 to 69 s
Coleus3,98525176 to 63184 to 104 s
Goose1,8132723 to 362104 to 136 s
Building1,4442610 to 289100 to 111 s

Every design gave the same names when planned twice, and no thread was named by its hex code: the traced colours are the machine’s threads. On a photo a deletion renames up to a fifth of the other shapes. A deleted shape changes which specks and thin lines merge into their neighbours, so shapes move between Fill and Left out, and the numbers of their thread below them move with them. Naming is one pass over the elements and the steps, and isn’t timed apart from planning. The trace’s own time wasn’t measured, since VectorCraft isn’t built here.

So the names are written once, when the picture is traced, and are then the shapes’ own names, as a name a person gives one is. StitchCraft never renames a shape that has a name. A shape the person draws later has none, and it is named, for that plan only, by the same rule with the next free number.

Places that show the names

One function of the engine makes a name (names::name), and one of stitchcraft-core gives the words that name a shape in any message (diag::called): its name in quotes, then its element id for a bug report, "Fill 3, Red", vc:63:fill. Planning gives each diagnostic about a shape with a name that name (Diagnostic::name), and its headline shows it. Every place below uses them.

  • The traced document. stitch plan picture.png --traced FILE writes each shape’s name into its node, and changes nothing else in the document (REQ-VC-033). The design that is planned is that document, with its names, so planning FILE again sews the same stitches and says the same notices.
  • The notices. A notice about a shape names it, and so does a warning of the reader. A line of stitch plan has the name before the id, info SC-I0912 ("Fill 3, Red", vc:63:fill): … (REQ-AUTO-127). The shapes at SC-W0913’s thickest spot are named the same way, each in brackets: ("Fill 3, Red", vc:63:fill) #ed171f 2.1. SC-I0916 and SC-I0918 are about a mark and a guide, which they name by its id as before, and they count the parts they keep or steer.
  • The report. Each diagnostic with an element has its name too, and a list shapes gives each shape’s name, id, thread, kind and place in the sewing order (REQ-CLI-023).
  • The bug-report bundle of a picture keeps the traced document with its names. A replay then names the shapes as the run did.

A path’s own Auto effect inside an automatic group

A group’s Auto effect makes everything inside it automatic with its settings. A path inside with an Auto effect of its own sews by its own settings alone (REQ-VC-016), since the nearest effects say how a path sews. VectorCraft writes an effect with every parameter, its defaults included (merged_params in its crates/effects, read at 3f8d7a0), so the path’s own effect stores every setting, and the reader can’t tell a setting the person chose from one left at its default. The group’s settings can’t be merged under the path’s.

The roadmap row said this happens without a word. A case showed that SC-W0807 said it, in the words for StitchCraft effects that reach a path from 2 levels: “it is sewn as the nearest say”. A person learns from them neither which of the group’s settings the path loses nor which path it is, since an unnamed path is only “the object” with its id. The warning SC-W0920 is said in place of SC-W0807 where an Auto effect lies inside an automatic group, with the path’s name and both values of every group setting that the path’s own effect sets otherwise, leaving out those the group keeps at their defaults (REQ-VC-034). The stitches stay as they are today.

Decisions taken

The owner took the 6 recommendations of phase 1 on 2026-11-19:

  • Names are written once, at the trace, and kept as the shapes’ own names. Names made again at every plan would follow the sewing order and change for many shapes after each edit.
  • The number counts in the Layers panel’s order, and the report gives the place in the sewing order beside the name.
  • The number stands in brackets, so that a search finds one shape.
  • The shapes left out are named too, as left out, since notices name the background.
  • The layers keep their order. Automatic digitizing cuts an area away under the areas over it in the layers, and the layers’ order is part of the design.
  • A new code, SC-W0920, says that an Auto effect inside an automatic group replaces the group’s settings, so that stitch explain can say what to do.

What isn’t built

  • SC-I0916 and SC-I0918 don’t name the shapes whose parts a mark keeps or a guide steers. The marks and the guides count the parts in digitizing, before the shapes have names.
  • The VectorCraft plug-in’s previews don’t show names. A document opened in VectorCraft has the names --traced wrote, and a path the person draws later has a name for each plan only.
  • VectorCraft’s own Layers panel isn’t changed.

Expand

Expand (M13.26) is the other way out of automatic digitizing. Marks, guides and names steer a design while it stays automatic. When no control gives what a person wants, Expand makes the design ordinary elements with ordinary effects, which the person edits one by one, as the automatic digitizing of other embroidery software leaves a design. The expanded design sews as the automatic one sewed, and it no longer changes when the picture, its marks or the Auto effect’s settings change. Phase 1 recorded this decision on 2026-11-20, with a prototype measured on 13 designs of the set of pictures, and the owner took each of its recommendations. The build followed the same day: stitch expand and auto::expand.

The decision

Planning already turns every automatic element into ordinary elements before anything is generated (automatic elements). Expand writes those elements out, each with the settings that say what it sews, and every later plan reads them as shapes set by hand. A second digitizer, or a translation of the rules into other settings, isn’t needed: the elements are the ones the automatic plan sews.

Planning does more after digitizing, by which elements digitizing made, and no setting of an element says so today. Between 2 elements that digitizing made, the needle runs under later shapes in place of a jump (REQ-AUTO-053). Every stitch of the base layer is underlay (REQ-AUTO-028). The cap at the end of planning thins the elements digitizing made and the lettering it placed, with the limits of the lowest automatic shape (REQ-AUTO-074).

Expand keeps them with one new setting common to every stitch effect, digitized_as: no, the default, for a shape set by hand, part for an element that sews as digitizing made it, base for a piece of the base layer, and lettering for lettering that digitizing placed, which keeps its underlay and its pull in the cap’s reach (REQ-AUTO-120, REQ-AUTO-121). Planning reads it from any element, so a person can also set it by hand, or turn it off on a part. The cap’s limits become design settings, kept with the document as its machine is (design settings). With them the expanded design plans to the automatic plan exactly (REQ-AUTO-142, REQ-AUTO-143).

The parts and their settings

Each part is an object, in the order the parts sew, and each object’s effect is its family’s (parameter registry). Ink/Stitch’s names are used where its parameters are the interoperability contract.

What digitizing makesThe ordinary elementIts settings
An area’s fill, with its angle, underlay and overlapthe area as digitizing sews it, which reaches under the shapes sewn after it and holds the specks merged into itthe fill effect, tatami, with angle, row_spacing_mm, max_stitch_length_mm, staggers, underpath, fill_underlay and turn_stitches
A satin columna line along the column’s centre, as wide as the column (REQ-SAT-016)the satin effect with satin_column, the underlay of its width, pull_compensation_mm, push_compensation_mm, the short stitches and the nearest start and end
A line, a narrow part and an outlinea line along the path the stitches followthe stroke effect, with bean_stitch_repeats for a triple run and running_stitch_tolerance_mm
A guided fill (REQ-AUTO-109)the fill, and its guide line grouped with itthe fill effect, guided
Lettering it placedthe letters’ columns and lines, as they sewas a satin column and a line
A part left out, the background or a specknothingnone
Lettering it placed for a markerits columnsdigitized_as = lettering
A piece of the base layera fill under the detailsthe fill effect, with digitized_as = base
The run from one part to the nextnothing: planning finds itdigitized_as = part on both
The cap’s thinningnothing: planning thins the plandigitized_as = part, and the design’s limits
The sewing orderthe objects’ ordernone
The lock stitches of a small partlock_start, lock_end and their scales

The prototype counted the settings the parts carry on the 13 designs below. Every one is in the parameters registry. Ink/Stitch has all but turn_stitches. The fill effect’s dialog leaves turn_stitches out, and VectorCraft admits only the parameters a manifest declares. The dialog needs the row. 2 elements also carried auto_fill_outline, a setting of the Auto effect. Expand drops every auto_ key.

Each part takes its shape’s name (names of shapes), numbered as the notices number the parts. What Expand can’t keep is said by a coded warning, and nothing is dropped silently:

  • Lettering becomes columns, and its text can’t be edited as text. Lettering set in a font no longer marks its underlay as lettering does (SC-W0922).
  • A shape that needs more work than the budget allows is left out, as planning leaves it out (SC-E0004), and SC-W0922 names it.
  • A part cut to a clip is written whole into a VectorCraft document, which SC-W0922 names. No part of the set has one: digitizing cuts its shapes to their clips first.
  • An SVG file sewn by Ink/Stitch loses digitized_as and the design’s limits, so Ink/Stitch sews it with jumps and without the cap.

A guided fill is written with its guide, in a group with it, as are a part’s start, end and target markers.

Expand on the command line and in VectorCraft

The command line. stitch expand picture.vectorcraft -o expanded.vectorcraft writes the documented .vectorcraft format, and -o expanded.svg an SVG file with Ink/Stitch’s attributes (stitchcraft_svg::written). It reads the design as stitch plan --auto does. Each object of a layer that holds an automatic shape stays in the document, hidden and locked, below a new group of the expanded parts, “Expanded embroidery”, as Image Trace keeps the source image beside its paths (stitchcraft_vectorcraft::expanded). The document stays the one VectorCraft saved but for those objects’ visible and locked, the new group, the document’s next_id and the Design Settings filter’s data, which keeps the cap’s limits. A picture given to stitch expand is traced first, as stitch plan traces it.

VectorCraft, today. In one undo step, a filter may replace the selected paths with new objects that have StitchCraft’s effect records, as Stroke to Satin does (tools). An Expand filter can’t work today:

  • A filter reads a path’s own effects alone (routing filters). The Auto effect is usually on the traced group, so the filter can’t read the rules’ settings.
  • Digitizing a design takes an export’s work. TS-13A to TS-13C took 7.9 to 8.0 billion instructions to export, where a heavy filter gets 1 billion, and a traced photo needs 51 to 69 billion (P10).

So in VectorCraft a person runs stitch expand and opens the document it writes. The smallest change to ask of VectorCraft, through the plug-in features RFC, is P11. With it a filter reads the effects of the groups round each path. A heavy filter needs P10’s budget as well. A filter can’t make a group either, which a guided fill’s guide needs. That is a later ask, and until then an Expand filter leaves the guide out and says so.

How faithful it is

Measured on 2026-11-20 with the prototype, main at ebbdd62a, built at opt-level 2. Each design is planned automatic, then expanded with ordinary effects alone, without digitized_as and the cap’s limits, and planned again with the same origin:

DesignStitchesJumpsJump threadsThickest, fillsAt 3 fills or more, mm²Needle points as with the cap off
Dog on turf, 6 threads9,530 → 10,21969 → 7860 → 693.87 → 5.0954.25 → 138.00no
Dog on turf, 9 threads10,100 → 10,88775 → 8262 → 693.97 → 5.1363.75 → 151.50no
Dog running, 6 threads10,630 → 11,16958 → 6949 → 603.92 → 4.9653.50 → 128.75no
Dog running, 9 threads11,779 → 12,67476 → 9264 → 803.98 → 5.8594.00 → 190.50no

TS-13C, digitized half5,949 → 6,08118 → 2013 → 153.92 → 4.5483.75 → 104.25no

TS-13E, digitized half2,712 → 2,716853.24 → 3.392.50yes

On every design the thread changes, the order of the elements, the stitches astray and the likeness are the same. Bare fabric is the same or less. With the cap off in the automatic plan, 5 designs have every needle point the same, in the same order, and differ only in the base layer’s stitches, which are underlay in the automatic plan and top stitches once expanded (187 to 1,085 a design). The other 8 jump where the automatic plan runs under later shapes, 1 to 16 jumps more, with 2 to 128 needle points different round them. So the cap and the runs account for every difference measured, and the base layer for every difference of the stitches’ parts.

The tolerance. With digitized_as and the design’s limits, the expanded design’s plan is the automatic plan. It has the same threads. Each stitch lies at the same point, with the same kind and part, in the same order. So the machine file is the same byte for byte. The planning functions are deterministic and get the same elements and the same 3 sets. The requirement holds the 2 plans to the bit. A document written out reads its points back to within a unit in the last place of a point (stitchcraft_vectorcraft::filter). A fill’s rows could then fall on the other side of a rounding. The case of a written document, req_cli_025_an_expanded_document_sews_as_the_automatic_design, plans the traced badge from the VectorCraft document and the SVG file stitch expand writes, and compares their PES bytes with the automatic design’s. A PES file holds tenths of a millimetre, so stitches within 0.001 mm of each other write the same bytes unless one lies on a rounding’s edge, and none of the badge’s does. The 2 dogs on turf have such points (the build’s measure). A person’s edit breaks it. So does a format that can’t store a part’s settings, and a guide that a filter can’t group with its fill.

Stitched previews of a traced dog, the lettered patch and TS-13C, automatic beside expanded with ordinary effects alone, show the extra jump threads, such as one along TS-13C’s balloon string.

Other embroidery software

  • Wilcom EmbroideryStudio’s Auto-Digitize makes objects of chosen kinds, Tatami Fill, Turning Satin, Centerline Run and Outline Run, with the current properties, and a person then edits their properties, stitch angles, entry and exit points and sequence (Auto-digitize individual shapes). Its Smart Design sets the sequence by closest join, and the person reorders colours and turns underlay on or off (Auto-digitize entire designs). The pages describe one run from the artwork to the objects, and describe no link that updates them.
  • Hatch Embroidery’s Auto-Digitize leaves objects that a person reshapes, gives another fill type in Object Properties and orders in the Sequence tab (a user guide).
  • Brother PE-Design’s Auto Punch sets, before it converts, which areas sew and which become lines, and is run again with Retry after a change (PE-Design 8 manual, p. 128).
  • Ink/Stitch has no automatic digitizing of a picture. Its Jump to Stroke tool makes the jumps between elements running stitches placed under the following stitches (stroke tools), which is how digitized_as = part sews.

A person coming from them finds the same objects with properties, in an order that is the sewing order, which they edit freely. What differs: the expanded fills already reach under their neighbours and have a base layer under them, and planning keeps the runs under later shapes and the cap while the parts keep digitized_as.

Decisions for the owner

Each with the recommendation of phase 1:

  1. One setting, digitized_as, for what ordinary effects can’t say. Recommended. The other way is to freeze each run as a stroke, as Ink/Stitch’s Jump to Stroke does. The cap would then be lost with a warning. On the dogs that raises the area at 3 fills or more 2 to 2.6 times, and a run frozen in place no longer lies under the shape a person moves.
  2. The traced group stays, hidden and locked, under the expanded parts. Recommended, so the person can compare and go back. The other way deletes it.
  3. Lettering placed for a marker becomes its columns. Recommended, since they are what sewed. The other way writes a text object with the lettering effect. A person can then edit its text. The lettering’s own rules sew it, and their stitches can differ from the automatic plan’s.
  4. The command line first, VectorCraft through P11. Recommended. A .vectorcraft exporter would write a second document, and a filter can’t run until P11 and P10 are in VectorCraft.
  5. The cap’s limits as design settings. Recommended. The other way keeps them on each part, where the parts could disagree.
  6. The name. VectorCraft’s Object › Expand already makes an Image Trace object plain paths. Recommended: stitch expand on the command line, and Expand Embroidery in VectorCraft’s menus, so that the 2 commands aren’t mistaken for each other.

The build’s measure

Measured on 2026-11-20, the branch built at opt-level 2, with a measuring program kept out of the repository. Each design is planned automatic, expanded, and planned again with the same origin: at the engine, read back from the SVG file stitch expand writes, and, for the 5 designs traced into a VectorCraft document, read back from the document it writes.

DesignPartsEngineSVG file.vectorcraft document
Dog on turf, 6 threads157bit for bitbit for bit4 needle points fewer of 9,599
Dog on turf, 9 threads175bit for bitbit for bit2 needle points more of 10,175
Dog running, 6 threads150bit for bitbit for bitwithin 1.4e-13 mm
Dog running, 9 threads192bit for bitbit for bitwithin 1.5e-13 mm

TS-13A to TS-13D, digitized halves22 to 78bit for bitbit for bit
