A VectorCraft document keeps points in points. A part’s millimetres read back to within a unit in their last place. Most products of a float and a point’s size can’t be written to read back exactly. The 2 dogs on turf sew the same few needle points otherwise at the engine when every point of their expanded design moves by a unit in its last place, so a fill’s row there lies on a rounding’s edge. Every other plan is the automatic one to 1e-13 mm. The traced badge of the command line’s case writes the same PES bytes through both files.

Editing a part. The largest fill of each expanded design was turned 45°, sewn as a contour fill, or deleted, and the other parts whose stitches changed were counted. On the badges, the emblem and TS-13A none to 3 change: the outline that sews with the fill and the part sewn next, which starts nearest where it ends. On the traced dogs 13 to 97 of 150 to 192 parts change. The cap at the end of planning accounts for most of them: it thins the plan where it sews thick, and an edited part changes where that is. With the cap out of reach, the turn doesn’t change another part on the 9-thread dog on turf, and changes 18 on the 9-thread running dog: the parts the needle sews under between 2 parts, and the next ones. A person who wants a part left alone by the cap sets its digitized_as to no.

Stitched previews of the running dog, the lettered patch and TS-13C, automatic beside expanded beside expanded with its largest fill turned, show the first 2 alike. The golden files stay as they were, since every design sews as before.

What isn’t built

The Expand Embroidery filter in VectorCraft waits for the RFC’s P11 and P10. Until then the person runs stitch expand and opens the document. The manifests declare digitized_as and turn_stitches already (REQ-VC-035).

Pictures from the command line

stitch plan picture.png and stitch export picture.png trace the picture with VectorCraft’s command line, as spike M13.9 decided (ADR-0015) and M13.10 built (REQ-CLI-012, REQ-CLI-013). A design whose extension is .png, .jpg or .jpeg, in any case, is a picture. VectorCraft publishes vectorcraft-cli with each release, and stitch runs the one --vectorcraft-cli names, or else the one on the PATH:

vectorcraft-cli run --commands - --export FOLDER/traced.vectorcraft

It gives it 4 steps on standard input, where a picture of any size fits. They are the compatibility gate’s trace case’s:

  1. swatch.library.load loads the threads stitch palette writes for the machine as a swatch library.
  2. file.place places the picture’s bytes, fitted inside a square --size millimetres across, or else inside the machine’s hoop 0.5 mm in from each side, its aspect kept.
  3. imageTrace.make traces it in colour with the library’s colours as they are, at most --colours of them, by the Abutting method, so that the shapes share their edges. With --fewer-points it fits their outlines with fewer points (the tracer’s options).
  4. imageTrace.expand keeps the paths, without the picture.

VectorCraft saves the document into a folder of the command’s own beside the machine file, named as stitch’s temporary files are, and the folder is removed once the document is read. It runs with VECTORCRAFT_NO_PREFS set, so that it neither reads nor writes the user’s VectorCraft preferences, and a picture traces the same for everyone. The document is then the design. It is read with --auto, planned for the machine --profile names or else the reference machine, and named after the picture. --traced writes it beside the machine file, to correct the trace in VectorCraft. A bug-report bundle keeps it too, so that a replay doesn’t need VectorCraft.

The picture’s size. VectorCraft places a picture at its own resolution, or 72 pixels to the inch when its file says none, so a logo a few hundred pixels across would sew larger than any hoop. stitch fits the picture into the machine’s hoop unless --size gives the length of its longer side. The size is the picture’s, its background included, and the shapes left once the background is out sew smaller. Fitted to the hoop, the picture keeps 0.5 mm from each side: a shape along its edge sews as a satin column, whose stitches reach 0.4 mm past its rails, its pull compensation, from rails that may lie up to the tolerance shapes meet within outside the shape. Fitted to the hoop itself, a picture whose shapes reach its sides would sew past the hoop, which SC-E0701 refuses.

How many colours. Image Trace keeps at most --colours of the library’s colours, 6 unless it says otherwise, as in its own panel. The count takes in the background’s colour. A picture with smooth edges, or a JPEG file, has colours between its shapes’ colours, which may trace as threads of their own. A --colours equal to the number of colours the picture has keeps them out. A badge in 3 colours on white traces in 4.

When VectorCraft can’t trace. A command line that can’t be started, missing from the PATH or from the path named, is SC-E0815, which says how to install it, or to trace the picture in VectorCraft. One that fails or doesn’t save the document is SC-E0816, which ends with what it said. Then nothing is written but the report.

StitchCraft traces nothing itself. A tracer of its own would repeat VectorCraft’s, which a harness measures. It would also trace a picture otherwise than VectorCraft does, and the command line would then sew it unlike the tutorial’s flow.

A picture in 6 threads

Roadmap step M13.20. The owner’s machine has one needle, so each thread change is a rethread by hand, and a design in 6 threads is what she wants to sew. Phase 1 measured what the colour count costs on a varied set on 2026-11-20, on main 571d3928. stitch plan lists each thread’s share of the stitches (REQ-AUTO-162). A person keeps a colour of the picture (REQ-AUTO-160) or leaves one out (REQ-AUTO-161). The owner chose each of them on 2026-11-21.

The colour count today

stitch plan picture.png asks Image Trace for at most --colours of the machine’s threads, 6 unless it says otherwise (how many colours). The tracer chooses the threads, and flat art takes fewer than asked: the emblem traces in 4 at every count, the lettered patch in 5 and the pear in 9 at most. StitchCraft merges no colours before planning. After it, automatic digitizing merges specks and thin lines into their neighbours, which may take a part’s thread away (likeness), and a mark keeps a part from merging (marks that keep parts). A mark keeps a part’s shape. It can’t give the part a thread the trace didn’t choose. The sewing order sews each thread as long as it can (sewing order). stitch plan lists the threads in the order the machine sews them, one line each time a thread is threaded, and the report has the same list under threads. How much each thread sews is the list of shares.

What 6 threads costs

Each picture was traced 100 mm across in the reference machine’s threads, by VectorCraft 0.24.1’s command line with the steps of pictures from the command line, and digitized with the defaults. The set is the 2 cut-outs of the dog, the pear, the drawn emblem, a lettered patch drawn in code, and 4 photos from Wikimedia Commons kept locally. One is of magnolia blossoms and one of a goose. The other 2 show coleus leaves and a castle by a pond.

  • Colour error is the mean distance in Oklab, times 100, between the picture and its trace, on a grid of 0.25 mm.
  • A small detail is a piece of the picture of 1 to 25 mm² whose pixels each lie 0.10 or more in Oklab from the median colour of the 4 mm round them, and whose mean colour lies 0.12 or more from the mean of a ring 1 mm wide round it. It is bright when it is lighter than its ring by 0.05 in lightness, or more colourful by 0.05 in chroma. The trace keeps a detail when the thread that covers most of it differs from the one that covers most of its ring, and lies nearer the detail’s own colour than the ring’s.
  • Likeness is the likeness of the stitches to the trace. The thickest spot is the cap’s measure, in fills. Stitches, jumps and thread changes are the plan’s.

From 4 to 6 threads the colour error falls on every photo, by 0.3 to 2.5. From 6 to 9 it falls by 0 to 0.9, and the thread changes rise by 3 to 5 on 5 of the 6 traced photos, and by 33 on the coleus. The coleus in 9 and 12 threads and the magnolia in 12 run the sewing order’s search out of its budget, and sew in the greedy order (SC-I0907). The small details kept don’t rise with the count: the goose keeps 51 of 79 in 6 threads and 38 in 12, since a count with more threads splits a detail’s ring between 2 of them. The stitches, the jumps, the thickest spot and the time to plan change little with the count.

The colours a person misses are the small ones. Image Trace ranks the threads by the area each would cover. The coleus is purple leaves with magenta veins, and in 6 threads it traces with no purple or magenta thread: grey, green, brown and black. It keeps 2 of its 19 small bright details. In 9 threads it gains a violet and keeps 10, in 12 a purple and keeps 12. The castle traces its sunlit walls in the sky’s light blue in 6 threads, and gains White in 9.

Keeping a colour

--keep-colour COLOUR names a colour the trace must have, for stitch plan and stitch expand. COLOUR is a thread of the machine by its name, in any case, a colour as #rrggbb, which keeps the thread nearest it by the palette’s own matching, or a pixel of the picture as X,Y, from its left and top edges, which keeps the thread nearest that pixel’s colour, its alpha left aside. A name is the first thread of that name. Brother’s palette has 2 named Flesh Pink, and the other is named by its colour. VectorCraft then traces twice.

  1. It traces the picture in at most --colours less the kept threads, chosen from the machine’s threads without the kept ones. These are the threads the tracer itself would choose for the rest. Image Trace takes 2 colours at the least, and the first trace is in 2 when fewer are left. With as many kept threads as --colours there is no first trace.
  2. It traces the picture again in a swatch library of exactly the threads the first trace’s shapes are filled in and the kept ones, and this trace is the design.

The colours are read before VectorCraft runs (apps/stitchcraft-cli/src/colours.rs). A mistake costs no trace. Each name that is no thread is answered with the 3 nearest names, by the count of letters to change. A pixel outside the picture, and more kept threads than --colours, are usage errors too. A pixel needs the picture decoded, which stitch does only with its cutout feature. A build without it, such as the WASI one that makes the golden files, refuses a pixel with a usage error that names the feature. A kept thread the second trace leaves out, since no part of the picture is nearer its colour than the other threads’, is the warning SC-W0822.

Measured with the build on 2026-11-21, at 6 colours and 100 mm, VectorCraft 0.24.1, by one rule for every picture in place of a person: keep the thread nearest the colour of the most visible small bright detail the 6-colour trace loses, the largest by area times contrast. The pear loses none.

PictureKeptThread changesColour errorSmall details keptBright ones kept

dog-runSilver, for a grey8 to 77.35 to 7.4119 to 21 of 3710 to 12 of 17
emblemPrussian Blue, a thread it has4, as before0.32, as before20 of 23, as before20 of 21, as before
patchWarm Gray, SC-W08224, as before6.16 to 6.1418 of 22, as before17 of 21, as before

A kept colour takes the place of one of the tracer’s threads, so the count of threads stays and the colour error moves by 0.25 at most. The coleus keeps 12 more of its small details, and the goose loses 12. A picture traced in a thread it already has sews as before. A trace takes 0.5 to 3.4 seconds on the set, and a kept colour traces twice.

Before the build, phase 1 kept a colour by trace scripts of its own:

The coleus in 6 threads, Dark Fuchsia kept from a vein: the first trace chose grey, green, pewter, brown and black. It keeps 17 of its 24 small details for 4, and 15 of its 19 bright ones, more than 12 threads keep. Its colour error falls from 10.4 to 10.3, where 9 threads give 10.0, since the veins are a small share of the leaves. It sews 11 thread changes where 6 threads sew 13, and 9 threads 46. In 6 threads the castle’s sunlit walls trace in the sky’s light blue. With White kept they are white, 6.5 % of its area, and the yellow-green of its hill goes, for a colour error of 7.3 where it had 7.2 and 11 thread changes for 9.

The other threads are the tracer’s choice, and no rule of StitchCraft’s drops one. Measured with a stand-in for a person, who keeps the colour of the most visible small bright detail the trace lost:

  • dropping the thread of least area dropped the patch’s red stars, 1.3 % of its area.
  • dropping the nearer of the 2 nearest threads raised the running dog’s colour error from 7.35 to 7.96.
  • the tracer’s own 5 threads with the kept one kept the patch as it was, gave the running dog 12 of its 17 bright details for 10, and the coleus 16 of 24 details for 4. On the goose, a small grey kept cost it 12 details, and the castle gained nothing.

So which colour matters is the person’s to say, as for marks and the style. A kept thread that the second trace covers with no area gets a warning, since the person asked for it.

Leaving a colour out

--drop-colour COLOUR leaves a thread out of the library of every trace, for stitch plan and stitch expand. COLOUR reads as for --keep-colour. The pixels of the thread left out take the nearest of the others. Without a kept colour the picture is traced once. A colour both kept and dropped, and dropping every thread but one, are usage errors before VectorCraft runs. The roadmap’s row planned a merge of 2 traced colours into one thread, which is not built. On the 7 pictures that traced in 9 threads, leaving out the nearer of the 2 nearest threads gave the colour error and the details of the tracer’s own 8 threads, within 0.06 and 2 details. So a merge of the nearest pair is one colour fewer, which --colours gives. A person who lacks a thread, or wants a rethread less, names the one that goes. Recolouring the traced shapes into the other thread after the trace would give pixels a colour farther than their nearest thread. The trace leaves the thread out instead.

The list of shares

stitch plan lists each thread once under shares, in the order it is first threaded: the stitches it sews, their share of the plan’s stitches and how many times it is threaded. The report has the same list under shares. One count makes both (commands/plan/shares.rs), so they can’t disagree. In 9 threads 6 of the 7 pictures that trace in 9 have a thread that sews under 3 % of the stitches: the goose has one of 1.0 % and the pear one of 0.9 %. In 6 threads only the patch has one, its red stars at 1.9 %. A person sees which thread sews little before rethreading for it, and decides. The list chooses nothing.

A thread is its colour and its name, as the machine file names it. The sewing order can thread one twice, when a thread sews under another’s shapes and again over them: it is one spool, so it is one share, threaded 2 times. The same colour by another name is another thread. A stop keeps the thread in the needle, so it is no threading. The stitches counted are all the plan’s stitches in the thread, underlay and lock stitches among them, as the stitches line counts them: each uses the spool and the time.

The shares are rounded to a tenth of a percent by largest remainder, so that the shares as shown add up to exactly 100.0. Each is rounded down to a tenth, and the tenths left over go one each to the shares with the largest remainders, the thread threaded first among equal ones. 3 threads of one stitch each show 33.4, 33.3 and 33.3. A thread too small to show a tenth shows 0.0 with its stitches counted. A plan without stitches shows 0.0 for each thread it threads. The count runs over the plan the engine made, which the work budget already bounds, as the plan’s other counts do, so it charges no meter.

What VectorCraft’s command line allows

Read in its command list (vectorcraft-cli commands, 0.24.1), the steps ADR-0015 allows:

  • imageTrace.make’s palette documentLibrary traces in at most colors of a library’s colours, as they are. The tracer chooses which. No option gives a colour a place among them.
  • keepColors traces only the library’s colours within about 30 levels of the listed ones and leaves the rest of the picture out. Measured, the coleus with 2 colours listed kept 38 % of its area, and a listed colour the trace hadn’t found traced as no area. It can’t keep a colour.
  • swatch.library.load loads any list of threads as the library, which is how a colour is kept or left out.
  • edit.colors.adjustBalance with includeImages changes the red, green and blue of a placed picture before the trace, a correction of brightness and colour cast.

In VectorCraft itself a person traces with Image Trace’s panel and chooses its palette: a colour group of the document’s swatches, colorGroup in imageTrace.make, traces in exactly the threads they put in it. Since the plug-in traces nothing, its dialog doesn’t need a control for a kept colour.

Tone correction of a cut-out’s parts

The row planned a tone correction, of brightness and colour cast, for each kept part of a cut-out, and a share of the threads for each. No picture of the set needs it. The set’s photos are lit evenly. The dim photo that showed the need is the owner’s own, which can’t be a test case here. A correction for each part needs the cut-out to name its parts, and a share of threads for each needs a trace for each part. A correction of the whole picture is edit.colors.adjustBalance before the trace, or the person’s own photo editor. It is left to a step of its own, with dim pictures to measure it on.

Decisions for the owner

Each with the recommendation.

  • The default count stays 6. It is the default already. 4 threads raise every photo’s colour error by 0.3 to 2.5 and lose the goose’s details. 9 threads cost 3 to 33 thread changes more on each traced photo for 0 to 0.9 of colour error. Recommended: 6. The owner answered on 2026-11-21: yes to each.

  • A kept colour, named or clicked. --keep-colour with a thread’s name, a colour or a pixel. Recommended: each of them. A person sees a pixel, and the share list shows a name.

  • The rest of the threads are the tracer’s. The other choices are a rule that drops the smallest or the nearest thread, which failed on 2 pictures of 5, or a full list of threads from the person, which --keep-colour given 6 times already is. Recommended: the tracer’s.

  • A dropped colour in place of a merge. Recommended: --drop-colour, since a merge of the nearest pair is one colour fewer.

  • The order of building. Recommended: the list of shares first. It guesses nothing and serves every design. Then --keep-colour and --drop-colour together, since they read colours alike.

  • No tone correction of a cut-out’s parts in this step. Recommended: a step of its own, once there are dim pictures to measure.

What isn’t built

  • A merge of 2 traced colours into one thread. Measured, a merge of the nearest pair is one thread fewer, which --colours gives, and --drop-colour names any other.
  • A rule that keeps a colour without being asked. The stand-in’s choice, the most visible small detail lost, helped 2 pictures of 5 and cost one of them details.
  • A kept colour drawn in VectorCraft as a mark is. A pixel of the picture says the same.
  • Tone correction, and a share of the threads, for each part of a cut-out.

Outlines thread can’t show

Roadmap step M13.51 measured the outlines of traced pictures and gives stitch plan an option, --fewer-points, off by default, that asks Image Trace for fitting options of its own (REQ-CLI-024). Step M13.52 built the smoothing pass that this section describes, as a setting off by default (REQ-AUTO-131 to REQ-AUTO-134), and the notice SC-I0921.

The owner, on 2026-11-19: “often time there are too many points and you need to simplify the number of points in the shape”. She simplifies traced shapes by hand in a drawing program before she sews them. The trade treats it as a fault: clusters of tiny points sew dense spots and ragged edges. Wilcom’s Smooth Curves and Bernina’s Smooth curves each take one precision value. A higher value leaves fewer points, which follow the outline less closely.

The points and wobble of traced outlines

Every traced picture of the repository was measured, with a traced lettered patch and 4 traced photos kept outside it. The test sheets’ traced halves are drawn exactly in code and have no wobble. A thread is 0.4 mm wide.

PictureShapesAnchorsAnchors per mmMost on one subpathPoints needed within 0.1 mmJags per 10 mmTeeth per 10 mm
The pear65920.5126713 %0.572.28
Dog on turf, 6 threads971,9020.8114612 %0.751.74
Dog on turf, 9 threads1152,0960.8416111 %0.671.82
Dog running, 6 threads831,8270.7511511 %0.461.45
Dog running, 9 threads1072,0900.749111 %0.451.46
Lettered patch, traced373,2300.751767 %1.550.53
Photo of a flower2,01451,6133.071,9059 %8.082.81
Photo of a leafy plant3,985118,6723.741,13211 %14.843.07
Photo of a bird1,81368,0993.493,62910 %11.143.15
Photo of a building1,44463,3614.143,93212 %16.582.18
  • The shapes share their edges exactly. Image Trace’s Abutting method gives 2 neighbours the same anchors along the edge they share. Of the pear’s 294 distinct anchors, 282 belong to 2 shapes and 8 to 3, where 3 colours meet. The edges coincide to less than a micrometre. 69 % of the pear’s outline length and 89 to 99 % of the others’ lies along another shape.
  • Few points draw what thread shows. The column of points needed counts the points of a polyline that stays within 0.1 mm of the outline flattened (Douglas and Peucker, 1973), as a share of the flattened outline’s points.
  • A jag is a turn of 20° or more that turns back within 0.4 mm along the outline, measured on the outline resampled every 0.05 mm, the direction taken over 0.1 mm. Thread is wider than a jag. A tooth turns back within 0.4 to 1.6 mm, and thread shows it.
  • The pear’s ragged colour edges are teeth. Its photo’s pixel is 0.28 mm. Where one band of colour meets the next in a gradient, the trace follows a staircase of 3 to 5 pixels, and its teeth stand 0.5 to 1.5 mm tall.

What the wobble costs. The planner’s rules read an outline’s points. The traced dogs, given as the same outlines flattened to polylines within 0.005 mm, sew 16 to 22 of their 69 to 92 shapes by another rule, most of them refused for columns where the curves gave columns. Today the 4 dogs sew 12,659 stitches under 0.5 mm, 29 % of their stitches, and lay 12 stitches astray. A move of the whole picture by 0.05 mm in x and y, the shift test of M13.47, changes the rule of no shape on any of these pictures.

Traced letters flip for another reason. M13.47 found traced letters sewn as fills where the same letters drawn exactly sew as columns. Its 558 letters, 3 typefaces at 6, 12 and 25 mm, each traced from a render of 40 pixels per mm, were measured. 32 letters sew by another rule than their exact outlines. The H of the sans-serif bold at 12 mm is refused for columns because its stems’ inner edges lean 0.025 mm over 4.5 mm. That edge is a straight line, and smoothing leaves a straight line where it is. Smoothed at 0.1 and 0.2 mm, 55 and 46 traced letters sew by another rule than their exact outlines smoothed the same way, and smoothing alone changes the rule of about 50 exact letters. A block H, T and L drawn with one jog of 0.025, 0.05 or 0.1 mm at an inner corner sew as columns, as they do without the jog. A leaning stem belongs to the columns’ design, and smoothing doesn’t fix it.

The tracer’s options

stitch plan picture.png asks Image Trace for its mode, palette, colours and method, and leaves its fitting options at their defaults, which are Paths 50, Corners 75 and Noise 25, with Snap Curves To Lines off. The tracer fits an edge once for both shapes that share it. Its options smooth an edge without opening a gap. The pear and the 4 dogs were traced again from their sources by VectorCraft 0.24.1’s command line, once for each value, and planned by main at 00b45915. The counts are the 5 pictures’ together:

The colour error is the mean distance in RGB between the trace and its source picture, over the picture’s pixels. Detail lost is the likeness measure’s, against the trace itself. A trace that drops shapes doesn’t lose detail by that measure, and its colour error shows the loss.

  • Paths sets how closely a curve follows the pixels: within 0.08 pixels at 100, 0.33 at 50 and 0.78 at 0. At 0 it saves 17 % of the anchors and changes nothing else.
  • Corners sets the turn that makes a corner: 90° at 0, 49° at 75 and 35° at 100. Each step of a staircase turns about 90°. The default keeps a staircase as corners. At 0 or 25 the jags fall by a third.
  • Noise is the size in pixels below which an area is dropped. Raised, it drops the dogs’ small shapes and their detail with them: the colour error rises by 1.4 at 50 and 6.6 at 200. Lowered to 10, it adds 294 shapes and 32 stitches astray.
  • Snap Curves To Lines moves each measure by less than 2 neighbouring values of Paths or Corners do.

Built (M13.51). stitch plan --fewer-points sends Paths 0 and Corners 0 for a picture it traces, in every style. Noise stays 25. Without the option, the default, stitch plan doesn’t send a fitting option, as before M13.51. Corners 0 rounds each turn under 90° of a drawn logo, such as an octagon’s, so a person leaves the option out for a logo.

Each picture was traced from its source by VectorCraft 0.24.1’s command line with the defaults and with the 2 options, and planned by this step’s code. The pictures are the pear, the 4 dogs, the badge of the compatibility gate drawn flat and a stitched preview of the lettered patch, traced as a photo of it. Each cell gives the trace with the defaults, then with the options. Area at 3 fills is the area under 3 fills of thread or more:

  • Better. The anchors fall by 28 to 57 %, the jags by 48 to 77 % and the teeth by 16 to 87 %. The dogs sew 1.5 % fewer stitches and leave 8 % fewer jump threads, and the colour error and the detail lost stay within 1 of what they were. Every picture traces into the same shapes, so no shape is lost. The badge’s ring keeps more of its likeness, since it is traced as a ring of even width.
  • Worse. The dogs have 28 % more area at 3 fills, 340 mm² where they had 266, and one of them sews 4.04 fills at its thickest spot, past the 4 fills where the warning starts. The lettered patch has 3 times the area at 3 fills, and 118 stitches astray where it had 90. The badge, flat art traced without the logo style, sews 18 % more stitches and 60 % more short ones.
  • Rules. 29 to 50 of each dog’s 69 to 92 shapes sew by another rule, as the shapes’ outlines change. The pear’s shapes sew as they did.

Off by default. The previews of the dogs with and without the options look alike to the eye, with other jumps, and the options change how a traced picture sews far more than its outlines change. A shape’s stitch rule reacts to how its outline is drawn, so a third to a half of each dog’s shapes sew by another rule, more of the design sews thick, and one dog passes the warning’s limit. That rule reacts to how an outline is drawn, and the fewer points change it. The option stays off. M13.53 looked for a rule that doesn’t react, and the owner stopped it on 2026-11-20 with main’s rule kept. A rule that keeps the drawing out of the choice without raising the area at 3 fills would open the question again.

The committed traces of the pear and the dogs are not traced again: they are the inputs of their own cases, and this step changes only what stitch plan asks the tracer.

Smoothing in StitchCraft

The tracer’s options reach only pictures stitch plan traces itself. A document traced in VectorCraft by hand, or by another tracer, reaches StitchCraft as it is. The pass below smooths an automatic area’s outline at a tolerance, before anything is digitized. M13.52 built it. The numbers below are a prototype’s, measured in M13.51 on traces made with the tracer’s defaults.

The rule. The setting auto_smooth_mm is how far a point of an outline may move. 0 turns the pass off.

  1. Edges. The outlines of the automatic areas whose setting is on are cut into edges at their junctions: a point where 3 or more edges meet. A segment is the same in 2 outlines when its ends and control points are, either way round, so an edge 2 areas share is one edge. An edge shared with an area whose setting is 0, or with a shape with settings, stays as drawn. An edge 2 areas share is smoothed at the smaller of their settings.
  2. Corners. Its corners cut an edge again. A corner is a point where the outline’s direction over 4 times the tolerance before it and after it differs by 60° or more, the sharpest point of each run of such points. Junctions and corners stay where they are. A staircase of pixels smaller than the tolerance turns both ways within that span, and so has no corner.
  3. Smoothing. Each piece between fixed points is resampled every fifth of the tolerance and smoothed by a Gaussian whose deviation is the tolerance, measured along the piece. The piece is reflected through each fixed end, so that the ends stay. The smoothed piece may lie no farther than the tolerance from the piece as drawn, nor the piece as drawn from it (their Hausdorff distance). Where it would, or where a ring would gain or lose more than 10 % of its area, the deviation shrinks by 0.7, up to 8 times, and then the piece stays as drawn.
  4. Crossings. An edge whose smoothed line would cross another line is smoothed again with half the deviation. That repeats until the line crosses none, and at last the edge stays as drawn.
  5. Fewer points. The smoothed edge is drawn with the fewest of its points that stay within 0.02 mm of it, and every area along it takes that line.

Every area that shares an edge has the same line along it, so no gap or overlap opens between them. The pass charges the element’s budget for each point it resamples and each pair of segments it tests for a crossing. An area whose pass needs more keeps its outline, and SC-I0921 names it.

The literature. Douglas and Peucker keep the points that lie farthest from a chord, so they keep the tips of jags, and cut points without smoothing. Visvalingam and Whyatt (1993) remove the point whose triangle with its neighbours has the least area, one point after another, and leave outlines less jagged. Saalfeld (1999) showed that removing points can make a line cross another, and gave tests that keep it from doing so. PostGIS’s ST_CoverageSimplify simplifies the edges a set of polygons shares, each once, with a variant of Visvalingam and Whyatt, so that the polygons still cover their area without gaps: the shared edges of the rule are the same idea. A Gaussian along a curve is the smoothing of curvature scale space (Mokhtarian and Mackworth, 1992). It pulls a convex curve inward by about the deviation squared over twice its radius, under 0.01 mm for a radius of 2 mm at 0.2 mm. The rule uses the Gaussian because it averages a jag away, where a method that keeps points keeps its tip.

VectorCraft’s Simplify. object.path.simplify refits each path, or each run of selected segments between anchors that stay (its MCP guide). It refits a path on its own, and its guide doesn’t say that a run refits the same way in both directions, so the 2 shapes of a shared edge can take 2 different lines. stitch plan doc.vectorcraft also runs without VectorCraft. The pass is StitchCraft’s. A Simplify that refits the edges a selection’s shapes share once is a candidate for the plug-in features RFC.

The prototype’s numbers

The prototype is a script of the step’s notes, written from the rule above without the 10 % area bound. It smoothed each picture at 4 tolerances, and main at 00b45915 planned the results. Points are those needed within 0.02 mm, each shared edge counted once. Short stitches and stitches are the change from the pictures as traced. Recoloured is the share of the picture’s area whose colour the smoothing changed, drawn at 10 pixels per mm.

  • At 0.1 and 0.2 mm nothing moves farther than half a thread. The photos, traced at 3 to 4 anchors per mm, need 21 % fewer points at 0.1 mm and 36 % fewer at 0.2 mm, and at 0.2 mm their jags fall from 12.7 to 4.1 in every 10 mm. Planned at 0.2 mm, the 4 photos sew 7 % fewer stitches and 18 to 24 % fewer stitches under 0.5 mm, with as many jumps, and plan in 18 to 59 % less time. Their fabric measures, stitches astray and likeness, were not run: each takes hours on the shared machine. The dogs and the pear, already fitted as curves at under 1 anchor per mm, need as many points as before, and sew 3 and 6 % fewer short stitches at 0.2 mm. The traced lettered patch sews 12 % fewer and 4 more of its parts as satin columns.
  • At 0.4 mm the jags and teeth of the dogs halve, and the outlines move as far as a thread is wide.
  • At 0.8 mm the pear’s teeth are gone in the step’s pictures, but 7 % of the dogs’ area changes colour and 136 of their shapes grow or shrink by more than 10 %. The photos lose 1,287 small shapes, which the area bound keeps.
  • Rules. The dogs sew 17 to 29 of their shapes by another rule at 0.1 and 0.2 mm, about as many as the same outlines flattened, with more columns (48 to 59 at 0.2 mm) and as many refused. The shift test doesn’t change a rule at 0.2 mm either. At 0.4 mm 104 parts are refused where 85 are today. Not every changed rule is better: on the flower photo at 0.2 mm, a petal’s part that was filled sews as a column whose stitches fan along an edge still ragged at a thread’s scale.
  • Points on the dogs. The tracer has already fitted their outlines as curves. The pass draws a smoothed curve with straight lines within 0.02 mm, which takes as many points. The tracer’s Paths 0 and Corners 0 are what cuts their points.

The setting and the notice

  • auto_smooth_mm, a length from 0 to 1 mm among the Advanced settings, labelled “Smooth outlines”. The Auto effect’s dialog holds 12 rows, so it has none. 0 is off and the default, for a document and for a picture stitch plan traces alike. A person sets it with --set auto_smooth_mm=0.2. 0.2 mm is half a thread: on every picture of the set it cut short stitches, with nothing moved that thread shows.
  • SC-I0921, info, once for the design: “The outlines of 107 automatic areas were smoothed within 0.2 mm: they are drawn with 3126 points, where 3067 drew them within 0.02 mm.” It names the areas whose pass needed more than the budget, which keep their outlines.
  • Shapes a person drew. VectorCraft’s Expand leaves plain paths, so a document doesn’t say which of its shapes were traced. StitchCraft never smooths a document’s shapes unless the setting asks, and never a shape with settings. An edge such a shape shares stays as drawn on both sides.

The owner’s decisions

On 2026-11-20 the owner decided:

  1. The tracer’s options. Paths 0 and Corners 0 as an option of stitch plan, off by default, as for the smoothing pass. M13.51 built it.
  2. The smoothing pass. Off by default everywhere, pictures stitch plan traces included, and built all the same as a setting (M13.52).
  3. The pear’s teeth. Thread shows them, so no default removes them. A person who wants a clean edge between 2 bands of colour sets auto_smooth_mm to 0.8 mm.
  4. Traced letters that flip. Not these steps’: a later step looks at the columns’ rule for a stem that leans by a pixel.

What M13.52 built

The pass is the engine’s auto::smooth module, run on the automatic areas after their clips and before the background and the layers. Its cases are in crates/stitchcraft-engine/tests/auto_smooth.rs and in the module’s own tests. 2 things differ from the rule above:

  • Corners next to each other. The 4 corners of a rectangle are 4 points next to each other in its outline. A run of turning points goes on only while its points lie within 4 times the setting of each other along the line: then a rectangle keeps its 4 corners, and a thin strip’s end is one turn.
  • An edge that keeps its line. An edge whose smoothed line has the same points as drawn, a straight side for one, keeps the segments it was drawn with.

The build’s numbers

The step’s probe planned every picture of the set off, at 0.2 mm and at 0.4 mm, with the branch at 076162e9 built at opt-level 2. The fabric measures of the 4 local photos were not run, since each takes hours. Points are those of SC-I0921, which counts an edge 2 areas share once for each area. The other columns are the change from the setting off.

What gets worse at 0.2 mm:

  • Stitches astray rise on every dog, from 1 to 4 to between 4 and 9. At 0.4 mm they reach 10 to 25. A shape whose part changes rule sews its stitches a little off the drawn outline.
  • The area at 3 fills or more grows on 3 of the 4 dogs, by 6 to 33 %, and the thickest spot by up to 0.15 fills, still under the cap’s warning.
  • The lettered patch loses 133 of its thin traced lines. Each was a sliver just over 1 mm², and smoothed within a tenth of its area it falls under the speck bound, 0.96 to 1.0 mm², and merges into its neighbour. So the patch sews 260 shapes where it sewed 389, with 73 fewer jump threads, but its bare fabric grows from 1.2 to 1.9 % and its stitches astray from 90 to 128.
  • More columns on the photos. The flower photo sews 27 parts as satin columns where it sewed 13, and their stitches fan along edges that are still ragged at a thread’s scale.

The likeness of the dogs moves by less than 0.01 either way, and the pear and the badge sew as before but for shorter stitches. The area bound keeps every ring within a tenth of its area, and every ring stays. The probe’s pictures, off, at 0.2 mm and at 0.4 mm, are in the step’s notes.

Against the prototype, the build cuts short stitches as much. The dogs sew 3 % fewer and the pear 6 %, as the prototype’s did, the patch 13 % where it cut 12 %, and the photos 19 to 23 % where it cut 18 to 24 %. Its points differ for 2 reasons. The notice counts a shared edge for each of its areas, where the prototype counted it once. A straight side is drawn with the segments it had, where the prototype drew every edge anew. So the dogs need 4 % fewer points at 0.2 mm, where the prototype’s needed 2 % more. The pear and the patch need 9 and 11 % more, since a smoothed curve takes more points within 0.02 mm than the curves the tracer fitted.

Subject cut-out

A photo’s background traces into shapes of its own, such as turf, a fence or tyres behind a dog. The background rule leaves out one shape, the lowest, when it lies behind everything (Neighbours). So --subject cuts a picture’s subject out before VectorCraft traces it, as spike M13.13 decided (ADR-0019) and M13.14 built (REQ-CLI-014 to REQ-CLI-019). It is off by default, and a picture given without it is traced as above.

The steps. With --subject, stitch changes the picture before the 4 steps VectorCraft takes, in stitchcraft-cutout:

  1. It decodes the picture as its file stores the pixels, with the image crate, as VectorCraft decodes it: a PNG or JPEG file by what its bytes say, at most 16,384 px on a side and 50 megapixels in all.
  2. It finds the subject’s box. --subject-box X0,Y0,X1,Y1 gives it in the picture’s pixels. Otherwise U²-Net-p maps how likely each pixel is to belong to the main subject, at 320 × 320 px, the picture squeezed to that square by Lanczos’s filter and normalized as rembg normalizes it. The map is stretched to run from 0 to 1, and its values of a half or more fall into pieces of pixels that touch, corners included. The box is the one round the piece with the most of the map in it, scaled back to the picture.
  3. MobileSAM cuts the subject out of the box, with input and output prepared as SAM’s own code prepares them. The encoder takes the picture once, the longer side scaled to 1,024 px, each side rounded to a whole pixel, normalized and padded with zeros to a square. Its decoder takes the points of --subject-point, labelled 1, and of --not-subject, labelled 0, then the box’s 2 corners, labelled 2 and 3, each scaled as the picture’s side along it is. It gives a map of 256 × 256 logits across the square, which is scaled to the square, cut to the picture’s part of it and scaled to the picture, each time bilinearly between the pixels’ middles. The logistic function of each value, times 255, is the pixel’s alpha, or the picture’s own where that is lower.
  4. The picture with that alpha, cropped to the box round the pixels of an alpha of 128 or more, is written as a PNG file. file.place places it where it would place the picture, so --size and the hoop fit the subject.

Image Trace leaves out the pixels whose alpha is under 128, and gives a pixel on the cut edge the colour beside it (VectorCraft’s crates/trace at 3f8d7a0). The trace then contains the subject alone, with no background for the background rule to find. If MobileSAM keeps none of the pixels, or if U²-Net-p’s map has no piece above a half and no box is given, SC-E1202 says so and suggests --subject-box. A picture the cut-out can’t read is SC-E1203. stitch then writes the report alone, as when VectorCraft can’t trace. Every loop over the picture charges the budget, which runs out with SC-E0004. A cut-out made says the box it came from, how much of the picture it kept and its size (SC-I1206), and the report keeps it all.

Correcting a cut-out. A cut-out is wrong when the box contains more than the subject and MobileSAM keeps it, or when MobileSAM keeps less than the subject. --not-subject X,Y marks a point of what isn’t the subject, and --subject-point X,Y a point of what is, each as often as needed and in the picture’s pixels. The decoder takes the points with the box, U²-Net-p’s when the user gives none, since a point without a box can lose much of the subject. A box without area or one that extends past the picture, a point outside it, and any of these options or --cutout without --subject are usage errors, and so is --subject for a design that isn’t a picture. --cutout FILE writes the cut-out beside the machine file, with the other files asked for, all or none, so that the user can see what was kept.

In VectorCraft. A filter can’t read or replace a placed image, and a plug-in couldn’t run the models within its budget (ADR-0019, decision 2). stitch cutout PICTURE -o FILE writes the cut-out alone, with the same marks, and says what it kept as stitch plan does. The user places it where they would place the photo, and traces it with Image Trace as the tutorial does. A picture whose background another program made transparent traces as its subject too, without --subject. The owner’s traced dogs were made so (M13.15, docs/fixtures/README.md › The traced dogs).

The models are 3 ONNX files, 49.0 MB together, which tract 0.23.10 runs in stitchcraft-cutout, a library of the apps’ layer that only stitch depends on:

FileModelIts weights’ licenceSizeMade
u2netp.onnxU²-Net-p, 1.1 million parametersApache-2.04.6 MBrembg’s ONNX conversion of its author’s checkpoint
mobilesam-encoder.onnxMobileSAM’s encoder, 5.7 million parametersApache-2.028.0 MBexported from its authors’ checkpoint
mobilesam-decoder.onnxMobileSAM’s decoder, 4.1 million parametersApache-2.016.5 MBexported from its authors’ checkpoint

The repository pins each file’s address, size and SHA-256 in apps/stitchcraft-cutout/models/models.toml, and none of the files is committed. StitchCraft’s release cutout-models-1 publishes them, with their licences, a notice and the record of how they were made. GitHub Actions builds that release from the authors’ own files, in the workflow models.yml. Its build job exports MobileSAM’s 2 files with the script beside the pins, export.py, from the checkpoint in MobileSAM’s repository at a pinned commit, with MobileSAM’s own code and PyTorch’s exporter, in the packages requirements.txt pins by hash. It exports twice, and the 2 exports must agree. It fetches U²-Net-p’s file from rembg’s release, and stops unless each file has its pinned size and SHA-256. The encoder’s sum is the one exception, since its export has one of 2 sums by the machine: ADR-0019’s decision 5 says what the build and the publish do then. The owner publishes the release by starting the workflow by hand on main and typing the release’s tag, and no other run publishes. A release published is never replaced, so a new set of models takes a new tag. (Amended 2026-11-09, M13.14.) U²-Net-p’s author publishes its checkpoint on Google Drive and Baidu alone, so its file is rembg’s conversion of it, as rembg’s own release publishes it, the file M13.13 measured: rembg is under the MIT licence, and the weights under the Apache-2.0 licence, as the author wrote in issue 208. (Amended 2026-11-08, M13.14.)

stitch fetches the files with curl on first use into the folder STITCHCRAFT_MODELS names, or else stitchcraft/models in the user’s cache folder, which cargo xtask corpus uses for the fonts too. It reads each whole and checks its size and SHA-256 before tract reads the same bytes. A file missing or different is fetched again into a temporary file beside it and renamed into place only once it matches. When a file can’t be fetched or still differs, SC-E1201 names each file’s address, size and SHA-256 and the folder it belongs in, and stitch writes the report alone. tract would seed a random operator from the computer’s own randomness, so a model that has one is refused before it runs (SC-E1205). A model tract can’t run is SC-E1204. NOTICE names the models and their licences.

Built in, or left out. The cut-out is stitch’s cutout feature, on by default, which brings tract and the image crate in. A build without it has the same options, and --subject and stitch cutout stop there with SC-E1207, which says the build leaves the cut-out out. The WASI build that makes the golden files (cargo xtask wasm --goldens) is one. tract’s own CI only checks that tract-onnx builds for wasm32-wasip1, and with getrandom’s JavaScript backend chosen (ci/cross.sh). It runs tract-core’s and tract-linalg’s tests there, and no model. The golden files never cut a subject out, and without the feature that build doesn’t depend on tract building for WASI or on getrandom there. In the release build for Linux x86_64, stitch is 33.3 MB with the cut-out and 6.8 MB without it, or 28.5 MB and 6.0 MB once stripped (2026-11-15). The owner accepted the larger program.

Replays. The models run in stitch alone, and their results may differ in their last bits from one processor to another. A bug-report bundle of a picture already contains the document VectorCraft traced. With --subject it also records the models’ SHA-256 sums, the box, where it came from, the points and the crop, and leaves the picture out. Its replay says the picture was cut out and runs without the models and without VectorCraft.

Measured. The cut-out was measured on the owner’s 2 photos of her dog, against masks checked by eye (ADR-0019). With tract in stitchcraft-cutout, the cut-out from U²-Net-p’s box kept the dog at an intersection over union (IoU) of 0.985 on turf and 0.987 running. It kept 0.5 % and 0.4 % of the dog’s area wrongly and lost 1.0 % and 0.8 % of it. A point on the tyre behind the running dog that isn’t the subject gave 0.988. U²-Net-p alone kept that tyre, 19.8 % of the dog’s area. M13.13 measured 0.985 and 0.988 with a third-party export of MobileSAM, whose encoder holds other weights than its authors’ checkpoint. On the 2.1 GHz Xeon of the technical design, with one thread, onnxruntime took 0.5 s for U²-Net-p, 1.6 s for MobileSAM’s encoder and 0.1 s for its decoder. tract took 1.0 to 1.2 s, 2.5 to 2.9 s and 0.15 to 0.23 s. In Rust, with tract, the whole cut-out of a photo took 5.1 to 5.4 s and 343 MB at most.

Cases (M13.14). The 2 photos are committed as prepared, 1,200 px wide, with a mask of each checked by hand, and recorded in conformance/fixtures/README.md. The owner took them of her own dog and gave them under the MIT licence. The models’ outputs for them are fixtures too, U²-Net-p’s map and MobileSAM’s map of each prompt, saved from what tract gave. The cases of the box, the marks, the alpha, the crop and the file run on those fixtures without the models, and so does REQ-CLI-019’s case of the bounds. Stand-ins for the models, tiny ONNX files with their inputs and outputs, run through the same tract runner in the cases of stitch plan --subject and stitch cutout. One picture drawn in code, a red disc on grey stripes that touches a blue square, has exact masks. With the models, 2 points on the disc must keep the disc alone, and a box round the square the square alone (REQ-CLI-015). REQ-CLI-016’s case runs stitch plan --subject end to end where no model can be had, and expects SC-E1201 and the report alone. A job of its own, models, runs the cases that need the models, which every other job leaves out, and conformance says where it takes them from. It compares tract’s maps with the fixtures, and each cut-out with its mask, which it must match at an IoU of 0.97 or more, with at most 2 % of the subject’s area kept wrongly and 3 % lost.

Testing

  • Each rule gets its requirements and failing cases before its code (ADR-0008).

  • The test pictures are drawn in code, as the test sheets are (stitchcraft_engine::testsheets::pictures), and no logo or picture of anyone else’s is used. From M13.15 the owner’s dog, cut out of her 2 photos and traced, is a test picture too (sewing order), and from M13.14 the subject cut-out’s cases use the photos (above).

  • Each test picture’s automatic plan is compared with its plan in paint order: colour changes, jumps, trims, stitches, and how much of each shape and of each shared edge the stitches cover (stitchcraft_testkit::metrics). The cases measure the pictures where stitchcraft_testkit::pictures places them.

  • Level B of the compatibility gate has a case, trace, in which VectorCraft’s command line traces a picture drawn in the threads stitch palette writes, and StitchCraft sews the document it saved, on every track. In its case picture, stitch plan traces the picture itself.

  • stitch plan’s own tests run it with a stand-in for VectorCraft’s command line, the test program itself, which saves a document VectorCraft 0.18.0 traced (apps/stitchcraft-cli/tests/picture.rs).

  • TS-13 sews the test pictures at MC-8 (machine testing), each twice. A sheet’s top half is digitized automatically, and its bottom half is sewn as drawn, in paint order. The bottom half sews first and stops, so that nothing digitized moves into it, and each half’s thread changes, jumps and time can be read. TS-13A sews the pictures of neighbours and sewing order, TS-13B block letters and lines traced as areas, TS-13C a face drawn with lines and shapes in parts, TS-13D the emblem and TS-13E the button, each traced abutting and stacked (test sheets). TS-13D sews the emblem 42 mm wide, at 0.7 times its size. There the white round each star is narrower than a satin column, and sews as a column round the star, while the navy is one fill under the disc and the bars. The PE800 cuts no jump, and the sew-out reports where a shape sews down the thread of a jump before it.

  • tests/auto_fabric.rs measures what each test picture and the traced pear of the user pages leave bare and lay astray, digitized (Sewing order). The pear is read from docs/fixtures/traced-pear.vectorcraft, a photo that VectorCraft traced, with the ragged edges a trace has.

  • tests/auto_sheets.rs answers the TS-13 checks that the sheets’ own stitches can, moved to where each sheet draws its shapes (stitchcraft_testkit::pictures::sheet). Where the bar runs on beside the square, each ends its rows at its own edge. The specks sew as the disc, and no shape sews over one drawn over it. A line traced as an area runs to within its width of its ends, and its branch from the line it meets. The smile’s column ends where its round caps end, the columns’ rungs lie within 10° of square to their strokes, and the face’s columns cover the edges of the areas under them. The sew-out shows what only the machine can: whether fabric or a ridge shows once the thread pulls in, and how small a word still reads.

  • The same cases count the jump threads each half leaves on the PE800, snipped at each thread change and stop, and how many a later stitch of their colour sews down (stitchcraft_testkit::stitches). Digitized, the letters sew as columns with a jump between each 2. A thread that sews once jumps between its shapes where it changed before M13.22, and the PE800 cuts at a thread change and not at a jump, so the digitized halves of TS-13A, TS-13C and TS-13D leave 3, 1 and 2 jump threads more. With an outline after each fill (M13.28) TS-13D’s half left 2 more again, while it held the buttons that TS-13E has now (M13.31):

    SheetAs drawn: jump threadssewn downDigitized: jump threadssewn down
    TS-13A80189
    TS-13B604319
    TS-13C94135
    TS-13D10113
    TS-13E0041

  • Ink/Stitch sews the TS-13 sheets too, every night (conformance, REQ-AUTO-021). Ink/Stitch can’t choose a picture’s stitches, but it can sew what StitchCraft chose: the elements StitchCraft sews a sheet with, written as an SVG file with their parameters. Its machine files then agree with StitchCraft’s by every measure of the differential. Their thread footprints overlap StitchCraft’s by 0.97 or more. StitchCraft sews 6 to 10 % fewer stitches, as it leaves out stitches shorter than the machine’s shortest (DEV-FIN-001): fitted to the machine as StitchCraft fits its own plans, Ink/Stitch’s files come within about 1 % of StitchCraft’s stitches. Ink/Stitch’s extra trims and jumps are jumps that go nowhere at thread changes, which a StitchCraft PES file has no record of (DEV-FMT-001), and without them the trims and jumps agree within 1.

  • A wrong choice sews alike in both programs. So, every night too, the lines of each shape sewn wholly as running stitches are compared with the lines Ink/Stitch’s Fill to Stroke draws for it (REQ-AUTO-022). The shapes are TS-13B’s word 4 mm tall and its windows’ lines, and the letters T, H and E from 3 to 10 mm tall in lines from 0.3 to 0.9 mm wide. Each branch Ink/Stitch finds has one of StitchCraft’s, and away from their ends the lines lie within 0.15 of a width of each other.

Codes 0900 to 0999 are automatic digitizing’s, and 1200 to 1299 the subject cut-out’s (diagnostics). Text in pictures uses lettering’s codes, of 1100 to 1199, and the cap’s SC-I1119 is among them too.
